# Rulings owed

Questions a fix ran into and could not answer alone, for which no other paper
had room. Each one is filed first with `fli file --sev decision --blocks
<row>`, which writes a row in `ISSUES.md` § Needs a decision and sets the fix's
row aside in `fli next`. Its options then go here, in a bullet whose lead names
that row's id. `fli decide` answers the bullet under the row's own id and closes
the row, so the held row comes back naming its ruling.

A question an existing paper argues belongs in that paper's `## Open questions`,
not here.

## Open questions

- **FJS-D1090 — Does Button's `size` prop scale its padding, or only its font?** `size` sets an inline `font-size` (`xs` 0.75rem, `sm` 0.8125rem, `md` none, `lg` 1rem), but `.btn` pads with `var(--control-padding-block, var(--space-sm)) var(--space-xl)` and `line-height: 1.25rem`. `--space-*` is `calc(<n>rem * var(--density))` and the line height is rem, so a smaller button gets smaller text in the same 8px/14px box and the same 20px line. `Button.mesa`'s header comment says the padding scales because `.btn` is em-sized; only `.btn.square` (`0.45em`) is. The rem padding is deliberate: `tokens.css` § Control height shares `--control-padding-block` with `.field` so an input and the button beside it are the same height.
  - **A** — em padding for the non-`md` sizes only: `.btn` reads `--btn-pad-block` / `--btn-pad-inline` with the current rem tokens as the fallback, and `Button` sets them in em for `xs`, `sm` and `lg`. `md` is unchanged, so it still matches `.field` and still follows `.dense`/`.roomy`. Cost: two knobs, and `xs`/`sm`/`lg` stop following density.
  - **B** — `size` also sets `--density` on the button (`xs` ≈ 0.7, `sm` ≈ 0.85, `lg` ≈ 1.15), so the existing axis does the work and no token is added. Cost: `--density` inherits, so it also shrinks anything inside the button (an icon, a nested badge), and the sizes become magic multipliers.
  - **C** — keep the rem box and fix only the comment: a size is a font size and the box is the control height. Cost: `xs` is not a compact button, and the workbench Layout button needs `.dense` or a `ghost` treatment to look small.
  - **Recommend A** — it keeps the one thing the shared padding token exists for (an `md` button level with an input) and makes the other three sizes mean what the name says. B reuses an axis that was designed for a region, not for one element, and leaks into the button's children. Whichever is ruled, the `Button.mesa` comment is wrong today and changes with it.

- ~~**FJS-D1089 — How does a row name a self-hosted counterparty — its address and its token, sometimes on a private network — without a stored credential going where a stranger says?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-16 (`FJS-D1089`): A — two halves, neither a new option. *The token is the row's own*: `address_from: 'request'` admits `bearer`, `api_key` and `signed` when `auth.per_caller: true`, and such a send states `grant`, refused by name (`invalid_request`, nothing sent) without it — the credential and the address are read off the same row, so the writer of the address holds the token, and `withCache` keyed on the grant keeps two rows in one tenant from sharing one cached token (`FJS-2232` per row). The register refusal stays for a process credential. *A private address is never a row's*: the public grade stays before every attempt, so a self-hosted instance behind a public name (the common case) is reached and one on a LAN or a tailnet is the operator's to declare as a fixed-address target per install, or waits for Outpost vantage, as `FJS-D1018` ruled. `allowPrivate` stays a test receiver's switch. Cost: a per-row LAN instance has no spelling until vantage; a `getFor` that ignores `grant` answers a tenant's token for every row's address, which conduit cannot see and the hazard names.** `FJS-2923`, found sizing a connector for a self-hosted vendor (Plausible CE is the same API at another address, with that install's own key). Three edges of one rule today: `address_from: 'request'` grades every address public before every attempt (`http.ts`), so an instance on 192.168.x or a tailnet is refused; the same target refuses `bearer`/`api_key`/`signed` at `put()` (`assertRequestAddressed`), and a self-hosted instance takes a token; `destinations.allowPrivate` is one boolean for the whole target, written for a test receiver on localhost. The workaround is the hole: one FIXED-address target `put()` per row, which takes `bearer` and is graded by nothing, so whoever writes the row picks where the token goes and which internal address the process dials. Two facts the options rest on: a fixed-address target is never graded, on purpose — a `unix` socket, a local LLM, a LAN PBX are the operator's declaration, written in config; and `per_caller: true` + `who.grant` (`FJS-D848`) already says *this credential belongs to whoever the send is for, and `grant` names which of their logins*. `FJS-D1018` ruled the private half for Basecamp monitors: no escape hatch, a private target belongs to an Outpost vantage.
  - **A** — two halves, neither a new option. *The token is the row's own*: `address_from: 'request'` admits `bearer`, `api_key` and `signed` when `auth.per_caller: true`, and such a send states `grant`, refused by name (`invalid_request`, nothing sent) without it — the credential and the address are read off the same row, so the writer of the address holds the token, and `withCache` keyed on the grant keeps two rows in one tenant from sharing one cached token (`FJS-2232` per row). The register refusal stays for a process credential. *A private address is never a row's*: the public grade stays before every attempt, so a self-hosted instance behind a public name (the common case) is reached and one on a LAN or a tailnet is the operator's to declare as a fixed-address target per install, or waits for Outpost vantage, as `FJS-D1018` ruled. `allowPrivate` stays a test receiver's switch. Cost: a per-row LAN instance has no spelling until vantage; a `getFor` that ignores `grant` answers a tenant's token for every row's address, which conduit cannot see and the hazard names.
  - **B** — A's credential half, plus an operator allow-list of private origins on the target, `destinations.origins: ['http://plausible.lan:8000']`: a row address on a listed origin is admitted ungraded, every other private address refused. Cost: the address is written twice (row and config) and a new client instance is a redeploy; it is `FJS-D1018`'s rejected **B**/**C** with per-origin granularity, and an allowed name that re-resolves somewhere worse is not caught.
  - **C** — grade a fixed address too: `put()` runs `assertPublicUrl` on every `http` address unless `destinations.allowPrivate`, so a target registered per row is caught. Cost: every localhost dev target, `unix` socket and LAN PBX needs the flag, the operator's declaration is graded like a stranger's, and conduit cannot tell a `put()` at boot from env from one at runtime from a row, so the hole closes only by closing the legitimate case with it.
  - **D** — rule the status quo: a fixed-address target per row is the spelling, documented. Rejected by the row itself — it is the hole.
  - **Recommend A** — the credential half is derived from `FJS-D848`'s seam rather than restated (the grant is already *which login*, and a row is a login), and the address half is `FJS-D1018`'s own answer applied to conduit, so the two rulings say one thing: a row names where, public only, and the credential that goes there is the row's own. Six months out it is one sentence; B is two places for one address, C grades the operator. Adjudications: *ergonomics vs. strictness* — the register refusal stays where the cost is a token leaving to a stranger's address and lifts exactly where the row owns the token; *paved road vs. the workaround* — the per-row fixed target was the workaround, and A is the road it measured. Artefact: the suite's case where two rows in one tenant each land their own token at their own address, and a send with no grant sends nothing; the `getFor`-ignores-`grant` hazard is `none`, written as such.

- ~~**FJS-D1042 — How does a `sierra.config.js` say *the page's own origin* for `junction.url`, now that `FJS-D552` evaluates it in Node?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D1042`): A — the page decides: `virtual:sierra` emits `url: import.meta.env.VITE_API_URL || location.origin`, and `junction.url` in the config is the answer where there is no page (the terminal shell, a prerender) and what turns the client on. The four configs shrink to a plain URL. The price: a config `url` naming a remote API is ignored by the page unless the build sets `VITE_API_URL`.** `FJS-D552` hands `junction` to the browser as JSON, so `typeof location !== 'undefined' ? location.origin : '<port>'` always takes the fallback. Haven's browser called `:5505` cross-origin and rendered only *Failed to fetch*; basecamp, example and sierra/example carry the same line, and `import.meta.env?.VITE_API_URL` in the config is dead for the same reason, so `fli deploy`'s check that the build carries the API origin would refuse.
  - **A** — the page decides: `virtual:sierra` emits `url: import.meta.env.VITE_API_URL || location.origin`, and `junction.url` in the config is the answer where there is no page (the terminal shell, a prerender) and what turns the client on. The four configs shrink to a plain URL. The price: a config `url` naming a remote API is ignored by the page unless the build sets `VITE_API_URL`.
  - **B** — a path-only `url` (`'/'`) means the page's origin, resolved by the client against `location`. One key, one meaning; the terminal then needs an absolute URL from `--api` and refuses a relative one by name, so `bun run tui`, `fli dev:tui` and `verify-tui` all change.
  - **Recommend A** — every config in the tree already meant *the page's origin in a browser, this port elsewhere*, and `VITE_API_URL` is already the one override every surface's build reads; A moves that sentence into the one place that runs in the page. B moves the terminal's default out of the config for no browser gain.

- ~~**FJS-D915 — Does an OAuth sign-in owe the TOTP code, as a password and a magic link do since `FJS-D888`?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-11 (`FJS-D915`): B — OAuth is a first factor; the three branches end at `firstFactorProven`, a challenge redirects with `code_required`.** `FJS-2541`: `oauthResolve` returns `issueSession(...)` from all three of its sign-in branches (`packages/auth/auth.ts` ~1365, ~1434, ~1465) and never reads `liveTotp`, so an account with a confirmed `totp` credential is one provider click from a session. `login()` and `verifyMagicLink` both end at `firstFactorProven()`, which issues the challenge. Nothing rules either way, and the TOTP settings screen tells nobody that the code covers two of the three ways in. The callback can already carry a challenge: OAuth requires `cookieAuth`, and `POST /auth/login/challenge` already reads its ticket from the `login_challenge` cookie in that mode.
  - **A** — the provider counts as both factors: `oauthResolve` stays as it is, and auth's `CLAUDE.md` and the TOTP docs say the code guards a password and a mailed link, not a provider sign-in. Nothing to build. The price is a stolen Google account that walks past a factor its owner turned on.
  - **B** — OAuth is a first factor like the other two: the three branches end at `firstFactorProven(user, { provider })`. On a challenge the callback sets the `login_challenge` cookie and redirects through `oauthFailure` with a new `OAUTH_ERRORS` code (`code_required`), and the page that reads `session.oauthMessage` finishes it through `completeSignIn`, as a password sign-in does. One error code and no new route. A newly registered account has no factor, so `oauth.registered` is unchanged. Owes the artefact nothing has today: an auth row asserting an OAuth sign-in on a `totp` account answers a challenge and no session, and a `verify:oauth` step that finishes one through the page.
  - **C** — a per-provider option, `oauth.providers[name].secondFactor: 'ours' | 'theirs'`, where `theirs` trusts the provider's own MFA through the OIDC `amr` claim. It honors a provider that already asked for a second factor, at the cost of a new option. GitHub sends no `amr`, so for most providers C would behave exactly like B.
  - **Recommend B** — turning on TOTP is the person saying *my account needs the code*. `FJS-D888` held the mailbox to that, and an exception for the third first factor is the kind of surprise a security setting must not have. B reuses the cookie, the route and `completeSignIn`, which already exist. C can be added beside it later if a provider's own MFA ever needs to count. Strictness follows what a mistake destroys (§ IV), and here that is the account. C is a flag that widens the road's shoulder rather than changing the road.

- ~~**FJS-D903 — What owns "a field changed" in a `<Form>`?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D903`): A — `$context.form.changed(name)`: every control calls it on commit, `writeField` calls it, and it is the one place that sets `dirty`, retires `_server` and arms autosave; `handleChange` becomes a caller of it.** `FJS-2590`: `Form.mesa`'s `handleChange` keys off `e.target.name` and is the only path that retires a server error; `writeField` (every click-driven commit) sets `dirty` and retires nothing. Six controls (DatePicker, Slider, MultiSelect, Combobox, GeoField's longitude, FileUpload) emit no named input event, so a hand-written form never turns dirty and a stale server message stays under a corrected value. Combobox putting `name` on its search box (forms-complex-17) is the same question from the native-form side. UI kit audit 2026-10-10.
  - **A** — `$context.form.changed(name)`: every control calls it on commit, `writeField` calls it, and it is the one place that sets `dirty`, retires `_server` and arms autosave; `handleChange` becomes a caller of it.
  - **B** — every control dispatches a bubbling `change` event from an element carrying `name` on commit (a hidden input where the value is not text), and `handleChange` stays the owner.
  - **Recommend A** — B makes six controls fake a DOM event to reach a function in the same component tree, and a click-committed value has no element to be honest about; A is Invariant 4's shape (add to the owner) and a control outside a form calls nothing. Either way a control that forgets is silent, so the ruling carries its artefact: a kit drive spec that commits a value in every form control inside a hand-written `<Form>` and asserts `dirty` and a retired server error, walking the component list the way `test/attributes.mjs` does.

- ~~**FJS-D904 — Who owns where a kit overlay mounts, and which overlay answers Escape?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D904`): A — one helper in `utils.js`, `overlayHost()` = the topmost `dialog:modal`, else `body`, which every portal calls; plus a rule that an overlay answering Escape calls `preventDefault()`, graded by a drive.** `FJS-2599`, `FJS-2109`, `FJS-2601`: six surfaces portal to `<body>`, which a `showModal()` dialog makes inert, and only ConfirmPanel looks for `dialog[open]` (`FJS-1983`). Two of five Escape handlers consume the key and three let the same press close the enclosing dialog (overlay-10, forms-complex-18). UI kit audit 2026-10-10.
  - **A** — one helper in `utils.js`, `overlayHost()` = the topmost `dialog:modal`, else `body`, which every portal calls; plus a rule that an overlay answering Escape calls `preventDefault()`, graded by a drive.
  - **B** — app-level surfaces (toast stack, global alert, palette) go into the top layer with `popover="manual"`, and in-dialog surfaces (Popover, DropdownMenu, the dnd ghost) use A's helper.
  - **Recommend A** — one owner for one question; B is two answers, and the top layer's stacking order against a later `showModal()` is a second thing to learn. A toast inside a dialog's host is removed with the dialog, which is what closing it means. The owner already exists: ConfirmPanel's `host()` (`ConfirmPanel.mesa` L42) is promoted to `utils.js`, not written a second time. Artefact: a drive spec that opens every portaling overlay inside a Modal and asserts the hit-test reaches it, and presses Escape once per overlay asserting the dialog stays open.

- ~~**FJS-D905 — Who resets per-principal UI state when the session changes?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D905`): A — sierra emits a session-changed event (`session.onChange` or a DOM event on `document`) and `<Toaster>`, `<AlertProvider>` and `<CommandPalette>` subscribe and clear.** `FJS-2606`: `toastStore`, `alertStore` and `commandPaletteStore` are module singletons, sierra's `session.clear()` cannot import ui (siblings), and no app calls `toasts.clear()`, so one person's toast with a live Undo is on screen for the next person who signs in. UI kit audit 2026-10-10.
  - **A** — sierra emits a session-changed event (`session.onChange` or a DOM event on `document`) and `<Toaster>`, `<AlertProvider>` and `<CommandPalette>` subscribe and clear.
  - **B** — a `principal` prop on the providers; a change of value clears the store, and the app passes `session.user?.id`.
  - **Recommend A** — B is a prop every app must remember to pass, and forgetting it is silent, which is today's defect; A needs no caller and is the seam sierra already owes its own resource stores (`FJS-786`'s shape). It is an event on `document`, so ui imports nothing from sierra and Invariant 1 is untouched; its name is one new word and goes in `VOCABULARY.md`. Artefact: a drive that shows a toast, signs out and asserts the stack is empty.

- **FJS-D871 — Is a non-numeric count (`?$limit=abc`) refused by name rather than dropped?** The toolbelt spec pins *dropped* (`toolbelt/test/specs/directives.spec.js` L67-69); `FJS-D237`'s reasoning, that an unknown `$`-key is refused by name because silence is not what this codebase does elsewhere, says refuse. `IDEAS/spec-directives.md` refuses and marks it. Fable audit run 4.2.
  - **A** — refuse, 400 by name (`$limit must be digits`), the same owner and shape as the unknown-key refusal; the spec vector changes.
  - **B** — drop to the default, as pinned, and the spec says so; a typo in a count is a default page, not an error.
  - **C** — drop on a URL (a bookmark should not 400) and refuse on a typed request body or socket bag, the split `FJS-D237` already makes for unknown keys.
  - **Recommend A** — C is the one that matches D237's letter, but D237's split exists because a URL cannot carry a refusal to a browser tab, and a count is typed by a program, not a person; one answer predicts more than two.

- **FJS-D870 — Does a legacy port get a data-move primitive, or is a mover an app script by `FJS-D518`'s reasoning?** `litestone import` converts the schema and nothing moves the rows, so remnant (`db/corpus/`), ela (Phase 3) and maidtech (`db/move/`, `bun run db:move`) each hand-rolled the same mover: `createMany` as the system principal, 1,000 a batch, epoch-ms → ISO, blank → null, a refused batch retried row by row, `litestone validate` after. `FJS-2037`, `2038`, `2039`, `2043` are what the ela move found on the way. Fable audit run 5.1.
  - **A** — an app script, as D518 ruled a data migration: the framework documents the shape once (a `docs/` page with the six steps and their reasons) and ships nothing.
  - **B** — a `fli db:move` that takes a source (SQLite file, CSV directory, or a `.lite` plus rows) and a field map, and runs the six steps as the system principal with the validate pass built in.
  - **C** — a litestone `seeder.move(source, map)` primitive the app script calls, so the steps are owned once and the per-port map stays the app's.
  - **Recommend C** — three ports proved the steps are the same and the map is not; A restates the steps in every port, B owns the map the framework cannot know.

- ~~**FJS-D865 — Three concepts to bless: Episode, Standing, Trait?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-18 (`FJS-D865`): A — all three get rows; Ceiling folds into Gate ladder's note.** Each is used as THE noun by code and by a 2026-10-09 ruling and has no row (`litestone/src/core/audit-log.js` L51, `VOCABULARY.md` L196 defines Principal with *standing*, `catalog.snapshot.md` L141). Fable audit run 4.4.
  - **A** — add all three: Episode under Session, Standing under Gate ladder, Trait under Model; fold Ceiling into Gate ladder's note.
  - **B** — add Episode and Trait; make *Standing* an alias of *level* and rewrite the Principal row to say level.
  - **C** — none; keep them package-local vocabularies (litestone, auth, mcp).
  - **Recommend A** — Standing is the word already carrying the Principal definition and the mcp charter, so aliasing it to *level* moves the drift rather than ending it; C fails § V-2, since a fourth noun (*support mode*, *impersonation*) is exactly what no row invites.

- ~~**FJS-D866 — What is junction's `Store` called, given ARCHITECT § 2 refuses *store* for a Resource and `client.resource()` returns one?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-18 (`FJS-D866`): D, word `Tether` — `TetherBinding`, `{tether, stale, load}`, `useTether`; *live store* becomes *tether*.** `junction/src/client/index.ts` L2663; sierra prose says *live store*. Fable audit run 4.4.
  - **A** — rename the class to `List` (`StoreBinding`→`ListBinding`, `{store, stale, load}`→`{list, stale, load}`, `useStore`→`useList`); *live store* becomes *live list*.
  - **B** — bless `Store` as the API-realm identifier for the ordered live list a Resource holds, narrowing § 2's refusal to prose about a Resource.
  - **C** — leave it; `FJS-D625` already lets the folder word stand.
  - **D** — a coined single word that names what the class does (synced, bounded, the active set), not its shape. Candidates, none of them in VOCABULARY or any `src/`: `Tether` (`useTether(employees.tether)`, `TetherBinding`), `Reel`, `Pane`, `Docket`. Rejected while searching: `Collection` (junction's REST collection routes, site-kit collections), `LiveQuery` (a query is the filter here, Invariant 10), `Feed` (`FJS-D1034` weighs it for a polled vendor feed), `Mirror` and `Replica` (both taken).
  - **Recommend D, word open** — on 2026-10-17 the owner turned down both `Store` and `List` (*more descriptive of logic/power than List*), saw the four call sites spelled with `Tether`, and deferred choosing the word. `FJS-D867`'s `Entry` lands first either way.

- ~~**FJS-D867 — `Node` is three concepts: junction's cached row, orion's flow node, mesa's IR node. Which keeps the word?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D867`): A — orion and mesa keep it (a flow node and an IR node are the field's words); junction renames `Node`/`NodeRegistry`/`NodeView`/`client.nodes` to `Entry`/`EntryRegistry`/`EntryView`/`client.entries`.** `junction/src/client/nodes.ts` L38, `orion/CLAUDE.md` L177, `mesa/src/ir.js`. Fable audit run 4.4.
  - **A** — orion and mesa keep it (a flow node and an IR node are the field's words); junction renames `Node`/`NodeRegistry`/`NodeView`/`client.nodes` to `Entry`/`EntryRegistry`/`EntryView`/`client.entries`.
  - **B** — qualify at every use (*client node*, *flow node*, *IR node*) and add no row.
  - **C** — junction keeps and blesses `Node` (Relay's word for a normalized cache entry); orion and mesa qualify.
  - **Recommend A** — six months out `client.nodes` is the one a Resource developer meets and the one with no field-standard sense; B is right only where the senses never meet in one file, and they meet in basecamp. `Cell` was refused as the target: `toolbelt/src/cells/cells.d.ts` L3 already spells `CellKind`.

- ~~**FJS-D868 — `FJS-D678` refused the interface sense of *surface*; junction's `Surface`, `JobsSurface`, `NotificationsSurface` and `describeSurface` still carry it. What is the rename target?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D868`): A — `AppDescription` / `JobsDescription` / `NotificationsDescription` / `describeApp`, matching the existing `ServiceDescription`.** `junction/src/core/app-model.ts` L53, L61, L158, L190, L225; `cli/core/app-entry.js` L207. Fable audit run 4.4.
  - **A** — `AppDescription` / `JobsDescription` / `NotificationsDescription` / `describeApp`, matching the existing `ServiceDescription`.
  - **B** — the `Projection` family, per D678's own sentence that mcp's tool set is a Projection.
  - **C** — leave the identifiers; the ruling governs prose.
  - **Recommend A** — B makes *Projection* (a derived shape of the schema) cover a runtime description of services and routes, a second sense of a blessed word; C is the undocumented divergence § IV's last row forbids.

- ~~**FJS-D869 — One word for the owned cross-package handoff that Invariant 4 and `fli check seam-owner` govern: *seam*, *handoff* or *bridge*?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-18 (`FJS-D869`): A — bless `Seam`; *handoff* stays the plain phrase, *Bridge index* the list title.** Root `CLAUDE.md` § Bridge index; `litestone/CLAUDE.md` says seam five times; `cli/CLAUDE.md` L1117 names the check. Fable audit run 4.4.
  - **A** — bless `Seam`; the check already grades by that name; *handoff* stays the plain phrase and *Bridge index* stays the list's title.
  - **B** — bless `Handoff`; rename the check `handoff-owner`.
  - **C** — no noun; Boundary, qualified, covers it.
  - **Recommend A** — the grader is the artefact (§ V-9) and it says *seam*; C fails because a Boundary is where two realms meet and a seam is one owned translation across it, and Invariant 4 counts seams.

- **FJS-D864 — What severity does `doc-examples-run` carry while 19 packages have no doc-example runner?** The rule (checks.js, repo scope, Invariant 16) grades WIRING: a package whose docs fence an unmarked runnable block must reach a `test/doc-examples.test.*` from its test script. It landed as `warn` on 2026-10-10 (Fable audit run 2.4) and fires 19 times; litestone's runner is the enforcer row. Refines `FJS-D860`: the extraction that moved to `fli check` is the wiring check, the runner stays per package.
  - **A** — `warn` now; `FJS-2331` is the ratchet, and the rule flips to `error` the day the count is 0.
  - **B** — `error` plus 19 named `structure` allowances in `scripts/ci-allowances.json`, each *runner not yet written*, removed as runners land; a stale one is reported by the engine.
  - **C** — `error` plus a per-rule baseline for the repo scope; `check-baseline.json` is app-scoped today, so this adds a mechanism.
  - **Recommend A** — B's 19 allowances each restate the row; C adds a mechanism to answer a count a row already holds. A's cost is the `FJS-D859` argument (a warn is read by whoever already knows), bounded because `fli next` ranks `FJS-2331` and the structure phase prints the 19 lines on every run.

- **FJS-D863 — Does the register re-file the rows run 3.2 names at the severity the legend gives them?** The S1 table is empty while the legend (Conventions, *S1 blocker — data loss, a security hole, or cannot ship*) describes rows sitting in S2–S4, so `fli next` ranks two confirmed tooling defects (`FJS-2366`, `FJS-2368`) above every access hole. Fable audit run 3.2, 2026-10-10. Each is one row and one decision; the list is the audit's proposal, not consent.
  - **Up to S1** — `FJS-2232`, `FJS-1725`, `FJS-1503`, `FJS-1743`, `FJS-1799`, `FJS-1660` (each a security hole by the legend; each waits on a ruling, which argues for filing them where the owner sees them).
  - **Up to S2** — `FJS-2334`, `FJS-2335` (a host page reaches a service call with the user's token) · `FJS-1656` (`@guarded` plaintext through a view) · `FJS-1438` (write escalation by foreign-key move, measured twice) · `FJS-1838` (TOTP oracle, no ceiling) · `FJS-1513`, `FJS-1490` (silently wrong on day one, every app pays) · `FJS-2140`, `FJS-1271` (stored-shape corruption that compounds).
  - **Up to S3** — `FJS-2228` (`'false'` grades 7) · `FJS-1847` (its own test is named oauth-link-hijack) · `FJS-1592` (address included, installation-wide) · `FJS-1297` (the audit trail committed on first `git add`) · `FJS-1853` (verify-request mail bomb from a public route).
  - **Down to S3** — `FJS-2084` (its own text: no user is broken today) · `FJS-2366`, `FJS-2368` (a missed drive, not a wrong answer to a user) · `FJS-1927` (reasoned from the code, not probed) until probed.
  - **Re-probe before ranking** — `FJS-053`, `FJS-266`, `FJS-268`, `FJS-274`, `FJS-373`, `FJS-503`, `FJS-555`, `FJS-610`, and `FJS-009`'s own body, all last verified in August.
  - **A** — re-file the ups and downs as listed, one `fli amend` each.
  - **B** — add a `fli register:check` rule that refuses a row whose title names a hole (a token list) below S2, and let the rule drive the re-file.
  - **C** — leave the severities; change `fli next` to weigh a blocked row's severity and keep blocked rows ranked (`FJS-2409`, `FJS-2410`), which is what put the tooling rows on top.
  - **Recommend A then C** — the legend is the rule and the rows break it; C is owed anyway and does not fix a mis-filed row.

- **FJS-D861 — Does `FJS-D291` still hold once the in-app row is weighed?** D291 lets an activated flow notify any recipient of any notification the app opts in through `orion({ notifications })`, weighing mail: the flow sends nothing the app did not write. The `notify` node also writes an in-app row (`orion/src/plugin.ts` L169-179 → `notifications/drivers/inapp.ts` L59, as the system) whose `type` picks the browser's renderer and whose `data` is the flow's payload passed through the definition untyped (`notifications/types.ts` L28, `data?: Record<string, unknown>`) — so a flow activated at 5 puts a `PaymentReceived` with its own amount into any user's bell. Fable audit run 1.5. The owner of what a payload may be is `defineNotification`, not orion (D290's shape: the handler grades its own input).
  - **A** — D291 holds: activation at 5 is the line, and a 5 that lies in a bell is a 5 that lies in mail.
  - **B** — a definition declares its payload shape and `define()` refuses a payload off it; orion compiles a `notify` node's payload against the same declaration. Owned by notifications, derived by orion.
  - **C** — a flow-authored row is marked (`source: flow`, rendered as such), so a bell entry says who wrote it. A new column and a new noun.
  - **Recommend B** — one owner, derived not restated, and it is the sibling of D290 with the grading where that ruling put it.

- ~~**FJS-D615 — Is an empty or blank scope list an unscoped key, and does an unscoped key reach the credential services at all?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-09 (`FJS-D615`): C — A, and the credential services refuse every API key, scoped or not: managing credentials takes a session.** The Fable audit 2026-10-05 (run 1.1). `api-keys.create` stores `scopes` as given, so `['']` mints a key that `caller()` reads as unscoped, with its owner's whole standing (`FJS-1850`). `FJS-1446` refused a SCOPED key at `account`, `sessions` and `api-keys` and left an unscoped one as its owner, so a leaked unscoped key can mint a child key before it is revoked and outlive the revocation. `audit-services-api-keys.test.ts` asserts the refusal in both cases.
  - **A** — a blank or non-string entry is refused at `create`. An unscoped key stays its owner at the credential services.
  - **B** — a blank entry means unscoped, as today, and is documented.
  - **C** — A, and the credential services refuse every API key, scoped or not: managing credentials takes a session.
  - **Recommend C** — the normalization fails open, and a key that can mint keys cannot be contained by revoking it.

- ~~**FJS-D616 — Should `GET /auth/email/verify` change anything?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-09 (`FJS-D616`): A — the link stays a GET that lands on a page, and the page POSTs the token.** The Fable audit 2026-10-05 (run 1.1). The GET consumes the single-use token and answers the whole `SessionContext`, so a mail scanner's prefetch burns the link (`FJS-1839`).
  - **A** — the link stays a GET that lands on a page, and the page POSTs the token.
  - **B** — the GET keeps consuming the token, for mail ergonomics.
  - **Recommend A** — scanners already consume the token, and a GET that writes breaks HTTP's own contract.

- ~~**FJS-D617 — Who sets `http.trustProxy` when `fli deploy` writes the nginx that appends `X-Forwarded-For`?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-09 (`FJS-D617`): A — the deploy that writes the proxy also writes `http.trustProxy` for it.** The Fable audit 2026-10-05 (run 1.1). `fli new` writes no `trustProxy` and the deploy's nginx appends the header, so every caller is keyed on the proxy's address and the login limiter is one bucket for the site (`FJS-1841`, `FJS-D449`).
  - **A** — the deploy that writes the proxy also writes `http.trustProxy` for it.
  - **B** — the operator sets it, and the deploy says so.
  - **Recommend A** — the one that adds the hop knows it is there. B leaves the scaffold broken by default.

- ~~**FJS-D619 — Is a workspace roster meant for viewers, and if so what of a colleague's `User` row does it carry?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-09 (`FJS-D619`): A — yes: the roster projects `User` to name, email and avatar.** The Fable audit 2026-10-05 (run 2.2). `workspaces.members` hands a viewer each colleague's whole `User` row, which the `User` read policy reserves to level 4 (`FJS-1862`).
  - **A** — yes: the roster projects `User` to name, email and avatar.
  - **B** — no: the roster is graded at the `User` read policy.
  - **Recommend A** — colleagues see colleagues. The defect is that the whole row goes out.

- ~~**FJS-D494 — How does a hand list of a model's field names declare its reason, so `restated-fields` stays silent?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-18 (`FJS-D494`): C — exempt by construct: `only=`/`except=` on a form is UI narrowing by definition and never fires; every other list fires with no silencing at all.** FJS-1393 asks for a `checks.js` rule that fires on any literal array naming a model's fields, "silenced by a declared reason", but no reason spelling exists. Today `narrowPatch(data, ['slug', 'status'])` sits at fifteen basecamp call sites (`resource.ts:134` hardcodes five more), and `example` has six `only={[…]}` form narrowings the row calls declared residual. Parts 1–2 (mark `@immutable`/`@system` in the schema; `narrowPatch` reads `db.$protectedFields()` + the update schema's readOnly set and takes no list) are mechanical once this is settled.
  - **A** — a line comment directly above the list, `// restated: <reason>`, read by the rule the same way `fli check` reads other inline allowances; no reason text means it still fires.
  - **B** — never inline: residuals are named in a per-app allowance table (path + reason) that the rule reads, like the `oracle/mockup` nested-dir allowance.
  - **C** — exempt by construct: `only=`/`except=` on a form is UI narrowing by definition and never fires; every other list fires with no silencing at all.
  - **Recommend C** — once `narrowPatch` takes no list, the only residual left is form narrowing, which is a UI choice rather than a restatement of access, so a silencing noun would have nothing left to silence.

- ~~**FJS-D511 — Should a named move's `@gate` be able to LOWER the model's update level, rather than only floor it?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-10 (`FJS-D511`): D — Keep the floor; *many may submit, few may edit* is a capability grant, not a gate. Everyone who makes the move sits at the update level, and `@@capabilities` (which covers moves by default, `FJS-D140`) grants `Timesheet.submit` to many roles and `Timesheet.update` to few. No new gate spelling, and the access stays in the schema.** `gradeMove` (`packages/litestone/src/core/client.js` ~1048) runs only after `@@gate`'s update level has passed, so `Timesheet @@gate("4.5.5.7")` with `submit: open -> submitted` (no gate) refuses USER(4) with `"Timesheet.update" requires level 5` — *many may make this one move, few may edit* has no spelling (FJS-1245, sibling of FJS-1244).
  - **A** — A move's `@gate` REPLACES the update level for a write that is exactly that move (only the state column, or `transition(id, name)`); an ungated move keeps the update level. Any other field in the payload still needs update. `fli check` warns on a move gated below update, naming it as a deliberate drop box.
  - **B** — Keep the floor; add an explicit move attribute (e.g. `@open` / `@gate(4) @replaces`) that opts one move out of the update level. Two spellings of gate, but the lowering is visible at the move.
  - **C** — Keep the floor; the drop box is a custom method under `asSystem()`. No framework change; the access leaves the schema (strains Invariant 6).
  - **D** — Keep the floor; *many may submit, few may edit* is a capability grant, not a gate. Everyone who makes the move sits at the update level, and `@@capabilities` (which covers moves by default, `FJS-D140`) grants `Timesheet.submit` to many roles and `Timesheet.update` to few. No new gate spelling, and the access stays in the schema.
  - **Recommend A** — the gate written on the move is read as *the authority this move needs*, the payload restriction keeps an arbitrary edit at update level, and the check makes a lowering visible without a second attribute.
  - **Owner, 2026-09-28: parked, no change.** Maybe not a gate question at all: the drop box is probably level-5 users told apart by capabilities, not a move that lowers the level. Revisit when capabilities are designed.

- ~~**FJS-D548 — Who owns a socket whose cookie bearer changes after the upgrade — the transport closing it, the client not sending that call over it, or neither?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-10 (`FJS-D548`): A — the transport closes the socket, and the client cycles it. `ctx.setCookie` on an HTTP response that changes a cookie the request carried closes every connection whose upgrade carried that name at that old value, through `disconnectSession`'s path (`channels.ts:781`: blunt by design, the reconnect re-resolves). Only a holder of the old value can trigger that close, so it closes nobody else's socket. The redeem half needs the client to know: the response carries a signal (one new wire name, e.g. `x-fjs-credential: changed`), and the client cycles its socket as `setToken` already does.** `http.ts` copies the upgrade's headers into `ws.data.headers`, every frame's `ctx.caller.headers`, so `cookie()` reads the cookie the UPGRADE carried for the socket's whole life (`FJS-1503`). A red test (junction, port 0, `channels()`, `bearerClaim({ from: cookie('portal_link') })`): open `/ws` with `cookie: portal_link=live-one`, `answers.find` → 1 row; `POST /portal/leave` (a raw route that `setCookie('portal_link', '', { maxAge: 0 })`) → 200; the same socket's `answers.find` still answers **1 row**, where the assertion is *socket closed, or 0 rows*. The converse is the redeem: a socket opened before the cookie was set never sees it, and the server cannot tell which socket is that browser's, because the upgrade carried nothing identifying it.
  - **A** — the transport closes the socket, and the client cycles it. `ctx.setCookie` on an HTTP response that changes a cookie the request carried closes every connection whose upgrade carried that name at that old value, through `disconnectSession`'s path (`channels.ts:781`: blunt by design, the reconnect re-resolves). Only a holder of the old value can trigger that close, so it closes nobody else's socket. The redeem half needs the client to know: the response carries a signal (one new wire name, e.g. `x-fjs-credential: changed`), and the client cycles its socket as `setToken` already does.
  - **B** — `cookie()` is HTTP-only. A socket frame never resolves a cookie bearer (the resolver answers `{}` there, with a `NO_CLAIM` reason saying why), and the client sends calls over HTTP when the manifest says the principal is cookie-borne. There is no stale read, but it drops the socket's latency for every bearer caller and adds a manifest fact the client reads.
  - **C** — document it: a cookie bearer is read at the upgrade, and an app that redeems or leaves calls `client.disconnect(); client.connect()`, the jazzhr workaround made official. There is no new code, and the security half is left to every app remembering.
  - **Recommend A** — the security half is fully owned by an existing owner (`setCookie` plus the connection manager) with no new noun, and the redeem signal is the smallest wire name that makes HTTP and the socket answer alike. It is the paved road against the workaround that jazzhr is shipping now.
- ~~**FJS-D552 — How does `virtual:sierra` hand `junction`, `analytics` and `theme` to the browser without importing `sierra.config.js` whole, when a custom analytics provider holds functions?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D552`): A — emit the three keys by value (`JSON.stringify`), and refuse a function anywhere in them at config load, naming the key path. A custom analytics provider becomes a module path, `analytics: { provider: '/src/analytics.js' }`, root-relative like `routeTable.output`. `virtual:sierra` imports that module alone. `virtual:sierra-config` (it re-exports the whole config and nothing imports it) is deleted.** `generateVirtualSierra` (`packages/sierra/src/virtual/virtual-sierra.js`) writes `import sierraConfig from '<config>'` to read three keys, so the browser evaluates everything the config imports: a post-build plugin reading `node:fs` blanks every `vite dev` page, and each remark or rehype plugin is shipped too (`FJS-1544`). `test/prefetch-theme.test.js:272-311` pins the import on purpose (*live reference, not JSON*). A red test is `_generateVirtualSierra({ target: 'spa', junction: { url: 'x' } }, 'config/routes.js')`, which must not contain `import sierraConfig from`. It fails today. `initJunction` and `initTheme` read only data. The one function-valued key is the custom analytics provider `{ init, pageview, track }` that README § config documents, and no config in the tree uses it.
  - **A** — emit the three keys by value (`JSON.stringify`), and refuse a function anywhere in them at config load, naming the key path. A custom analytics provider becomes a module path, `analytics: { provider: '/src/analytics.js' }`, root-relative like `routeTable.output`. `virtual:sierra` imports that module alone. `virtual:sierra-config` (it re-exports the whole config and nothing imports it) is deleted.
  - **B** — a separate client-side config file (`config/sierra.client.js`) holding the browser keys, which `virtual:sierra` imports instead. It is a second origin for one config and a new file noun.
  - **C** — emit by value when the three keys are pure data, and fall back to the whole import when a function is present. That leaves two mechanisms, and the fallback silently brings the defect back for any app with a custom provider.
  - **Recommend A** — the config stays the one origin, and a function the browser cannot receive is refused by name instead of dropped. Only one README shape changes, and nothing in the tree uses it.
- ~~**FJS-D568 — How does a sealing model freeze a column from a LATER move than the seal — a move-scoped argument on `@immutable`, a second attribute, or no language change?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-10 (`FJS-D568`): A — `@immutable(after: <move>)`: the existing word with a scope. The column is writable until the named move's target, frozen from there and from every state reachable from it (`sealedStates`' own closure, generalized in `core/seal.js` to take a move name). Bare `@immutable` keeps its two meanings (create on a plain model, the seal on a sealing one). No new noun; one more argument form for the same word, and `db:explain @immutable` (FJS-1482) must describe all three.** Red test, measured: `Offer { signedName String? @immutable, @@transitions(status, approve: draft -> approved @seals, send: approved -> sent, accept: sent -> accepted, decline: sent -> declined) }` — the one-write `update({ status: 'accepted', signedName: 'Ada' })` at `sent` is refused (the column froze at approve), and with no `@immutable` the same column is rewritable after `accepted`. A second `@seals` is refused at parse (`parser.js` `sealFaults`, *already sealed*). Whatever lands, the freeze is graded below `asSystem()` the way the seal is, which the `@allow` + `@@check` recipe jazzhr ships is not.
  - **A** — `@immutable(after: <move>)`: the existing word with a scope. The column is writable until the named move's target, frozen from there and from every state reachable from it (`sealedStates`' own closure, generalized in `core/seal.js` to take a move name). Bare `@immutable` keeps its two meanings (create on a plain model, the seal on a sealing one). No new noun; one more argument form for the same word, and `db:explain @immutable` (FJS-1482) must describe all three.
  - **B** — a new attribute `@sealedBy(<move>)` (or `@frozenAt`): a second word for the same guard, so `@immutable` stays exactly "create or the seal" and the later freeze is greppable on its own. A new noun beside one that already means "frozen".
  - **C** — no language change; document the shipped recipe (`@@check("(status = 'accepted') = (signedName IS NOT NULL)")` plus `@allow('write', status == 'sent')` on the field). It leaves the silent 200 no-op on a later rewrite and `asSystem()` can still overwrite the signature.
  - **Recommend A** — the freeze guard, its error (`SealedDocumentError`) and its closure already exist in `seal.js` and the write guards in `client.js`; the argument only changes which move the closure starts from, so nothing is restated and the move name is checked at parse like `@seals`' own.
- ~~**FJS-D571 — Does a CSV extract neutralize a text cell that starts with `= + - @`, tab or CR, at the cost of what a re-import reads back?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D571`): A — always guard, one owner: a `csvCell` in `@frontierjs/toolbelt/cells` beside `parseCell`, which prefixes `'` to a TEXT cell whose first character is `= + - @`, tab or CR unless the whole string reads as a number by `parseCell`'s own number rule, not a second regex (`-12.50` is left alone). `export.js`, Studio's dump and Transit call it and their copies are deleted, and the export manifest records that the guard ran. CSV becomes the format a person opens, and `ndjson` stays the round-trip format, already exact.** `FJS-1660`. `csvCell` in `packages/litestone/src/export.js:121` quotes by content only, and Studio's table dump carries a copy of it (`tools/cli.js:2650`), so both write `=cmd|' /C calc'!A0` and `=HYPERLINK("https://x","Refund")` bare, and a spreadsheet evaluates them when staff open the file (CWE-1236). An `@@export` is taken at the caller's standing over rows other people wrote. A red test, measured: a `@@export(csv)` model with `ref: "=cmd|' /C calc'!A0"`, run `system: true, format: 'csv'`, emits `2,=cmd|' /C calc'!A0` where the assertion is `2,'=cmd|' /C calc'!A0`. Transit's `api/src/reports/csv.ts` carries the guard today as a third writer.
  - **A** — always guard, one owner: a `csvCell` in `@frontierjs/toolbelt/cells` beside `parseCell`, which prefixes `'` to a TEXT cell whose first character is `= + - @`, tab or CR unless the whole string reads as a number by `parseCell`'s own number rule, not a second regex (`-12.50` is left alone). `export.js`, Studio's dump and Transit call it and their copies are deleted, and the export manifest records that the guard ran. CSV becomes the format a person opens, and `ndjson` stays the round-trip format, already exact.
  - **B** — guard on request: `@@export(csv, guard: formulas)` or a run option, default off. It keeps CSV byte-exact by default, so the unsafe extract is the one nobody asked for, and it adds an option.
  - **C** — no guard: document that a CSV extract is data, not a spreadsheet, and point a spreadsheet reader at a sanitizing import. No code changes, and every app that hands staff the file must remember.
  - **Recommend A** — a CSV extract is opened in a spreadsheet far more often than it is parsed back, a reader that needs fidelity already has `ndjson`, and one writer in toolbelt removes three copies of the rule instead of adding a fourth. *Ergonomics vs. strictness*: an evaluated formula is destructive and a stray `'` is cosmetic, so the guard is the default. *Paved road vs. the workaround*: Transit's copy measures the road, and B is the flag that widens the shoulder. Artefact: the red test above plus a toolbelt test over `= + - @`, tab, CR and numeric text; a fourth hand-rolled writer stays silent (`none`). Tier: Register (a `DECISIONS.md` ruling).

- **FJS-D572 — What does `verifyGateLadder` return when some rows are mismatches and some are gates it could not ask: a `{ mismatches, ungraded }` object, an `ungraded: 'throw' | 'return'` option, or a filtered array with the rest read from the env?** `FJS-1213`. Red test, measured: a two-model schema — `Slot` with `@@check("startTime < endTime")` and `@@gate("1.4.4.5")`, `Note` with `@@gate("1.2.2.5")` and `@@allow('create', ownerId == auth().id)` — makes `await env.verifyGateLadder()` return 29 rows (`Slot:error` 23, `Note:skipped` 6) and zero gate defects, so the generated `expect(await env.verifyGateLadder()).toEqual([])` is red. `testing.js:610–638` pushes `error` (no fixture) and `skipped` (policy / required `@guarded`) into the same `mismatches` array as a real `allow`/`deny` disagreement. The same mixed shape is in `verifyRowPolicies` and `verifyTenantIsolation` (`got: 'skipped'`), `mutationScore` already sorts the rows apart by `INCONCLUSIVE` (`mutate.js:362`), and every caller (`litestone.test.ts`, `view-access.test.ts`, `basecamp`, `orion`, the `fli new` scaffold, `tutor`) asserts `toEqual([])`.
  - **A** — `{ mismatches, ungraded }`: the ladder returns an object, `mismatches` holds only `allow`/`deny` disagreements and a thrown non-refusal at a level whose fixture DID build, `ungraded` holds `skipped` and *no fixture could be built*. The generated assertion becomes `expect((await env.verifyGateLadder()).mismatches).toEqual([])`, and an app that wants the count holds `ungraded.length` steady in a second assertion. Every caller moves in the same change, and the other two verifiers take the same shape or are left as a follow-up row.
  - **B** — `verifyGateLadder({ ungraded: 'return' })`: the array stays and the default is the strict one, so a bare call returns mismatches only and `'return'` appends the non-gradings. The return type then depends on an option, and the default hides unaskable gates from the one assertion that is the app's whole view of this tool.
  - **C** — the array is mismatches only, and `env.ungradedLadder()` (or `env.ladderReport`) holds the last run's non-gradings. No call site changes, but it is a second call and a hidden dependency on call order, and a mutation run reads a stale report.
  - **Recommend A** — two channels is the row's own diagnosis, and a returned object keeps the non-gradings visible in the same call that produced them. Which rows leave `ungraded` for good (the synthetic-principal FK sentinel as in `FJS-1200`, `@immutable` columns as in `FJS-1487`, a `@@check` the auto-factory cannot satisfy) is separate fixture work each cause owns.

- ~~**FJS-D577 — How does a container's port bind follow its hostnames between releases: the `domain:dns` job dispatches a release, or `/route` recreates the container from `docker inspect`?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-16 (`FJS-D577`): A — the job dispatches a release when `rebind` is set (`routeDomainApp` in `domain-dns.job.ts`, the `deploymentRun` path of `deployments.service.ts`). One deploy path, and the container is recreated from the app's own settings. Every hostname edit then mints a Deployment and its step list, and restarts the app, where `/route` today restarts nothing; a rollback target's settings are not the app's, so the release must be a plain redeploy of the live digest.** `FJS-1616`. `docker.deploy` binds `127.0.0.1:` only when `/deploy` names hosts (`FJS-D565`, `packages/outpost/src/docker.js:189`), and `POST /route` (`server.js:118`) moves Caddy without touching the container, so a first hostname leaves the port open off the machine and the last one removed leaves the app on loopback where nothing reaches it. Red test, measured on the shape: `outpost.test.js` with a stub `published()` answering `{ port, loopback: false }` and `hosts: ['a.example.com']` gets `rebind` set and no `docker run`; the assertion that fails today is that after `/route` the container is bound to the bind its hostnames call for.
  - **A** — the job dispatches a release when `rebind` is set (`routeDomainApp` in `domain-dns.job.ts`, the `deploymentRun` path of `deployments.service.ts`). One deploy path, and the container is recreated from the app's own settings. Every hostname edit then mints a Deployment and its step list, and restarts the app, where `/route` today restarts nothing; a rollback target's settings are not the app's, so the release must be a plain redeploy of the live digest.
  - **B** — `/route` recreates the container with the new bind, rebuilding its argv from `docker inspect` (env, volume, limits, restart policy, logging). No Deployment row and no basecamp change, but it is a second deploy path on the machine: any `deploy` flag added later has to be read back from inspect too, or the recreate drops it.
  - **C** — keep the bind as the last release set it, and surface `rebind` on the App screen instead of only in a job log warning. No restart and no second path; the exposure and the dead app both wait for an operator to read a banner and release.
  - **Recommend A** — a restart is a release, and the Deployment row is the record that it happened. B's recreate reconstructs a run config from the daemon's view, which cannot hold what `deploy` was handed and never stored (`config.containerPort`, the log driver choice), so it drifts from the real path the first time `deploy` grows an argument. The dispatch is gated on `rebind`, so an edit that changes no bind restarts nothing.

- ~~**FJS-D578 — How does a field `@allow('write', auth().isStaff)` reach the generated form?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D578`): A — Litestone carries the CALLER-ONLY subset as a fact beside the bit (`x-litestone-write-claims`, e.g. `{ isStaff: true }` or `['auth().isStaff']`), derived by one function next to `determinedColumns` in `policy.js`; sierra's control table disables the control when the session user fails it, and a predicate that reads the row stays a bare bit. Same shape as `x-determined` (`FJS-D492`): an affordance, the boundary enforces regardless (Invariant 6). Needs a defined subset (`auth().<claim>` truthiness, `==` a literal, `&&`) and unknown answers permissive.** `FJS-1671`: `jsonschema.js:606` emits `x-litestone-write-policy: true` for every field write allow, so `field-rules.js` cannot tell a caller-only predicate (`auth().isStaff`) from a row predicate; the form offers the column to a caller the policy refuses and only `declinedFields` says so after the save. The code comment at `jsonschema.js:597` calls carrying the predicate "a second reader on the policy language and a decision of its own".
  - **A** — Litestone carries the CALLER-ONLY subset as a fact beside the bit (`x-litestone-write-claims`, e.g. `{ isStaff: true }` or `['auth().isStaff']`), derived by one function next to `determinedColumns` in `policy.js`; sierra's control table disables the control when the session user fails it, and a predicate that reads the row stays a bare bit. Same shape as `x-determined` (`FJS-D492`): an affordance, the boundary enforces regardless (Invariant 6). Needs a defined subset (`auth().<claim>` truthiness, `==` a literal, `&&`) and unknown answers permissive.
  - **B** — carry the whole predicate expression as data and ship an evaluator to the browser. Answers row predicates too, but is a second reader of the policy language in the client, which the schema comment already refuses.
  - **C** — keep the bit; the page hides the column by the session itself (what Transit does now) and `declinedFields` reports a refusal after the save. No new keyword, but every app copies the policy by hand.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the `x-determined` pattern applied to the write half, answers the case that caused the row without an evaluator in the browser, and a row-dependent predicate degrades to today's behavior.

- ~~**FJS-D579 — From a framework checkout, does `fli new` default `--source local`, refuse npm when a template needs a newer feature than the published package, or keep npm?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-09 (`FJS-D579`): A — default `local` when `fliRoot` sits in a framework checkout (`<root>/packages/junction/package.json` beside the cli), npm otherwise. A published `fli` never has that sibling, so the person the comment protects still gets npm, and the dev cli writes templates against the code those templates were written for. `--source npm` still forces it; the plan summary already names the source.** `FJS-1637`. Measured: a scaffold made without `--source local` installs junction 0.1.8 from npm, and the template's `async work()` (`project/new.md:635`, `FJS-D551`) fails `bun run check` with TS2353 on day one; the tree's own junction is also 0.1.8, so no version compare can see the gap. `new.md:2306` defaults `flag.source || $FJS_SOURCE || 'npm'`, and the comment above it argues npm is the right starting point to hand somebody.
  - **A** — default `local` when `fliRoot` sits in a framework checkout (`<root>/packages/junction/package.json` beside the cli), npm otherwise. A published `fli` never has that sibling, so the person the comment protects still gets npm, and the dev cli writes templates against the code those templates were written for. `--source npm` still forces it; the plan summary already names the source.
  - **B** — keep the npm default and refuse it when the template needs a newer feature: a `requires` stamp per template feature checked against the registry's latest. Names the real cause, but is a second list that must be updated with every plugin-protocol change, and "newer than published" is a network lookup in a scaffold.
  - **C** — keep npm and publish before the template changes. No code, and a red scaffold from the tree is the normal state between publishes; the next unpublished feature does it again.
  - **Recommend A** — the failure only exists from a checkout, where the cli and the packages are one version of one tree, so choosing the tree removes the gap instead of detecting it. The comment's reason for npm (a starting point for somebody else) is a published cli's case, and A leaves it untouched.

- ~~**FJS-D580 — Do error mappers and the service-cache override become per-app, stay process-global with a one-app guard, or stay as they are?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D580`): A — per-app: `createApp({ errorMappers: [...] })` / `app.errorMapper(fn)` and `app.serviceCache = cache` (claimed), and `toFrameworkError(err, app)` takes the mapper list from the app; the module-level `registerErrorMapper`/`setServiceCache` are deleted and basecamp moves in the same change. Isolates apps, but every `toFrameworkError` caller must thread the app, and `hooks.ts:215` and `plugins/export` have only a ctx.** `FJS-1510`. `registerErrorMapper` pushes to a module-level `_mappers` (`errors.ts:84`) read by `toFrameworkError(err)` (`errors.ts:224`), which takes no app and is called from `bridge.ts:375,477`, `hooks.ts:215`, `service.ts:723`, `middleware.ts:360` and `plugins/export`; `setServiceCache` sets `_overrideCache` (`service.ts:77`), checked before the per-app cache in `resolveCache`. Two `createApp()`s in one process (tests, embedders) share both: app B's mapper rewrites app A's errors, and one app's override cache serves the other's reads. Basecamp's `registerErrorMapper` call (`app.ts:255`) is app-wide on purpose. `app.claim()` bypass (`app.x = v` after a claim) and `ready()` running after `listen` (`ready-hooks`, `needsHost`) are separate calls and ride in the row, not here.
  - **A** — per-app: `createApp({ errorMappers: [...] })` / `app.errorMapper(fn)` and `app.serviceCache = cache` (claimed), and `toFrameworkError(err, app)` takes the mapper list from the app; the module-level `registerErrorMapper`/`setServiceCache` are deleted and basecamp moves in the same change. Isolates apps, but every `toFrameworkError` caller must thread the app, and `hooks.ts:215` and `plugins/export` have only a ctx.
  - **B** — keep the globals, add a guard: a second `createApp()` in one process while a mapper or override is registered throws or warns by name. One owner stays one owner and no signature changes, but it forbids two apps in a process, which `createTestEnv` and embedders do.
  - **C** — keep as is and state the process-global contract in the JSDoc and the junction `CLAUDE.md` (the cache JSDoc already says "prefer it only when one process runs one app"). No code, and the cross-app leak stays for the next test suite that boots two apps.
  - **Recommend A** — the cache already resolves per app and only the override leaks, so A finishes a move the file's own comment began, and deleting the global spelling is free pre-alpha. Thread the app through `ctx.app`, which every caller except a bare `toFrameworkError(err)` already has.

- ~~**FJS-D581 — What refuses a write made inside a `read: true` custom method?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-10 (`FJS-D581`): A — Junction hands a read call a client that refuses writes: for an `isRead` custom call, `ctx.locals.db` is a Proxy whose model accessors throw on `create`/`update`/`remove`/`*Many`/`upsert`/`transition` by name, one owner in `withLitestoneDb` where the client is already scoped. No Litestone change and no new word. It covers only the handed-over client: `db.asSystem()`, an imported root client and a raw `$db` statement walk past it.** `FJS-1477`. The row names `$tapEvents` as the seam, and it cannot be one: `dispatchEvent` (`litestone/src/core/client.js`) runs a tap in `setImmediate`, swallows its throw, and inside a transaction holds it to the commit — an Observer (`FJS-D06`) by construction. A tap sees the write after the statement ran, so it cannot throw a 500 into it or roll anything back. `isReadMethod(service, method)` (`service.ts`) already knows the call is a read; nothing between it and the statement can say no. Red test: a `read: true` method that does `ctx.locals.db.pass.create(...)` answers 200 today, and the assertion is a 500 naming the method and the model with zero `Pass` rows left.
  - **A** — Junction hands a read call a client that refuses writes: for an `isRead` custom call, `ctx.locals.db` is a Proxy whose model accessors throw on `create`/`update`/`remove`/`*Many`/`upsert`/`transition` by name, one owner in `withLitestoneDb` where the client is already scoped. No Litestone change and no new word. It covers only the handed-over client: `db.asSystem()`, an imported root client and a raw `$db` statement walk past it.
  - **B** — Litestone grows a synchronous write guard, the `$logContext` shape pointed the other way: `db.$guardWrites(fn)` is asked `(model, operation)` before each statement, inside the transaction, and a throw refuses it and rolls back. Junction installs one closure over its call store (`isReadMethod` of the current call), so every path to the client is covered, `asSystem()` included. One new Litestone surface, which ships as two halves (the spelling and the refusal) and a typegen line.
  - **C** — keep the tap and only report: a write seen under a read call logs one error naming the method and model and counts on `/metrics`. No refusal, no rollback and no 500 — the retry still spends twice and the write is announced by the tap, so it catches the silence and not the double spend. Nothing new to build, and the row's *refuse* half is not met.
  - **Recommend B** — the guard has to sit below every path to the client or a method that reaches for `asSystem()` is exactly the one that escapes, and `$logContext` is the precedent for Junction handing Litestone a closure over its own store (Invariant 1 holds). A is the cheaper cut if the owner decides a read method is only ever written against `ctx.locals.db`.

- ~~**FJS-D588 — Does a copy derived from other rows (an inherited tree grant) get a write hook that hears every write with its rows inside the transaction, does the framework own the tree grant so no copy exists, or does the writing service keep the copy?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D588`): A — complete the hooks: `createMany`/`updateMany` fire `onAfterWrite` once per row (rows by `RETURNING *` only when a plugin listens, the `deleteMany` prefetch precedent), and nothing more yet: the in-transaction timing is not built, and it and B (the framework owns the tree grant) are decided after notion Q1's B is measured.** `FJS-1725`. Re-probed at `6bfa023e` with a `Plugin` on a plain model: `create` reaches `onAfterWrite` with the row, `deleteMany` reaches `onAfterDelete` with every row it removed (prefetched only when a plugin listens), and `createMany` and `updateMany` reach NO after-hook at all, though `plugin.js` documents `onAfterWrite` as firing with `null` for a many-op. Every after-hook runs after the commit, so a throw leaves the write landed and the copy stale, and a revoked guest keeps reading (fails OPEN). The after-commit timing is deliberate for one consumer: `onAfterDelete` exists so S3 cleanup never runs for a row that survived, so moving the hooks inside the transaction breaks that one.
  - **A** — complete the hooks: `createMany`/`updateMany` fire `onAfterWrite` once per row (rows by `RETURNING *` only when a plugin listens, the `deleteMany` prefetch precedent), and a second, in-transaction timing is added for derived rows, where a throw rolls the write back. If `FJS-D581` picks B, that seam is `$guardWrites` grown to carry the rows, one in-transaction seam rather than two. The copy stays a hand-kept second origin, but nothing can skip it.
  - **B** — the framework owns the tree grant (notion PLAN Q1 spelling B, a `@@tree` closure table it maintains, or C, a recursive policy, or D, a materialized path). No copy exists, so nothing goes stale. B–D are not measured yet; Q1 parked them.
  - **C** — neither: the copy is a convention the writing service keeps (what the stressor's passing run did), documented in the litestone hazards. No code, and every writer that forgets it fails open silently.
  - **Recommend A's first half now, B decided after Q1's B is measured** — a bulk write that no hook hears is a predictability defect whatever Q1 answers (its siblings are heard), and fixing it costs no new noun. The in-transaction timing is a new concept, and B would make it unnecessary for this case, so it waits for B's numbers instead of being built before them.

- ~~**FJS-D596 — Is a channel bound to the row it is for, so joining and staying are graded at the Data boundary, or is a bound channel's every presence frame and send graded per recipient, or does admission stay the app's call?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-10 (`FJS-D596`): A — bind the channel to a row: `channels({ rooms: { 'page:{id}': 'Page' } })` names which model a channel name stands for. A `join` on a matching name asks `$readAs` for that row and the principal (refused: no join, and `join` says so); after each committed write to a model the bound model's read policy reaches, the manager re-asks once per (room, cohort) and evicts the members who lost the row, sending each of them a `presence:sync` with no members, so their screen empties. Presence and `send` then need no per-frame grading, since membership stays true. The reach set (which models a read policy depends on) is the litestone fact `FJS-1738` also needs, so it is built once for both. Adds one option, and amends `FJS-D175`'s clause to say that a BOUND channel's join is graded.** `FJS-1743`. `Channel.join` (`transport/channels.ts:139`) adds a connection with no question asked, and `FJS-D175` rules that a join is a subscription and never a permission because a PUBLISH is graded per recipient. Presence (`presence.ts:126`, diffs batched to every member) and `channel(name).send` are not publishes, so on a room like `page:D` they are the two paths nothing grades: measured in the notion stressor (PLAN § Q4), a guest whose share is revoked keeps both directions of cursors and receives a raw fragment, while a comment published in the same second is withheld. `presenceLeave` queues `left` only to the members who remain, so a connection taken out of a room is told nothing and its own roster stays full. The red test: open two sockets as owner and guest, join both to a channel for a row the guest reads, revoke the guest's grant, and `channel(name).send` — the guest receives the frame today.
  - **A** — bind the channel to a row: `channels({ rooms: { 'page:{id}': 'Page' } })` names which model a channel name stands for. A `join` on a matching name asks `$readAs` for that row and the principal (refused: no join, and `join` says so); after each committed write to a model the bound model's read policy reaches, the manager re-asks once per (room, cohort) and evicts the members who lost the row, sending each of them a `presence:sync` with no members, so their screen empties. Presence and `send` then need no per-frame grading, since membership stays true. The reach set (which models a read policy depends on) is the litestone fact `FJS-1738` also needs, so it is built once for both. Adds one option, and amends `FJS-D175`'s clause to say that a BOUND channel's join is graded.
  - **B** — grade every frame on a bound channel: presence diffs and `send` go through `gradeRecipients` as a publish does, with the row the binding names, cohort-cached per (connection, room) and invalidated by `$tapEvents`. Leaves `FJS-D175` whole and nothing leaks, but cursors are the highest-rate frame an app sends, the revoked member still shows on everyone else's roster, and a send that carries no row has to be told which row it is about.
  - **C** — keep admission the app's call, as `FJS-D175` has it: add `channel.evict(conn)` that sends the evicted connection its empty sync, and document the pattern. No new option, but every app restates the read policy in a hook (Invariant 6), sees only the grants it copies (the stressor's missed inherited grant), and acts before the transaction commits.
  - **Recommend A** — membership is the one thing presence and `send` share, so keeping it true covers both paths without grading a cursor per frame, and the rule comes from the schema rather than from a hook. The reach set it needs is owed to `FJS-1738` anyway.

- ~~**FJS-D597 — When a write lands on a model another model's read policy reaches, does the Data boundary regrade the dependent rows, does the write announce `changed` on every dependent model, or does the app announce it?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-10 (`FJS-D597`): B — announce at the model level: litestone derives `db.$readDependents(accessor)`, the models whose read policy reaches this one, transitively, from the schema; the tap adds a count-only `changed` (gate mode, the frame bulk writes already send, `FJS-307`) on each dependent model's channels. Stores already re-read on `changed`, so lists heal under one frame per model; every reader of a dependent model re-reads, and `record(id)` heals only once `FJS-1740` is fixed. Fails closed: the over-read is a cost, never a leak.** `FJS-1738`. `gradeRecipients` (`junction/src/transport/channels.ts:443`) grades the payload against the WRITTEN accessor only, and `announceDataWrites` (`junction/src/core/litestone.ts:4132`) announces only under `servicesFor(e.model)`; litestone has no reverse edge of its policy graph (`policy.js` builds `shares.some(...)` and `check(page)` forward only). So `shares.remove` sends `shares removed` and nothing on `pages` or `blocks`: the red assertion is a revoked reader's `resource('pages')` still holding D, and `blocks.load({pageId: D})` still holding 3, after the one frame they receive. The reach set is the same one `FJS-D596`'s A needs.
  - **A** — regrade the dependent rows: litestone answers which rows of which models a written row's policy edges reach (Share → its page → that page's blocks, transitively, and down a tree copy), and the tap grades each per recipient, sending `created` to a new reader and `removed` to a lost one. Exact frames, but N rows per write (the notion stressor's 187-page move), a row enumeration per policy shape, and a missed edge fails OPEN as silently as today.
  - **B** — announce at the model level: litestone derives `db.$readDependents(accessor)`, the models whose read policy reaches this one, transitively, from the schema; the tap adds a count-only `changed` (gate mode, the frame bulk writes already send, `FJS-307`) on each dependent model's channels. Stores already re-read on `changed`, so lists heal under one frame per model; every reader of a dependent model re-reads, and `record(id)` heals only once `FJS-1740` is fixed. Fails closed: the over-read is a cost, never a leak.
  - **C** — leave it to the app: the service that writes a Share announces on `pages`/`blocks` itself. No code, but every app restates the policy graph by hand (a second origin), and the stressor's own `service` arm shows it still misses Blocks.
  - **Recommend B** — it derives from the one origin, reuses the frame and store behavior siblings already have, and its failure is an extra read rather than a revoked page left on screen; A can follow as a narrowing where a measurement shows the re-read costs more than the rows.

- ~~**FJS-D602 — When `outpost.service` is sandboxed, does the sandbox cover `/exec`'s recipe scripts too, or do they run outside it?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D602`): A — sandbox the unit as the row says, and have `exec` leave it: the command is run as `systemd-run --pipe --wait --collect -- sh -c …`, a transient unit with the manager's own (unsandboxed) environment. A flaw in `/static/publish` or any other route is bounded by the sandbox; `/exec` is root over the box by design and the screen's wording stays true. Adds one call shape to `docker.js`'s runner (the one place a command runs), and the sandbox is a seatbelt rather than a wall for anyone who can already reach `/exec`, since that route is root. Never run on a machine (`FJS-257`): `systemd-run` from inside the namespace is the thing to prove first.** `FJS-1761`. `docker.exec` (`outpost/src/docker.js:391`) runs `sh -c <script>` as a child of the Outpost, so every recipe inherits the unit's mount namespace. The seed's own recipes (`db/seed.js:721`) are `apt-get update && apt-get upgrade` and `nginx -t && systemctl reload nginx`; under the row's `ProtectSystem=strict` with `ReadWritePaths` for `/etc/basecamp`, `/var/lib/outpost` and bun's cache, the first fails on a read-only `/usr` and `/var/lib/dpkg`, the second on `/etc` and `/run`. The recipe form says *there is no sandbox* (`web/src/routes/recipes/index.mesa:237`), which stops being true. Red test: `installScript()` holds none of `NoNewPrivileges`, `ProtectSystem`, `PrivateTmp`, `ReadWritePaths` today; it can be written once this is answered, since the answer decides which lines it asserts.
  - **A** — sandbox the unit as the row says, and have `exec` leave it: the command is run as `systemd-run --pipe --wait --collect -- sh -c …`, a transient unit with the manager's own (unsandboxed) environment. A flaw in `/static/publish` or any other route is bounded by the sandbox; `/exec` is root over the box by design and the screen's wording stays true. Adds one call shape to `docker.js`'s runner (the one place a command runs), and the sandbox is a seatbelt rather than a wall for anyone who can already reach `/exec`, since that route is root. Never run on a machine (`FJS-257`): `systemd-run` from inside the namespace is the thing to prove first.
  - **B** — sandbox the whole unit, recipes included, and say so on the recipe screen: a recipe can write only the Outpost's own directories and run docker. The strongest unit by `systemd-analyze security`, but host-administration recipes (patching, `systemctl`) stop working, and `server-posture.md`'s patching phase would need its own door anyway.
  - **C** — a weaker unit that keeps every recipe working: `NoNewPrivileges`, `PrivateTmp`, `ProtectKernelModules`, `ProtectControlGroups`, `RestrictSUIDSGID`, and no `ProtectSystem`. Nothing written by a route bug is confined, which is the row's named risk, so `outpost.sandbox` would grade a unit that still writes anywhere.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only one that narrows the routes a flaw would live in without taking away the thing `/exec` is for; B trades a feature for a number, and C leaves the row's risk standing.

- ~~**FJS-D623 — How does an Oracle answer name which membership role administers a container?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D623`): A — a key on the container's access, `roles: { update: ['recruiter'] }` (spelled by `FJS-D1006`), naming values of the membership's role enum; `checkAnswer` refuses a value the enum lacks and a container with `members` and no `roles.update` emits membership writes as system-only (gate 8), so forgetting it fails closed.** `accessFor` (`packages/oracle/src/emit.js`) writes the container's update as `members.some(userId == auth().id)` and delegates every membership write to it, so on the hiring fixture an interviewer re-roles themselves recruiter, adds a stranger, or removes the recruiter (`FJS-1799`). The fix the row asks for — `members.some(userId == auth().id && role == <admin>)` — needs `<admin>`, and nothing in the answer names it: the role enum is the product's (`recruiter`, `manager`, `interviewer`). The red test: a real client on the emitted hiring schema, an `interviewer` member's `membership.update({ role: 'recruiter' })` on their own row, and `membership.create` of a stranger, each expected refused; both succeed today.
  - **A** — a key on the container's access, `admins: ['recruiter']`, naming values of the membership's role enum; `checkAnswer` refuses a value the enum lacks and a container with `members` and no `admins` emits membership writes as system-only (gate 8), so forgetting it fails closed.
  - **B** — a reserved value: the role enum must carry `owner` (and may carry `admin`), which administer, as `references/Organization.lite` and quo already spell it; `checkAnswer` refuses a role enum without `owner`. No new key, but the product's enum loses a word.
  - **Recommend A** — the catalog says the role enum is the product's, so which word administers is the product's answer and the answer is where Oracle reads access from; the fail-closed default keeps a missing key from reopening this row.

- ~~**FJS-D624 — Are a raw `app.ws` route's frames handled one at a time, in arrival order, and is that the default or the route's choice?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D624`): A — ordered by default for a raw route: each socket's frames run on one chain, and `maxInFlight` bounds the queue length rather than concurrency. `channels()` marks its handler set (as `SESSION_SOCKET` already does) to keep concurrency. No new option; a raw route that wants concurrency fires and forgets inside its own handler.** `_wsMessage` (`packages/junction/src/transport/http.ts`) starts each frame's `message()` on arrival and awaits it on its own, so a handler that awaits I/O sees frame 2 before frame 1 settles, and past `maxInFlight` (32) frames are DROPPED with an error frame. A stream (Telnyx media: `connected`, `start`, then audio every 20 ms) needs order and no drops; quo chains each socket's frames itself (`fjs-prototypes/quo/api/src/domain/agent.ts`). The app's own `/ws` (`channels()`) multiplexes independent service calls and wants them concurrent. FJS-1828 fixed the other half: frames now wait for `open`.
