- **FJS-D974 — How does a connection get into a bound room (`channels({ rooms })`) whose row was created after its socket opened: the client asks by `subscribe`, or the server joins every live connection the new row admits?** `FJS-2807`: the only server moment that names a connection is `channels.on('connection')`, and a `subscribe` from a non-member is ignored (`transport/channels.ts`, `parsed.type === 'subscribe'`), so a `SupportThread` created after the customer's socket opened answers no `presence:sync` on `support:{id}` until the socket reconnects. The red test's assertion: open a socket, create the row, send `subscribe` on `support:{id}`, expect a `presence:sync` listing that connection.
  - **A** — a `subscribe` on a BOUND name joins, through the same `admitToRoom` the server's join takes (graded at the Data boundary, re-asked after every write); an unbound name is ignored as today. No new option and no new noun; it amends `FJS-D175`'s "subscribe does NOT join" for exactly the names `FJS-D596` made gradable. Only the tab that asks is in the room, and each ask costs one row read, bounded by the frame rate.
  - **B** — a rule on `rooms`: when a write creates a row of a bound model, grade every live connection against it and join the admitted ones. Needs no client change, but it is an option or a rule word, grades O(connections) per create, and puts every tab of every admitted person in a room none of them opened, so presence lies about who is looking.
  - **C** — keep admission the app's: document the `localConnectionsOf(userId)` scan from a service hook as the paved road. Nothing to build, but it over-joins every tab of the session and each app restates the read policy in a hook (Invariant 6).
  - **Recommend A** — membership stays server-graded by the one owner, the client only asks to be considered, and presence shows who opened the room.

- ~~**FJS-D975 — What word says a `@@transitions` move stamps a time column in the same write?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D975`): A — a per-move attribute beside `@gate`/`@system`/`@seals`: `send: draft -> sent @stamps(sentAt)`. `transition()` (and `update()` landing that move) puts the clock in the named `DateTime` columns in the one UPDATE, the way `@updatedAt` stamps; the column is engine-owned (a payload naming it is refused like `@system`). Move-keyed, so two moves into one state can stamp different columns, and it extends an attribute list that already exists.** `FJS-2801`: `transition()` writes the target field alone (`client.js` `transition`, `data: { [targetField]: targetValue }`), and a `@@allow('update', … && status == 'draft')` hides the row from any update made after the move, so `sentAt` is stamped before the move (two writes for one event) or never. Red test's assertion: a model with `sentAt DateTime?`, `send: draft -> sent` and an update policy keyed on `status == 'draft'`; `transition(id, 'send')` then reads back `sentAt` non-null in the same audited write.
  - **A** — a per-move attribute beside `@gate`/`@system`/`@seals`: `send: draft -> sent @stamps(sentAt)`. `transition()` (and `update()` landing that move) puts the clock in the named `DateTime` columns in the one UPDATE, the way `@updatedAt` stamps; the column is engine-owned (a payload naming it is refused like `@system`). Move-keyed, so two moves into one state can stamp different columns, and it extends an attribute list that already exists.
  - **B** — a field attribute naming the state it records: `sentAt DateTime? @enteredAt(status.sent)`. The column declares itself, so every write that lands `status` in `sent` stamps it, `transition()` or a form's `update()` alike, and the row says where its time came from. A new noun on the field, and a state reached by two moves stamps once per entry unless it says otherwise.
  - **C** — neither: let `transition(id, name, { data })` carry extra columns written with the move. No new schema word, but the caller supplies the time (forgeable, restated per call site) and the stamp stays optional, so a direct `transition()` still leaves `sentAt` null.
  - **Recommend A** — the move is already the unit that carries `@gate`, `@system` and `@seals`, so the stamp belongs on it and the engine writes it, which is what makes a direct `transition()` unable to leave it null.

- ~~**FJS-D980 — What spells a merge-field hole in text a user wrote, and is the body's format stated by the caller or sniffed?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D980`): A — `{path}` single brace, the path a dotted field name or a `toolbelt/predicate` values expression (`FJS-1998`). Matches both ela and ss-planner and what office users already type, and `fill` runs only on stored text, never `.mesa` source, so Mesa's `{…}` (`FJS-873`) never meets it. `data.template` moves onto `fill` in the same change, `{{x}}` gone, so orion keeps one spelling. A literal brace is written `\{`.** `FJS-2680`: toolbelt has no `fill` (`rg 'fill\('` over `packages/toolbelt/src` is empty), orion's `data.template` is `{{x}}` with `String(val)` (`packages/orion/src/engine/nodes/index.ts:108`), and the apps that filled by hand wrote `{group.field}` (ela, ss-planner) and `{{expr}}` (orion, chatwoot). The red test is `fill('<p>{client.name}</p>', { client: { name: '<img onerror=x>' } }, { mode: 'html' })` returning `&lt;img…`, and `{ mode: 'text' }` returning it plain; no function exists to run it against.
  - **A** — `{path}` single brace, the path a dotted field name or a `toolbelt/predicate` values expression (`FJS-1998`). Matches both ela and ss-planner and what office users already type, and `fill` runs only on stored text, never `.mesa` source, so Mesa's `{…}` (`FJS-873`) never meets it. `data.template` moves onto `fill` in the same change, `{{x}}` gone, so orion keeps one spelling. A literal brace is written `\{`.
  - **B** — `{{path}}` double brace, the orion/chatwoot/Mustache spelling. Survives a sentence with a stray `{`, and cannot be mistaken for Mesa's `{…}`, but it moves ela and ss-planner's stored templates, and an office user types it less readily.
  - **C** — `fill` takes the hole delimiters as an argument and both spellings ship. Nothing moves, but it is an option, and two spellings for one thing is the state this row complains of.
  - **Format** — under any of A–C the caller states `mode: 'html' | 'text'` (escape the value in `html`, plain in `text`; `{ html, text }` for app-built markup), and `fill` never guesses from tags, which is what makes ela's `htmlOf` wrong. An unknown name is refused by a `check(source, fields)` beside `fill` (the registry is `FJS-2683`), a falsy value fills as written, and a field the context lacks is left as written.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the spelling both ported apps already stored in their templates, so no stored body changes, and the `data.template` move leaves one hole syntax in the repo rather than two.

- **FJS-D999 — What counts the distinct local days an instant column falls on, when SQLite has no zone database?** `FJS-2615`: `aggregate({ _count: { distinct: 'sentAt' } })` is `COUNT(DISTINCT col)` (`client.js` `aggregate`), so it counts instants, not days; ELA's contact bars key each instant through `Intl.DateTimeFormat` in a hand-written read step. Neither engine can do it in SQL exactly: `bun:sqlite` registers no user function (`rg 'createFunction' packages/litestone/src` is empty), and SQLite's date modifiers take a fixed offset, so `date(col, '-5 hours')` is wrong for the half of the year a DST zone is not on that offset. The zone is also the account's, a relation, which `_count` cannot read.
  - **A** — `_count: { distinct: { day: 'sentAt', offset: '-05:00' } }` (or a bound `$offset`): `COUNT(DISTINCT date(col, ?))`. One query, no new noun beyond the object form, but a fixed offset is the zone only outside DST, so the answer is silently an hour off across a transition — the `FJS-2170` failure again.
  - **B** — the app passes a day table: litestone grows `distinctDays(field, { zone, where })` as a helper that selects the instants (`select: { sentAt }`, graded and policy-filtered as any read) and keys them in JS through `Intl.DateTimeFormat`, DST-exact on either engine. A second read path beside `aggregate`, and O(rows) rather than one aggregate, but the rows are one parent's last 30 days.
  - **C** — nothing in litestone: this is a display-side question of the same family as `FJS-2008`, `FJS-D288` already ruled a zone is spent only where an instant and a day cross, and the toolbelt `datetime` kit is where that crossing lives (`toolbelt` grows `distinctDays(instants, zone)`). The read step stays, but calls one tested function instead of its own `Intl` keying. Closes `FJS-2615` as a toolbelt row.
  - **Recommend C** — no SQL form is exact (A) and a second read path in litestone (B) re-owns a crossing `FJS-D288` put in the kit; the caller already holds the instants, so one tested kit function removes the duplication with no new litestone surface.

- **FJS-D1000 — Should the theme switch offer a mode+accent pair, or stay one whole theme per choice?** `FJS-2513`: `applyTheme` (`packages/sierra/src/theme/index.js:266`) does `classList.add(value)` for one name from `theme.themes`, so `theme-dark` + `theme-sunset` (accent-only, `packages/css/src/themes/sunset.css`, which nests over any surface) cannot both come from the switch; `classList.add('theme-dark theme-sunset')` throws `InvalidCharacterError`. The red test: `setTheme('theme-dark theme-sunset')` with that entry declared leaves `<html>` carrying both classes and neither stale one; today it is refused or throws.
  - **A** — a `themes` entry may name several classes, space-separated. `applyTheme` splits it and removes every class of every declared entry before adding; `setTheme`, `toggleTheme`, persistence and `theme.value` keep treating the entry as one opaque choice, and `buildThemeScript` splits the same way. No new noun or option; a picker's swatch wears the entry's classes.
  - **B** — two axes: `theme: { modes, accents }` with `setMode` / `setAccent`, persisted under two keys and written as two classes. The honest model of "dark with a red accent", but two public verbs, a second persisted key and a `system` map that must say which accent follows the OS.
  - **C** — no switch change: the kit ships (or the app writes) one whole dark-with-accent theme per accent. Sierra is untouched, but it multiplies kit themes by modes and is the app stylesheet restating `theme-dark`'s tokens the row names as the cost.
  - **Recommend A** — the switch already owns a list of whole choices and the css themes already nest, so letting an entry name more than one class closes the gap without a second axis, a new verb or a second persisted key.

- ~~**FJS-D1001 — Where does a color that is the data (a levels ramp, a hue swatch) live under Invariant 13, and does `css-raw-literal` learn a declared exemption for it?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1001`): B — a declared exemption in the stylesheet: a custom property named `--data-*` is a declaration like any `--x:` and the rule already blanks custom-property declarations (`checks.js:3585`), so `--data-ramp: linear-gradient(to right, black, white)` is silent and the rule `background: var(--data-ramp)` reads a token. No new syntax and no new noun, since the rule already treats a declared token as the place a literal belongs; what it adds is a naming convention, graded by nothing unless `css-token-undefined` is told `--data-*` need no theme definition.** `FJS-2493`: `css-raw-literal` (`packages/cli/core/checks.js:3560`) reads `<style>` blocks only, so `background: linear-gradient(to right, black, white)` warns twice there and the same literal in a script const passed through `style:background` is silent. The red test's assertion: a `<style>` rule carrying the declared form yields `findings` of `[]` while the same literal undeclared yields two. Today the only way through is to hide the literal where the rule does not look, and nothing says the color is data.
  - **A** — the ruling only: a data color lives in script and reaches markup through `style:` (what Darkroom does today). No rule change; the check's blind spot becomes the documented form, and `css AGENTS.md` § Escape hatch says so. Cheapest, but a data color is then unreadable as data to anything that greps, and the rule's silence stays an accident of where it scans.
  - **B** — a declared exemption in the stylesheet: a custom property named `--data-*` is a declaration like any `--x:` and the rule already blanks custom-property declarations (`checks.js:3585`), so `--data-ramp: linear-gradient(to right, black, white)` is silent and the rule `background: var(--data-ramp)` reads a token. No new syntax and no new noun, since the rule already treats a declared token as the place a literal belongs; what it adds is a naming convention, graded by nothing unless `css-token-undefined` is told `--data-*` need no theme definition.
  - **C** — a comment form (`/* data-color */` above the declaration) the rule reads and skips. Explicit and local, but a second spelling for what a custom property already says, and a comment is the form nothing else in the stack reads.
  - **Recommend B** — the rule already exempts a declared custom property because declaring a token is where a literal belongs, so a data color is the same act with a name that says it is not themed; it needs no new syntax, and it leaves the slider-tone gap (`Slider.mesa` `--bg-mix`) to its own row.

- ~~**FJS-D1002 — How does a generated `<Form>` draw and write an implicit m2m relation: which entry carries it, what loads its members, and which nested-write op does `save` send?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-30 (`FJS-D1002`): A — the relation is a field of the form's rule table: `buildFieldRules` appends a synthetic rule per m2m (`{ type: 'array', relation: { kind: 'm2m', model, field } }`, writable, `multiselect` control) and `options(name)` answers from the related resource as it does for a FK. Loading: the resource's detail read adds `include: { serviceAreas: { select: { id } } }` for each m2m a form draws, and the field's value is the id list. Writing: `save()` converts a changed list to `{ set: ids.map(id => ({ id })) }`, one op, idempotent. Everything rides existing seams (`formFields`, `options`, `save`) and `<Form>` itself is untouched; the cost is a rule that is not a column, which `buildFieldRules`, `toFieldErrors` and `stripReadOnly` each have to learn not to treat as one.** `FJS-2674`: `buildFieldRules` reads `properties` only, and an implicit m2m is in `x-relations` (`type: 'm2m'`) and in no `properties` (`schema-generation.test.js` 'an implicit m2m relation is not a field of the model'), so `formFieldList(fields)` has no `serviceAreas` entry and `<Form>` draws nothing; the red test is `formFieldList` over a `SurveyTemplate` schema with `x-relations: [{ field: 'serviceAreas', model: 'ServiceArea', type: 'm2m' }]` expecting a `serviceAreas` entry with a `multiselect` control, which finds none. Three things have to be chosen and the code settles none: `options(field)` answers only for an enum, a `values` set or a `rule.references` FK (`resource.js` options), `save()` patches the diff against the row `_read` holds, and that row has no relation under it unless the read included one; the write is now possible since `FJS-2633` (`{ serviceAreas: { set: [{ id }] } }` passes autoValidate).
  - **A** — the relation is a field of the form's rule table: `buildFieldRules` appends a synthetic rule per m2m (`{ type: 'array', relation: { kind: 'm2m', model, field } }`, writable, `multiselect` control) and `options(name)` answers from the related resource as it does for a FK. Loading: the resource's detail read adds `include: { serviceAreas: { select: { id } } }` for each m2m a form draws, and the field's value is the id list. Writing: `save()` converts a changed list to `{ set: ids.map(id => ({ id })) }`, one op, idempotent. Everything rides existing seams (`formFields`, `options`, `save`) and `<Form>` itself is untouched; the cost is a rule that is not a column, which `buildFieldRules`, `toFieldErrors` and `stripReadOnly` each have to learn not to treat as one.
  - **B** — same drawing and loading as A, but `save()` sends the diff against the baseline as `{ connect: [...added], disconnect: [...removed] }`. A concurrent edit by someone else survives (the `FJS-809` reason a patch carries only what changed), and a gate on a row the editor never saw is not exercised, but it needs the baseline id list kept and compared, and a member removed elsewhere while the form was open makes `disconnect` a no-op the screen cannot explain.
  - **C** — no generated field: `<Form>` stays columns-only and a relation is a named control the app draws (`<RelationPicker resource field>` in the kit, reading `resource.options` and writing through a service method), listed beside the existing `reason` warnings so the omission is said by name. Smallest change and no new rule shape, but ela's `serviceAreas` picker stays hand-written in every app, which is the restated list `formFieldList` exists to end.
  - **Recommend A** — a generated form is meant to be the whole of a model's writable surface, `FJS-D17`'s control table already routes a name to a control, and `set` is the op the form's mental model (these are the members) means; the baseline-diff of B can follow if a lost concurrent edit is ever measured.

- **FJS-D1003 — Where does a list cell's label come from when the row holds only a key?** `columns()` names the label field (`FJS-1973`) but `list()` is built with no `include`, so `<Cell>` finds no `record[relation]` and prints the id; a `@values` enum column (`display: 'enum'`, `set/model/valueField/labelField`, no `options`) has no relation to include at all, so `enumLabel` prints `String(v)`. The red assertion: for `Order.customerId` with a `Customer.name`, `orders.columns().include` is `{ customer: true }` (today `undefined`); for a column bound `@values`, a resolved `options` list reaches the entry (today `enumLabel` has none).
  - **A** — two halves, each at its owner. `columns()` answers a top-level `include` (`{ customer: true }`, one entry per relation column it shows) and the generated list passes it as `list({ directives: { include } })`; a value-set enum is resolved by a new async `resource.columnOptions(cols)` that fills `options` from `options(field)` (one request per value set, not per row), which the page awaits before drawing. Server joins once for relations, one cached lookup per set; costs one new answer key and one new async method.
  - **B** — relations only: `columns()` answers `include` and the generated list passes it; a value-set enum stays the app's job (`column.options` from `resource.options(field)`, which `enumLabel` already honors), documented as a hazard. Smallest change, but the generated list still prints an id for every `@values` column.
  - **C** — `<Cell>` resolves its own labels, given the `resource`: a relation not on the record is read through `resource.options(relation)` and an enum through the same call, cached per resource and column. No change to `list()` or the page, but every `<Cell>` gains a resource prop and an async state, and a table of 50 rows makes the cell a data fetcher rather than a renderer.
  - **Recommend A** — `include` is already the transport's word for "carry the related row" (`FJS-D237`), `columns()` already knows which columns are relations, and `options(field)` is the existing owner of a value set's members; A adds no second place that knows what a label is, where C splits it across the cell.

- ~~**FJS-D1004 — How does a client's tag replace a template's of the same name in `autoImport`: a later directory wins, or an override is declared?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1004`): A — the ordered list `markdownLayouts` already uses: a later `components` directory wins a name, and the conflict stays an error within ONE directory and between a directory and a `modules` binding. No new key and no new noun, and site-kit's `autoImport` already merges engine, preset and site in that order. The cost is that a duplicate across two directories is no longer reported, so an accidental shadow is silent; the build log would have to name each override to keep it visible.** `FJS-1494`: `claim` in `auto-import-plugin.js` throws *Auto-import naming conflict* for a name found twice, so a site cut from a template cannot put its `Hero` over the engine's. The red assertion: `autoImport.components: ['components/UI', 'components/Other']` with a `Button.mesa` in each builds today with a throw (`auto-import.test.js` 'throws on naming conflict between two dirs'); the fix turns that into `autoImportMap.get('Button').from` being `Other/Button.mesa`. The conflict check is also the only thing that catches an ACCIDENTAL duplicate, and a package binding sharing a directory component's name is a different kind of clash (the injected identifier is the same, the sources are not ordered). The page-local tier (`_Hero.md` beside a page) is a second question: a co-located `_x.mesa` is neither routed nor imported, and no config key can spell *this directory, for this subtree*.
  - **A** — the ordered list `markdownLayouts` already uses: a later `components` directory wins a name, and the conflict stays an error within ONE directory and between a directory and a `modules` binding. No new key and no new noun, and site-kit's `autoImport` already merges engine, preset and site in that order. The cost is that a duplicate across two directories is no longer reported, so an accidental shadow is silent; the build log would have to name each override to keep it visible.
  - **B** — an explicit declaration: `autoImport: { components, overrides: ['content/blocks'] }`, where only a directory named there may take a name another already holds and every other duplicate still throws. Keeps the accidental-duplicate guard, but adds a second key that restates an order the `components` array already carries.
  - **C** — A plus the page-local tier: a `_X.md` or `_X.mesa` beside a page is resolved for that page's subtree first, then the ordered list. Covers ksite's whole four-step resolver, but needs the importer to know which file it is injecting into and a scan per routes directory, which is a new scope concept in a plugin whose map is global today.
  - **Recommend A** — `FJS-1493` already ruled the ordered list for layouts and a template's whole job is to have its names replaced, so the same word should mean the same thing in both lists; the page-local tier waits for a client that needs it, since the second client (Luminous Heart) needed neither.

- **FJS-D1005 — Where does the download of a screen's filtered list live: a prop on FilterBar/Table, an `ExportButton` component, or a resource helper?** `FJS-2835`: `exporter`'s `splitQuery` (`packages/junction/src/plugins/export/index.ts:96`) takes a screen's filter keys as-is and refuses any `$` key (Invariant 10), and `client.fetch('/exports/<dataset>?…')` plus `download(blob, name)` (`packages/ui/utils.js`) already carry the session and save the file — what no component does is turn the list's query into that call. The red test's assertion: a mounted list with `{ status: 'open' }` triggers a fetch of `/exports/Order?status=open` and `download` receives its blob; today nothing on FilterBar or Table can be clicked to do either.
  - **A** — a prop: `<FilterBar export="Order" {client}>` (and Table) renders a Download button beside the filters. One tag, but it gives two display components a `client` and a fetch-and-save duty, and the same button can then be on both, so the owner is unclear.
  - **B** — a component: `<ExportButton dataset="Order" query={page.query} {client}>` in `packages/ui`, which drops `$` keys, calls `client.fetch` with `encodeQueryString`, and hands the blob to `download`. FilterBar and Table stay unaware of transport; a screen places the button where it likes.
  - **C** — a resource helper: `resource.export({ query })` in sierra's `createResource`, which already holds the client and the noun (the model name is the dataset), returns the blob; a screen pairs it with `download` on any `<Button>`. No new UI noun, but the UI kit ships no one-click affordance.
  - **Recommend B** — a download is one element with one job, and a Resource's query already names the dataset, so the button takes them as two props; a prop on two display components (A) is the same behavior declared twice, and C leaves every screen to rewrite the same four lines.

- **FJS-D1007 — How does an answer say every signed-in staff member writes a row?** A salon's staff all reschedule every appointment, and no access word gives a write to every signed-in user: `shared` opens the read only, and a write goes to an actor link or, when none holds it, an administrator (`emit.js` `accessFor`). The read half of `FJS-2550` is fixed (`public: ["create"]` now sits beside `shared`); the red test for this half is an answer whose entity says every signed-in user updates a row and whose emitted `@@gate` and `@@allow` let a second signed-in user update it, where today the update is gate 5 and refused.
  - **A** — `shared` takes the ops it covers: `shared: { reason, ops: ['read', 'update'] }`, a bare string staying read-only. One word, the finding already states the reach in a sentence, and the gate level 4 with no `@@allow` is what `shared` already emits for the read.
  - **B** — a new access word, `team: 'reason'` (every signed-in user reads, creates and updates, deletion stays with an author link or an administrator). Names the one-business shape directly; a second word beside `shared` that differs by the ops it opens.
  - **C** — no spelling: the one-business team shape is out of scope, and the `open` question (the builder writing a rule outside the answer) stays the answer. The person's rule lives in the schema's `@@allow`, outside Oracle.
  - **Recommend A** — it extends the word that already means *every signed-in user*, coins no second noun, and the ops it opens are stated and graded as a finding like the read already is.

- ~~**FJS-D1008 — When an app requires a second factor and an account has none, what does the login path do?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-16 (`FJS-D1008`): B — a session limited to enrollment: issue a real session whose row carries `enrollmentOnly`, and `callService` refuses everything but `account.totpStatus/setupTotp/confirmTotp` with a 403 `second_factor_required` until `confirmTotp` clears it. Reuses the existing `account` services and the password re-check, and one owner (junction's method-policy edge) grades it; the cost is a session that exists before the factor does, and a junction change for a package that sits below it (Invariant 1 allows auth to depend on junction, so the hook is auth's own plugin `before` rather than junction's).** `FJS-2554`: `firstFactorProven` (`packages/auth/auth.ts`) issues a session when `liveTotp(user.id)` is empty, and `setupTotp` is a service on `account` that needs a session AND the current password (`FJS-D20`), so refusing the session outright leaves the account no way to enroll. The option itself is settled by the row (`requireSecondFactor: true | { level: n }` on `auth`/`createLitestoneAuth`, enforced in `firstFactorProven`, which every first factor already ends at per `FJS-D888`/`FJS-D915`); what the red test asserts depends on this answer: a login on a required, unenrolled account answers today a plain `{ token, user }`.
  - **A** — an enrollment ticket: `firstFactorProven` answers `{ enroll: ticket, expiresAt }` beside `{ challenge }`, no session issued. `setupTotp`/`confirmTotp` get a ticket-taking twin (a raw route like `/auth/login/challenge`, since it cannot be gated by a session), and `confirmTotp` on a ticket ends in the session. Nothing is signed in until the factor exists, and Sierra's `session` gains one more state beside `awaitingCode`; the cost is a second ticket kind and four more wire answers (password, magic link, OAuth, confirmOAuthLink).
  - **B** — a session limited to enrollment: issue a real session whose row carries `enrollmentOnly`, and `callService` refuses everything but `account.totpStatus/setupTotp/confirmTotp` with a 403 `second_factor_required` until `confirmTotp` clears it. Reuses the existing `account` services and the password re-check, and one owner (junction's method-policy edge) grades it; the cost is a session that exists before the factor does, and a junction change for a package that sits below it (Invariant 1 allows auth to depend on junction, so the hook is auth's own plugin `before` rather than junction's).
  - **C** — refuse and leave enrollment to the operator: login answers 403 `second_factor_required` and an admin enrolls the account out of band (`resetTotp`'s mirror). Smallest build, no new ticket or session kind; an app that turns the switch on locks out every existing unenrolled account until someone acts.
  - **Recommend B** — A needs a second ticket kind and a new route plus a client state across four entry doors, and C locks a live app out by flipping a setting. B keeps one session path and one service owner for enrollment, and the refusal lives in auth's plugin beside the `totp` credential it reads.

- ~~**FJS-D1010 — How does a declared `gate:` grade a method against the workspace `$.id` names rather than the one the `X-Workspace-Id` header names?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-30 (`FJS-D1010`): D — the app's `tenantFrom` (`workspaceOrKeys`) answers `$.id` on the workspaces service, so `membershipClaim` resolves the addressed workspace before `gateAuth` reads a level. `addMember`, `setMemberRole`, `removeMember`, `push` and `testPing` declare `gate: 5`, `patch`/`remove` fall to `Workspace`'s `@@gate(1.1.5.6)`, and `restandingFor` and the hook go. No junction change and no client rename; the cost is one branch on a service name in the resolver. Built and proved red-then-green by `services.test.ts` (an admin of the session's workspace, a viewer of the id's: 403 with the gate's sentence).** `workspaces.addMember`, `setMemberRole`, `removeMember` and `transferOwnership` (FJS-2858) address `$.id`, but `gateAuth` reads the level off the caller's client before the service's `before.all` runs `restandingFor`, so a `gate:` would grade an admin of the workspace on screen against a patch of any other. Today `requireWorkspaceRole()` is the only grader (`core/hooks.ts` `requireWorkspaceRole`), a second ladder beside the gate's; the red test is an admin of A, header A, calling `setMemberRole` on workspace B where they are a viewer, and it must be 403 by the gate's own message rather than the hook's.
  - **A** — junction resolves the level for the addressed workspace: a service declares which `$`-param names the tenant (`gateOn: 'id'`), and `gateAuth` re-reads the standing for it before grading. Fixes every service that addresses a tenant other than the header's and deletes `requireWorkspaceRole` from the four, but adds an option to junction and a second path into the claim resolver (Invariant 4).
  - **B** — split the four into a `workspaceMembers` service keyed by `workspaceId` in the header. The header then names the addressed workspace, so the declared gate is correct with no junction change; the client sends the header it already sends. Costs a service and a client-call rename (`workspaces.setMemberRole` → `workspaceMembers.setRole`), and `leave` stays behind because it addresses the caller's own row.
  - **C** — leave `requireWorkspaceRole()` on the four and close the row as accepted: the hook is correct after `restandingFor`, and the cost is the second ladder the error mapper does not translate.
  - **D** — the app's `tenantFrom` (`workspaceOrKeys`) answers `$.id` on the workspaces service, so `membershipClaim` resolves the addressed workspace before `gateAuth` reads a level. `addMember`, `setMemberRole`, `removeMember`, `push` and `testPing` declare `gate: 5`, `patch`/`remove` fall to `Workspace`'s `@@gate(1.1.5.6)`, and `restandingFor` and the hook go. No junction change and no client rename; the cost is one branch on a service name in the resolver. Built and proved red-then-green by `services.test.ts` (an admin of the session's workspace, a viewer of the id's: 403 with the gate's sentence).
  - **Recommend B** — the header is already the app's one statement of which workspace a call is about, so a method that wants a different one is a different resource; B uses the gate as it is and adds nothing to junction.

- ~~**FJS-D1011 — How does `providerOnly()` tell an account a provider made from one the mailbox made and later linked a provider to?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-16 (`FJS-D1011`): A — record the origin on the credential: § 4 writes the linked `oauth:*` row with a marker (a nullable `Credential.linked` flag in `auth.lite`), and `providerOnly` and `confirmPasswordReset` count only an unmarked `oauth:*` credential, so the account § 3 made stays refused and the account the mailbox already vouched for keeps both doors. One schema column, one helper both callers share.** `providerOnly` (`packages/auth/auth.ts`) is `confirmPasswordReset`'s test: no `password` credential and some `oauth:*` one. A magic-link account holds no credential, so `oauthResolve` § 4 links the provider (the account was `emailVerified`, which `linkable` requires) and the account is then refused magic-link sign-in at the request and at the click. The red test: create a user with `verifyMagicLink`, `oauthResolve('github', verified identity for the same address)`, then `requestMagicLink` on that address — it should mail and today it audits `no-password-credential` and mails nothing. An invited account that links a provider before its first password hits the same test in `confirmPasswordReset`. Nothing stored today records whether the mailbox or the provider created the account.
  - **A** — record the origin on the credential: § 4 writes the linked `oauth:*` row with a marker (a nullable `Credential.linked` flag in `auth.lite`), and `providerOnly` and `confirmPasswordReset` count only an unmarked `oauth:*` credential, so the account § 3 made stays refused and the account the mailbox already vouched for keeps both doors. One schema column, one helper both callers share.
  - **B** — infer it with no new state: a provider-made account's `oauth:*` credential is written in the registration transaction, so treat a credential created later than its `User` row as linked. No column, but it rests on two `now()` defaults agreeing, and a registration that retries or a backfill breaks the inference silently.
  - **C** — leave the refusal and state it: an account that links a provider loses magic-link sign-in and password reset by design, and the person keeps the provider. The cost is the FJS-2545 lockout stays, for the one account (magic-link-made) that was never secured by anything but its mailbox.
  - **Recommend A** — the distinction is a fact about how the credential arrived, so it is stored where the credential is, and B reads it off a coincidence of timestamps.

- **FJS-D1012 — Does a dispatch into a RUNNING `unique` key queue one follow-up (changing what `unique` means, or behind a new option), or stay dropped as documented?** `findByUniqueKey` and the `jobs_unique_live` partial index both cover `status IN ('pending','running')` (`packages/caravan/src/db.ts:126,781`), so `dispatch` (`src/index.ts:757`) returns the running job's id and a recompute asked for after the running one read its inputs is lost (ELA `crew-budget`, FJS-2118). The red test: dispatch `unique: 'k'`, claim it so it is `running`, dispatch `unique: 'k'` again — today returns the running id and the table holds one row; the row wants a second `pending` row, and a third dispatch absorbed into it. README and `CLAUDE.md` state `unique` as "only one of these at a time", so a follow-up that a second worker could claim while the first runs breaks the lock apps rely on (`deploy:1.2.3`).
  - **A** — change `unique` itself to coalesce-while-pending: the index covers `pending` only, a dispatch into a running key inserts one pending follow-up, and the claim query skips a pending job whose key is running, so same-key work still never overlaps. Cost: the meaning of `unique` moves from "no second queued-or-running" to "no second queued", and a caller that wanted the drop-while-running behavior loses it.
  - **B** — keep `unique` as the lock and add `DispatchOptions.coalesce: true` (with `unique`) that does A's behavior for that key only. Cost: one more option on `dispatch`, and the index must still cover both states for plain `unique`, so the follow-up row needs a distinct key form or a second index.
  - **C** — leave it: record in `api-hazards` that `unique` drops into a running key, and the app uses a time-bucketed key or no `unique` (the row's stated outs). Cost: the framework never says the recompute shape.
  - **Recommend A** — "a lock on work in flight" and "queue the recompute asked for after the read" are the same intent seen from the two ends of one job, and the claim skip keeps the no-overlap guarantee; B grows an option for a distinction the index can carry.

- **FJS-D1013 — Which claim does Oracle's emitter write into an update policy so the app's system principal can make a `@system` move on an owner-only model?** `accessFor` ([emit.js](packages/oracle/src/emit.js)) emits `update` as `hostId == auth().id` for an owner link, so `Booking.complete: … @system` is a move `createApp({ system })` cannot make: the fire finds no row (`FJS-1788`'s throw names it at runtime, never at emit). The red test: emit an answer whose entity has an owner link and a lifecycle move `by: 'system'`; its `update` policy today has no term but the owner's. Any fix widens who may write the row, so the claim is the choice.
  - **A** — `auth().level >= 7` appended to `update` on a model holding a `by: 'system'` move: SYSADMIN is the one level the framework itself names (`gate.js`), and `FJS-D296` plus orion's `level >= 5` reads are the precedent. Cost: every SYSADMIN, not only the system principal, may then update those rows by plain `update`, past the owner-only rule; and an app whose `getLevel` grades its system principal lower stays silent-broken.
  - **B** — a dedicated claim, `auth().isSystem`, that the app's `SYSTEM` principal states and nothing else carries. Narrowest: exactly the principal that makes the move. Cost: a new noun every app must put on its principal, and a missing claim is the same silent no-row, so it needs the `fli check` half (`FJS-1884`) to be safe.
  - **C** — emit nothing and have the emitter refuse (or `checkAnswer` name) a `by: 'system'` move on an owner-only entity until the answer states who else may update it, so the person approves the widening in the answer rather than the emitter inventing it.
  - **Recommend B** — the move is the application's and `@system` already says so; naming the one principal that makes it widens nothing for a human SYSADMIN, and `FJS-1884`'s check is the same claim's other half.

- ~~**FJS-D1014 — Does a read method's declared `input:` validate its query, or does every method that takes input read it from the body only?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1014`): A — on a `read: true` method, `validateInput` validates the query (its `reservedQuery` keys included) merged under the body, coercing its strings through `coerceToSchema`, the owner that already does it for a form, so one declaration covers both carriages. Cost: `validateInput` learns which carriage a read uses, and a key named in both is one more precedence to state.** `validateInput` ([litestone.ts](packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts)) reads `ctx.data` alone and refuses a missing one with *Request body is required*, so a basecamp method that reads `$.query.x ?? $.data.x` cannot declare `input:` without refusing its GET form: `servers.reconcile`/`catalog`/`localSshProbe`/`metrics`, `metrics.*`, `edge.*`, `posture`/`cleanup` `server_id`, `flags.resolve`, `workspaces.removeMember` and `git.localRepos` (`reservedQuery: ['root', 'depth']`) each check the shape by hand (FJS-2865). The red test: declare `{ method: 'localRepos', read: true, input: 'GitLocalRepos' }` and call it as `GET /git?depth=9` — today a 400 for the missing body, where the row wants *depth* named.
  - **A** — on a `read: true` method, `validateInput` validates the query (its `reservedQuery` keys included) merged under the body, coercing its strings through `coerceToSchema`, the owner that already does it for a form, so one declaration covers both carriages. Cost: `validateInput` learns which carriage a read uses, and a key named in both is one more precedence to state.
  - **B** — a method with `input:` reads the body only, and these methods drop the query form: the client sends a read's input as the body of a `POST` with `X-Service-Method`, as `flags.resolve`'s own comment already describes. Cost: every caller of the GET form moves, and a read loses its cacheable URL.
  - **Recommend A** — a read is a GET and its input is its URL; B turns every parameterized read into a POST to fit the validator, where A keeps one declaration for both forms.

- **FJS-D1016 — Which directories does a workspace walk count as an app when there is no schema: any manifest plus a surface dir, or only one whose surface holds its own config?** `findApps` ([runnables.js](packages/cli/core/runnables.js)) demands `db/schema.lite`; `checks.js`'s `isAppRoot` (FJS-1617) accepts a manifest plus any `SURFACES` dir. Red test: a tree `ws/apps/webonly` holding `package.json` + `web/src/routes/index.mesa` — `findApps(ws)` is `[]` today, should list it. Probed with `findApps` switched to `isAppRoot` over this repo: it adds `website` (private, a real `site/` — 8 errors, 1445 findings), `packages/orion` (a `web/` dir; 0 errors) and `packages/cli` (a `web/` that is the GUI's `index.html` served by `server.js`, no Sierra config; 95 errors), so CI's `structure` phase, which runs `findApps(ROOT)`, goes red on a library that is not an app.
  - **A** — `findApps` = `isAppRoot` as is (moved to runnables.js beside it, one definition), and the three directories take allowances in `scripts/ci-allowances.json` or are fixed (website's 8 errors). Cost: `packages/cli` is a library wearing an app's rules, and every library with a `web/` dir meets the same.
  - **B** — tighten `isAppRoot` itself: a surface counts only when it holds its own build config (`sierra.config.js`, `vite.config.*`, `desktop.config.js`, `manifest.json`), the file `surface-config` already looks for. Excludes `packages/cli` and `packages/orion`; keeps `website`. Cost: a surface directory with no config is no longer an app, so `app-layout`'s "a config is missing" finding stops firing for the web-only case it was written for.
  - **C** — A, and exclude workspace members that are published (a non-`private` `package.json` under `packages/`) unless they carry a schema. Cost: a second, manifest-derived sign beside the layout one.
  - **Recommend B** — Invariant 3 defines a surface by what it builds, and a directory with `index.html` and no build config is not one; the same predicate then answers both the single-root rules and the walk.

- **FJS-D1021 — Where does a per-queue rate limit live: a window counted over the jobs rows, a claim ledger table, or nowhere shared?** `QueueConfig` has `concurrency` and nothing that bounds starts per window, so a queue in front of a rate-limited provider (`FJS-1522`) can only be throttled per replica, and N replicas multiply the limit. The claim (`claimNext`, [db.ts](packages/caravan/src/db.ts)) is already the one atomic place that decides a job starts, and `jobs.started_at` is overwritten on every claim. Red test: a `rateLimit: { max: 2, perMs: 1000 }` queue with 5 pending jobs and two `caravan` instances on one `jobs.db` starts at most 2 in the first second; today it starts all 5.
  - **A** — `rateLimit: { max, perMs }` on `QueueConfig`, enforced in the claim's `WHERE` by counting `jobs` rows of that queue with `started_at > now - perMs`. No new state, shared by every replica through `jobs.db`. Cost: it counts each job once at its LATEST attempt (a retry overwrites `started_at`), and `cleanupAfter` shorter than `perMs` forgets finished jobs, so a retry storm against the provider is undercounted.
  - **B** — the same option, enforced by a `queue_claims(queue, at)` row appended inside the claim's transaction and pruned past `perMs`. Exact, every attempt counts, shared through `jobs.db`. Cost: one more table the claim writes to, and an index.
  - **C** — a per-process token bucket on `QueueWorker`, no schema. Cost: it is the per-replica workaround the row says is incorrect; N replicas run at N times the limit.
  - **Recommend B** — a retry is a call to the provider and the provider counts it, so A's undercount defeats the option's one purpose; C does not answer the row.

- ~~**FJS-D1022 — `FJS-D812` runs a commitment hook before its move; example's renewal then opens the next period while the closing one still holds `@@unique([subscriptionId], where: status == 'open')` — amend the ruling, or the app's constraint?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-16 (`FJS-D1022`): E — the service pipeline's three tiers, in its own order (`around enter → before → move → after → around exit`, `packages/junction/src/core/hooks.ts`): `before`, `after` and `around` (`(ctx, next)`, `next()` the move), all in the move's transaction. Spelled as one spec object per commitment, which is `FJS-D1077`'s `defineX(spec)`: `defineCommitment({ before, after })`. Lago's seal is `before`, example's renewal `after`. Added 2026-10-16.** Built as ruled ([commitments/index.ts](packages/junction/src/plugins/commitments/index.ts) `fireCommitment`: read the row at its from-state, run the hook, then `transition()` in the same transaction), the junction suite passed with a `@seals` red test turning green — the hook numbered the draft and found it by `status: 'draft'` — but example `verify:jobs` failed `renewal.periodCloses` with `fire: 'dead'`, *SubscriptionPeriod: subscriptionId 5 is already taken*. `renewPeriod` writes nothing the move reads, but it CREATES a row that the move's own from-state contends with, so D812's *renewPeriod is unaffected* is wrong. The fix is reverted, and FJS-2163 waits on this.
  - **A** — keep D812 (one hook, one order) and respell example's guard as `@@unique([subscriptionId, startsOn])`, the shape lago already uses against a second draft (`@@unique([subscriptionId, periodStart], …)`). A duplicate renewal is still refused. Cost: *at most one open period per subscription* is no longer enforced, only *one period per start*.
  - **B** — D812's rejected B: two keyed tiers, `before` and `after`, both in the move's transaction. Renewal stays `after` and a sealing document's owed writes go `before`. Cost: two spellings of one hook, but there is now a current hook that needs each.
  - **C** — the hook runs before the move, and the plugin hands it `move()`, so a hook that has to write after the move calls `await move()` first and the plugin makes the move itself if the hook did not. The from-state lock and the one-hook spelling both stay. Cost: the order lives in each hook's body rather than in the plugin.
  - **E** — the service pipeline's three tiers, in its own order (`around enter → before → move → after → around exit`, `packages/junction/src/core/hooks.ts`): `before`, `after` and `around` (`(ctx, next)`, `next()` the move), all in the move's transaction. Spelled as one spec object per commitment, which is `FJS-D1077`'s `defineX(spec)`: `defineCommitment({ before, after })`. Lago's seal is `before`, example's renewal `after`. Added 2026-10-16.
  - **Recommend C** — renewal and lago's seal each get the order they need under one key, and the plugin still owns the move and its lock. A trades away a real invariant to keep the ordering simple, and B adds a second option for what C does in one call.

- ~~**FJS-D1141 — Should a commitment hook be a file named for its move, as a job and a notification are?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-16 (`FJS-D1141`): C — both, feeding one registry, as caravan has `*.job.ts` beside `handle()`/`schedule()`. The paved road is any `*.commitments.ts` under the app root, so a hook lives in its domain folder (`domain/billing/renewal.commitments.ts`); it `export default`s a map `{ 'Model.move': defineCommitment({ … }) }`, because one domain file spans models (lago's renewal holds `Subscription.activate` and `SubscriptionPeriod.close`). `commitments({ hooks })` stays as the escape hatch, the path a drive with no app (`verify:billing` through `fireCommitment`) takes. A key claimed twice, by two files or by a file and the map, is refused by name, and `refuseUnknownHooks` checks both sources at boot. Jobs and notifications follow: `*.job.ts` and `*.notification.ts` are found anywhere under the root too, so one discovery rule covers every file kind. Cost: the key is a string again, caught at boot rather than derived; and the scan widens from `jobs/` to the root.** Today a hook is a key in the map an app hands `commitments({ hooks })`: example writes it in `api/src/core/commitments.ts` (`'SubscriptionPeriod.close'`), lago spreads `invoiceHooks` and `subscriptionHooks` from its domain files. A job is `<name>.job.ts` and a notification `OrderPaid.notification.ts`, stamped by the loader from the filename.
  - **A** — `commitments/SubscriptionPeriod.close.commitment.ts` under the app root (`FJS-D1080`), `export default defineCommitment({ … })` per `FJS-D1077`; the loader stamps the key from the filename and boot refuses one naming no `@@commitment` (`refuseUnknownHooks` already does for the map). The `hooks:` option goes.
  - **B** — keep the map; an app groups hooks as it likes.
  - **C** — both, feeding one registry, as caravan has `*.job.ts` beside `handle()`/`schedule()`. The paved road is any `*.commitments.ts` under the app root, so a hook lives in its domain folder (`domain/billing/renewal.commitments.ts`); it `export default`s a map `{ 'Model.move': defineCommitment({ … }) }`, because one domain file spans models (lago's renewal holds `Subscription.activate` and `SubscriptionPeriod.close`). `commitments({ hooks })` stays as the escape hatch, the path a drive with no app (`verify:billing` through `fireCommitment`) takes. A key claimed twice, by two files or by a file and the map, is refused by name, and `refuseUnknownHooks` checks both sources at boot. Jobs and notifications follow: `*.job.ts` and `*.notification.ts` are found anywhere under the root too, so one discovery rule covers every file kind. Cost: the key is a string again, caught at boot rather than derived; and the scan widens from `jobs/` to the root.
  - **Recommend A** — knowing where a job lives teaches where a hook lives (§ V *predictability*), and the key, a string a typo breaks, is derived from a filename the loader checks. Cost: lago's hooks leave their domain files, each for a two-line file that calls into them.

- **FJS-D1024 — What does `dndzone` grow for ELA's inbox: drop ONTO an item (merge), a foreign item that lands as something else (promote, with a placeholder), and a multi-item drag?** Found fixing `FJS-2004`. Today `hitTest` ([dnd.js](packages/ui/dnd.js)) answers one question — which insertion INDEX in which zone — and `finishDrag` hands `onfinalize` the zone's whole list with the dragged item spliced in at `placeholderIndex`; the dragged item is always one record of the zone's own `items`. Each of the three gestures breaks one of those: a drop onto an item has no index (it names an item), a foreign item that becomes a ticket is not an element of the target's `items` (the placeholder must stand for a record the host builds), and a selection is N records leaving the origin at once. The red test would be a drop with the pointer over the middle of a card, asserting an `onfinalize` that names that card as the target; nothing today can say it. `FJS-D671`'s `createSelection(keyOf)` is the selection ELA drags, so the multi-drag half has a source already.
  - **A** — options on `dndzone`, one session: `dropOnto: true | (item) => bool` makes the middle band of each item a target (edges keep inserting) and finalizes with trigger `droppedOntoItem` and `info.target` = that item, the list unchanged; `accept: (dragged) => record | null` on the receiving zone turns a foreign item into the record the host wants, shown in the placeholder; `dragSet: () => items[]` on the source (the host passes `selection`'s rows) makes the ghost a stack and the finalize carry `info.items`. Three options on one attachment, one hit-test, one set of triggers. Cost: `dndzone` is already 262 lines and `configure` 91; the three interact (a stack dropped onto an item), and every zone pays for options only the inbox uses.
  - **B** — ship only the two that need the hit-test: `dropOnto` and multi-item `dragSet`; leave the foreign-item placeholder to the host, which already receives `onconsider` with a foreign item in the list and can render the placeholder (`isDndShadowItem`) as a ticket card itself. That is the contract today, so nothing is added for it. Cost: the host maps a foreign record to its landing shape in its own `{#each}`, once per screen.
  - **C** — nothing in `dnd.js`: the inbox draws the merge target as its own pointer-band drop target (`data-dnd-target` is already styled by the app) and `dndzone` stays a flow-list reorderer, the contract its header states. Cost: ELA reimplements pointer capture, ghost, touch arming and auto-scroll for the one gesture `dnd.js` already owns, and keyboard drag (`FJS-1323`) would not reach it.
  - **Recommend B** — `dropOnto` and `dragSet` are the two things only the zone's hit-test and ghost can do, while the foreign-item placeholder already works through `onconsider` plus the shadow item, so adding `accept` would restate a mechanism the host holds. Ship `dropOnto` first, since the merge gesture is the one nothing else can reach.

- **FJS-D1025 — `FJS-D1006` ruled a role-limited move as `by <role>` beside `by: 'system'`, but Litestone's `@@transitions` carries only `@gate`, `@system` and `@seals` per move, and an update policy is a WHERE over the PRE-state row that cannot tell a move from a plain write. What `.lite` does the emitter write for `finalize: open -> final by finance`?** Found fixing `FJS-2761`. Red test: an answer whose Membership `role` is `admin|finance|support`, Invoice moves `finalize` and `void` `by: 'finance'`; a `support` member's `invoice.transition(id, 'finalize')` is expected refused, and today `checkAnswer` refuses `by: 'finance'` outright (`answer.js:479`) and the emitted update is every member's. The plain-write half (`roles: { update, delete }` on any entity access, `FJS-D623`'s key generalised) needs no ruling and ships with whichever of these is picked.
  - **A** — Litestone grows a per-move row predicate in the policy grammar, `finalize: open -> final @allow(org.members.some(userId == auth().id && role == 'finance'))`, graded on the pre-state row beside the move's `@gate`; Oracle emits it from `by`. Exact, and a move refused names itself (`TransitionGateError`). Cost: a fourth per-move attribute and a parser, client and `x-transitions` change in litestone, for one consumer.
  - **B** — No litestone change: `by: <role>` narrows the entity's update policy on the move's FROM-states (`status != 'open' || <role term>`). Cost: it also bars the other roles from plain edits of a row in that state, so 'any member edits a draft, only a manager submits' has no spelling, and the refusal is a silent empty update.
  - **C** — `by: <role>` is refused with a finding that sends the move to the app (`open` item, a hand-written `@gate`), and only the plain-write `roles` ships. Today's behavior for moves; Charter's People step collects roles for writes but not for moves.
  - **Recommend A** — a move's role is a question about a row and a caller, which is what a policy already answers, and B can only get there by over-restricting. The attribute is the same shape as `@gate` on the move, and the client already renders `transitions(row)` from the gate's verdict, so a UI would hide the button a role cannot press with no new path.

- **FJS-D1026 — How does a keyboard drag in `dndzone` cross into another zone, and how does a host word its announcements?** Found fixing `FJS-2659`. `startKeyboardDrag` ([dnd.js](packages/ui/dnd.js)) measures only the origin zone into `session.zones`, and `keyboardKey` moves `meta.placeholderIndex` along `meta.axis` inside `session.current`, so a roster that assigns by dragging into another zone has no keyboard path; `announce()` and `where()` build English literals with no seam. Both halves add a surface to the zone's options or keys, so neither is derivable from the tree.
  - **A** — Cross: the arrows PERPENDICULAR to the zone's axis hop to the nearest zone of the same type in that direction (by measured rect), landing at the index nearest the item's current position; no new key. Wording: one option `announcements: (event, info) => string | undefined` where `event` is `lifted | moved | dropped | cancelled` and `info` is `{ label, position, total, zone }`; `undefined` falls back to the English default. Cost: a wrapped or grid zone already reads by line, and perpendicular arrows there are ambiguous with line moves.
  - **B** — Cross: a modifier on the axis arrows (Shift+Arrow along the axis hops to the previous/next zone of the type in registry order). Unambiguous in wrapped zones and needs no geometry, but the key is undiscoverable and the order is the DOM's, not the screen's. Wording: an `announcements` object of one function per event, each returning a string — four options to state instead of one.
  - **C** — Cross: a drop-target cycle on `[` / `]` (or PageUp/PageDown), announced on lift; geometry-free like B, no collision with arrows. Wording: a `messages` object of template strings (`'{label}, lifted, {position} of {total}'`). Cost: a template syntax is a second mini-language, and a language with plural or word-order rules needs a function anyway.
  - **Recommend A** — a kanban's columns sit across the axis of its lists, so the perpendicular arrow is what a sighted user already expects, and one `announcements` function is the single seam both English and another language need; the wrapped-zone ambiguity is answered by taking the perpendicular arrow as a zone hop only when no item lies that way within the current zone.

- **FJS-D1027 — Which copy survives the one-hop bound: convert `Order`/`PaymentMethod`/`Subscription`/`Invoice` to `customer.userId` and trade the grandchildren to `customerId`, keep every `userId` copy, or lift the bound to two hops?** Found fixing `FJS-915`. Nine copies, not seven: `Order`, `PaymentMethod`, `Subscription`, `Invoice` hang off `Customer` (one hop to `customer.userId`); `OrderLine`, `Payment`, `SubscriptionPeriod`, `InvoiceLine`, `CreditNote` hang off those (`order.customer.userId` is two hops, a parse error under `FJS-D221`). Converting a parent makes its child need a copy of `customerId` instead, so the copy is traded rather than removed. Behavior moves too: `Order.userId` is stamped at sale and stays null for a guest sale on purpose (the `Order.userId` comment), whereas `customer.userId` makes an account registered later read the customer's old guest orders. Every model is read by `verify:account`, `verify:cart`, `verify:money` and `verify:billing`, so a conversion is five-drive work.
  - **A** — Convert the four direct children to `customer.userId` (four copies gone outright); each grandchild keeps ONE copy, of `customerId` (an immutable relation key that cannot drift when an account is linked) in place of `userId`. Accepts the late-registration read of old guest orders and says so in the `Order` comment.
  - **B** — Keep all nine `userId` copies; rewrite the nine comments to cite the bound as measured and make `carts.checkout`/billing's one transaction the stated owner. Zero behavior change, and `FJS-915` closes as a measurement.
  - **C** — Lift `FJS-D221` to two hops in Litestone's parser and compile (an `EXISTS` chain), then delete every copy. Removes all nine, and costs a parser/compiler change plus a re-ruling of a bound that was deliberate.
  - **Recommend A** — it removes the four copies a single hop can remove, shrinks the survivors from a mutable account id to an immutable key, and leaves the bound alone; C is the right next ruling only if a second app also pays for two hops.

- ~~**FJS-D1028 — Should `@frontierjs` packages ship `.d.ts` beside their `.ts` source, so an app's `tsc` and editor stop compiling the framework?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1028`): B — Stay source-only; leave `fli typecheck`'s output filter as the answer and record the per-run compile cost as accepted. Zero new artifacts; the cost scales with framework size, and an editor still compiles the framework on open.** Found fixing `FJS-268`. Output is clean now (junction 0 errors, every other `.ts` package already clean), so no red test: what remains is cost. Every `exports` map points at a `.ts`, tsc follows it and re-checks the framework in each app's program and editor, and `fli typecheck` ([typecheck.js](packages/cli/core/typecheck.js)) filters the output rather than skipping the work. `skipLibCheck` covers `.d.ts` only.
  - **A** — Ship generated `.d.ts` per package (`tsc --emitDeclarationOnly` at publish, `types` condition in `exports` ahead of the `.ts`). Apps and editors read declarations and skip the compile; `skipLibCheck` then covers the framework. Cost: a second artifact per package, a build step at publish, and a stale one is worse than none unless a check fails when it drifts (the `exports.snapshot.md` `snapshots` phase is the shape).
  - **B** — Stay source-only; leave `fli typecheck`'s output filter as the answer and record the per-run compile cost as accepted. Zero new artifacts; the cost scales with framework size, and an editor still compiles the framework on open.
  - **C** — Ship declarations for the hot packages only (junction, litestone, mesa) where the compile cost is, leave the small ones as source. Less machinery, but two shapes of package and a line to draw.
  - **Recommend B** — nothing is wrong today and pre-alpha has no consumer paying the cost; A is right when `fli typecheck` time or editor lag is measured on a real app, and A's drift check should be built with it. Revisit then.

- ~~**FJS-D1029 — Does a Release's configuration set get APPLIED to the target (`fli` writes the env file), or does the Release say it is unmanaged and hash only what it can see?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1029`): E — `06-swap` passes the plain `configuration` values to `docker run` as `--env K=V` after `--env-file`, so they win (Kamal's `env.clear`). No file is written on the target, so `FJS-D1186` holds as written. `secrets` stay in the operator's env file, checked present by key in `01b-env-check`, and that step warns on a key set in both places. The hash names what the process runs on, and a revert restarts with the old Release's values. Cost: the values show in `docker inspect`, which is why secrets never go this way.** Found fixing `FJS-585`. `configurationSet(deployConf, target)` ([release.js](packages/cli/core/release.js)) hashes `deploy.configuration` + `deploy.secrets` into the Release id and journals them, but the container starts with `--env-file .env.production` that `fli` reads (`01b-env-check`) and mounts and never writes — so editing a `configuration:` block mints a new Release, bumps the generation, and changes nothing the process runs on. `deploy:revert` already refuses once the generation has moved for the same reason. The red test would be: deploy with `configuration: { A: '1' }`, then read the target env file — `A` is absent.
  - **A** — `fli` writes the env file from the configuration set at the swap step: values verbatim, secret refs resolved (pinned `name@3`) at deploy time into the file. The pair (Release, Generation) becomes real and `deploy:revert` can restore it. Cost: secret RESOLUTION lands on the critical path (a resolver per backend, a deploy that fails when one is unreachable), and `.env.production` becomes generated, so hand-edited keys on the machine need a merge rule or are overwritten.
  - **B** — The Release records that its configuration is unmanaged: the hash covers only what `fli` can see (the declared KEYS and the file's key names, never values), `formatRelease` drops `(declared)` for `(unmanaged)`, and `configuration:`/`secrets:` values leave the Release id. Cost: a Release no longer identifies values, so `deploy:revert` of the pair stays impossible by design and invariant 2 of `IDEAS/release-transitions.md` is reworded to the code alone.
  - **C** — A but values only: `fli` writes `configuration` (plain values) into a managed block of the env file and `secrets` stay on the machine, checked present by `01b-env-check` against the declared keys. The pair is real for everything a Release can carry without resolution; secrets are named and graded, never applied.
  - **E** — `06-swap` passes the plain `configuration` values to `docker run` as `--env K=V` after `--env-file`, so they win (Kamal's `env.clear`). No file is written on the target, so `FJS-D1186` holds as written. `secrets` stay in the operator's env file, checked present by key in `01b-env-check`, and that step warns on a key set in both places. The hash names what the process runs on, and a revert restarts with the old Release's values. Cost: the values show in `docker inspect`, which is why secrets never go this way.
  - **Recommend C** — it makes the hash a fact about the deployment for the part that has no resolution problem and keeps secret handling off the critical path, which is the half `FJS-D158` already grades by `process.env`; A is the follow-on once a secret backend exists.

- ~~**FJS-D1030 — What does a form control's `onchange` hand back: one shape for all, or a rule that sorts them (native control = DOM event, composite = value)?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1030`): C — Two shapes with a stated rule: a control that IS a native element (`Input`, `Textarea`, `Select`, `CodeInput`) hands the DOM event; every composite hands its value. `Slider`/`NumberInput` drop `{ value }` for a bare number (a range stays `[start, end]`), so four shapes become two and the split follows what the control is. Each prop comment and the AGENTS table state the rule.** Found fixing `FJS-2491`. Today `Input`/`Textarea`/`Select`/`CodeInput` forward the native event, `Checkbox`/`Switch` pass `checked`, `RadioGroup`/`Combobox`/`MultiSelect` the value, `Slider`/`NumberInput` `{ value }` or `{ start, end }`; `packages/ui/AGENTS.md` § Forms already tabulates them, so the red test is a handler written for one control run against its neighbour: `<Checkbox onchange={(e) => e.target.checked} />` throws on click. About 57 `.mesa` files pass `onchange={…}`, so any change moves them all in one commit.
  - **A** — Every control's `onchange` receives the VALUE (`target.value`/`checked`/number/array/`[start, end]`), the event dropped. One handler shape; but `Input`'s event is what `e.target.files`, `e.target.form` and `FormField` read, and `oninput` would still be an event or need the same change.
  - **B** — Every control's `onchange` receives `{ value, event }` (event absent for composites with no DOM event). One shape and nothing lost, at the price of every consumer destructuring, including the `Checkbox` that is `(on) => …` in AGENTS § Tables today.
  - **C** — Two shapes with a stated rule: a control that IS a native element (`Input`, `Textarea`, `Select`, `CodeInput`) hands the DOM event; every composite hands its value. `Slider`/`NumberInput` drop `{ value }` for a bare number (a range stays `[start, end]`), so four shapes become two and the split follows what the control is. Each prop comment and the AGENTS table state the rule.
  - **D** — Change no code: put one line above each `onchange` prop comment and the AGENTS table naming the neighbours' shapes. Cheapest; a handler still fails on the neighbour, which is the row's complaint.
  - **Recommend C** — the split follows something a reader can see (is it a native element), it removes the only two shapes with no justification (`{ value }` object around one number, and `Checkbox` boolean vs Slider object), and it keeps `Input`'s event for the callers that need `files` and `form`; A and B reopen the 57 call sites for a gain C mostly gets.

- **FJS-D1031 — How does a method answer in frames through the pipeline: what spells the declaration, and does a service context name its connection?** Found fixing `FJS-1420`. `FJS-D853` ruled that a streaming method DECLARES itself and a non-streaming transport buffers, but nothing in the tree implements it: `refuseStream` (`core/envelope.ts:234`) still refuses every stream, `CustomMethodFn` (`core/service.ts:1273`) has no word for "answers in parts", and `ServiceContext` carries a session and no connection, so an after-return frame is `app.channel(x).send` to a PERSON (the notion `pages.enter` joins both of a person's tabs). The red test is a declared method that yields three frames: today the call is refused by name, and the frames reach no store. `IDEAS/chat-surface.md` Part 1 has the behavior (hooks and gate before the first frame, each frame through `protect()` and the after-hooks, `ctx.sse()` under HTTP and the `event` frame under WS, one client call shape yielding chunks); what is open is the spelling and the second half of the row.
  - **A** — Frames only. `methods: { search: { stream: true, input, gate } }`, the method an async generator, each yielded value a frame through `protect()`; the client's `invoke()` returns an async iterable that resolves to the last frame. Frames go over the calling connection by construction (the transport owns it), so a service context needs no connection. Closes the Portal search shape and the chat answer; leaves notion's `pages.enter` (join ONE connection, not the session's) open as its own row.
  - **B** — A, plus `ctx.connection` (an id on the service context for HTTP-with-socket and WS calls) so `app.channel(x).join(ctx.connection)` and a late `send` can address one connection. Closes the notion half too; adds a noun and a way to learn the id on an HTTP call that has no socket (undefined, which every caller then handles).
  - **C** — Only `ctx.connection`, no streaming declaration: a method returns once and sends later frames to its own connection, ungraded as today. Smallest, but the frames skip `protect()` and the pipeline, which is the row's complaint, and `FJS-D853` is left unimplemented.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the shape `FJS-D853` and chat-surface Part 1 already ruled, it puts every frame through the pipeline rather than around it, and the connection half is a separate defect with a different fix that can follow once frames exist.

- **FJS-D1032 — How does a button list learn the grid — what the caller HOLDS — so `resource.transitions` grades `@@capabilities` (and the post-update policy)?** Found fixing `FJS-1508`. `transitionsAt` (`sierra/src/resource/field-rules.js`) has the move→capability names already (`x-capabilities.moves`, emitted by `jsonschema.js`) but not the caller's held set, and no browser path carries it: a staff `session.user` may hold `capabilities` (`sessionFields`), a `principal`-resolver caller's set is read per request and never on `session.user`, and a bearer's is on a grant row behind an httpOnly cookie. Litestone's own `db.<model>.transitions(row)` (`client.js:9304`) grades gate, `@system` and policy and not the grid either. The red test: `createResource` over a schema with `x-capabilities.moves: { withdraw: 'Application.withdraw' }` → `transitions({ stage: 'applied' }, 0)` marks `reject` `allowed: true` where the server answers 403.
  - **A** — Sierra only: `resource.transitions(row, level, held)`, `held` an array/Set of capability names, and `transitionsAt` refuses a move whose `x-capabilities.moves[name]` is not in it (`refusedBy: 'capability'`). Absent `held` stays permissive. The screen supplies the set, so staff on `sessionFields` works and the per-request `principal` and the bearer do not; the policy half stays ungraded.
  - **B** — A, plus the server says the held set per load: Junction answers `$capabilitiesFor(request principal)` (a built-in method or the session payload) and `createResource` keeps it, so `transitions(row, level)` grades the grid with no third argument for all three callers. New wire surface, one owner (litestone's `$capabilitiesFor`); the policy half stays ungraded.
  - **C** — Ask the SERVER for the list: litestone's `transitions()` also grades the grid (it holds the principal's capabilities), Junction exposes it as a call, and `resource.transitions` stays the permissive gate-only affordance for the cases a round trip is wrong. Closes the grid AND the post-update policy half (Rita's `approve`) with one grader, at one request per record.
  - **Recommend C** — three gaps (grid, policy, held set unknown) share one cause, that the browser regrades what only the Data boundary can know; `transitionsAt`'s own comment already says *a screen that needs the true list asks the SERVER*, and it keeps Invariant 4's one grader.
  - **Also holds `FJS-2997`** — `resource.can('update')` offers Edit on a row the caller reads and the update policy refuses; litestone's `$checkWrite(accessor, 'update', row)` already grades gate, grid and policy and answers `{ ok, rule }`. Red assertion: under a row policy `owner = $auth.id`, a reader's `can('update', level)` is `true` while the PATCH is 403. Under C the per-row ask answers `can` and `transitions` with one call; under A the row stays open.

- **FJS-D1033 — Where does a vendor subscription's lifecycle live, and is it a new noun?** Found fixing FJS-2573. Nothing in `packages/conduit/src` or `packages/caravan/src` models a vendor-side subscription (conduit's `subscriptions` map is live BROKER consumers, `FJS-D235`, a different thing), so there is no red test to write until a shape is chosen. The need, measured in ELA's Graph mail and Gmail's watch: a lease with a max lifespan (4230 min / 7 days), renewed before it lapses, re-created on `reauthorizationRequired` / `subscriptionRemoved` / `missed`, with a per-subscription secret (`clientState`) minted at create, kept on renew and rotated on re-create; a hand-built self-re-arming chain dies silently. The red assertion: a subscription declared with `lifespan` and `renewBefore`, after a renew job throws once, still has a renew scheduled and `stats()` names it overdue — today there is nothing to declare.
  - **A** — a conduit noun: a connector declares `subscription { create, renew, lifespan, renewBefore, onLifecycle(event) }` (pure vendor calls, `FJS-D153`), the app binds it to a credential, and conduit owns the schedule (a Caravan job under a stated `dispatch({ id })`), the secret's mint/keep/rotate, and an overdue entry in `stats()` and readiness. A new noun and a new conduit→caravan dependency edge, and the lifecycle events arrive through the Receiver, so it waits on `FJS-D899`.
  - **B** — no new noun: the connector exports `create`/`renew`/`recreate` as plain functions and a `renewAt(subscription)` helper; the app writes one `*.job.ts` cron that calls them and re-dispatches itself. Smallest, but it is the hand-built chain the row says dies silently, now with a helper.
  - **C** — a Caravan primitive, vendor-blind: a job that re-arms itself by declaration (`rearm: { before: expiresAt }`) and reports a broken chain as overdue; secrets, create and lifecycle events stay in the connector and app. Fixes the silent death for every self-re-arming job, not just subscriptions, and leaves conduit unchanged, but the secret and re-create halves of the row stay app code.
  - **Recommend C first, A's declaration only when a second vendor asks** — the silent death is the measured harm and it is a Caravan property, not a vendor one; `FJS-D899` C already keeps lifecycle events out of the Receiver until a second app asks, so A would be built against a record not yet ruled.

- **FJS-D1034 — Is a polled feed — a vendor read on a clock, resumed from a durable cursor — a new noun, and does it live in Caravan, in Conduit, or in the app?** Found fixing `FJS-2572`. `conduit-connectors.md` § *What is deliberately not on it* rules sync engines out of conduit ("their own noun when wanted") and ELA wants three (Service Autopilot's day window, 3CX's overlap-and-cap window, Graph's per-folder `deltaLink` with a 410 restart), each hand-building the cursor row, overlap and dedupe, cap, suspension, 04:00 backstop and one-runner-per-feed. Nothing in `packages/caravan/src` or `packages/conduit/src` holds a cursor; Caravan owns the clock and `dispatch({ id })` the one-runner guarantee, Conduit owns the dial and its breaker (`FJS-2580`, in memory). No red test yet — the shape decides what it asserts (a cursor that survives a restart and advances only on a completed non-backstop run).
  - **A** — a Caravan primitive, `defineFeed({ every, poll(cursor, ctx) -> { items, next }, overlap, cap, backstop })`: Caravan keeps the cursor in its own table, stamps it only when a round completes, clamps cold-start and future cursors, dispatches under a stated `id` per feed so there is one runner, and a thrown 410-class error resets the cursor. Vendor-blind (the vendor call is the app's `poll`, through `app.conduit.send`), so `FJS-D177` and `FJS-D153` are untouched; dedupe and tombstones stay the app's, in `items`. New noun and one new table.
  - **B** — no new noun: Caravan gains only a durable per-job `state` (get/set a JSON cursor atomically with the job's completion); each feed stays a `*.job.ts` that computes its own window. Smallest, and `FJS-D1033` C (re-arming) may want the same state column, but overlap, cap, backstop and the single runner stay hand-built three times.
  - **C** — a Conduit noun: a connector declares a `feed` beside its targets and Conduit owns the cursor and the schedule. Puts a sync contract in conduit — the `FJS-D153` inversion `conduit-connectors.md` names — and couples the clock to a package that has none.
  - **Recommend A, built on B's state column** — the harm measured is the same hand-built cursor, window and runner three times, and all of it is clock-and-state, which is Caravan's; the vendor call stays outside, so no sync contract enters conduit.
  - **Prior art — Junior** ([usejunior-sh/junior@533052b](https://github.com/usejunior-sh/junior/tree/533052b), read 2026-10-25), a local agent that polls GitHub, Slack and MCP sources on a clock. It supports A and adds four things A's shape should hold:
    - *A second feed shape: a snapshot diff, not an event cursor.* Its PR watch ([core/babysit/watch.ts](https://github.com/usejunior-sh/junior/blob/533052b/core/babysit/watch.ts)) diffs this poll's snapshot against the last one, a pure function with no I/O and no clock. Between polls it keeps only what a dedupe key can't (fix attempts per check, one rerun per check per commit, fingerprints of comments already handled), and the tasks dedupe on their key. An order's status or a PR's checks have no cursor to resume from, so A's `next` is the feed's opaque state, not necessarily a position.
    - *Dedupe by id makes the window safe.* Its job runs keep no cursor at all ([core/job/connectors.ts](https://github.com/usejunior-sh/junior/blob/533052b/core/job/connectors.ts)): each run scans a look-back window, and every match is an idempotent upsert keyed by a canonical id (`github:owner/repo#N`). That is `FJS-D1354`'s dispatch under the event's own id, so overlap needs no ledger beyond the job id, and A can leave dedupe to `dispatch({ id })`.
