  - **Recommend A and B both, A first** — A is the only shape that stops `check(parent)` inheriting a clause the child never meant (`FJS-1789`, `FJS-1801`, `FJS-1953` are one defect), and B is `FJS-D1027`'s C, owed a second app's evidence that Quo now supplies; if one must go first, A, and declare it with `@@allow` on a name the schema lists rather than a separate noun.

- ~~**FJS-D1094 — Do the `@@transitions` move words `@system` and `@gate(N)` keep their spelling with the overload documented once, or take move words of their own?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1094`): A — keep the spelling and document the overload once: the reference states the move word as the same fact at move grain (a floor on the act; the app's write), each move entry says so in its first sentence, and the *Move words* lead stops apologizing. Zero parser change, and knowing the column's word predicts the move's. A test that `explain @system` and `explain @gate` name both grains keeps it said.** Found fixing `FJS-2951`. The parser reads `@system` and `@gate(N)` after a move's target (`parseTransitions` in `src/core/parser.js`) and the reference's *Move words* lead apologizes: `litestone explain @system` answers for both the column and the move. Measured: both pairs say one thing — `@gate(N)` is a level floor on the act (the column, the model, the move), `@system` is "the application owns this write" (a `@system` column is written by the app, a `@system` move is made by the app with `{ system: true }`). About 58 files spell a move with either word.
  - **A** — keep the spelling and document the overload once: the reference states the move word as the same fact at move grain (a floor on the act; the app's write), each move entry says so in its first sentence, and the *Move words* lead stops apologizing. Zero parser change, and knowing the column's word predicts the move's. A test that `explain @system` and `explain @gate` name both grains keeps it said.
  - **B** — a move word of its own for one or both: e.g. `@engine` for the app's move and `@needs(N)` for the floor. Cost: two new nouns beside two that already mean the same thing, a rename across the parser, the catalog, the reference, the VS Code grammar and every schema that spells a move; and the column's `@system` and the move's `@engine` become two spellings of "the application owns this".
  - **C** — rename only `@system` on a move (the one whose move meaning is the narrower, "decided by the app on the call"): `@gate(N)` already composes with it as the same floor. Smaller reach than B, but a half-split — one word shared and one not.
  - **Recommend A** — the two grains are the same fact (`FJS-D46`-style: one word per fact, grain by position), so a second word is the second origin; the defect is a lead that reads as an apology, which prose fixes.
- ~~**FJS-D1095 — Which word gives way where `scope` (the schema-level key an `@@exclude` serializes on) and `@@scope` (a model's named read filter) collide — the declaration, the read filter, or neither?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1095`): C — keep both and delete the disclaimer, since a top-level keyword and a model attribute are told apart by position (`FJS-D46`-style: grain by position). Zero code change; the collision stays for anyone searching "scope".** Found fixing `FJS-2950`. The parser reads top-level `scope person(employeeId)` (`parseScope`, `FJS-D474`) and the model attribute `@@scope` as a named read filter; the reference's `scope` entry ends "Not `@@scope`", a disclaimer. Measured: `@@scope` is 167 hits in 54 files (one `.lite`); the top-level `scope` declaration is 4 hits in 3 files (reference, concurrency.md, assistant snapshot). `@sequence(scope:)` and `@rank(scope:)` use `scope` for the same thing the declaration does — the columns rows are grouped within — so the read filter is the odd use.
  - **A** — rename the declaration (e.g. `lock person(employeeId)`, matching the `$lock('person:…')` key `FJS-D474` compiles it to): `@@exclude(person, …)` still cites it by name. Smallest reach (parser, catalog, VS Code grammar, 3 docs), but `scope` then means "grouping key" in `@sequence`/`@rank` and not in `@@exclude`'s declaration — one concept, two words.
  - **B** — rename the read filter (e.g. `@@filter`): `scope` keeps its one meaning across `@sequence`, `@rank` and the declaration. Cost: 167 hits / 54 files, and `@@filter` collides with nothing in the catalog but sits near `where:` grammar the filter shares with `@@allow`.
  - **C** — keep both and delete the disclaimer, since a top-level keyword and a model attribute are told apart by position (`FJS-D46`-style: grain by position). Zero code change; the collision stays for anyone searching "scope".
  - **Recommend B** — one word per fact: `scope` already names "the group a rule applies within" three times, so the read filter is the second origin; the 167-hit cost is mechanical and pre-alpha owes no alias.

- **FJS-D1096 — How does a policy name a grant level that sits between two operations, so a child model can reach it through `check()`?** Notion's *can comment* (read < comment < edit) is not one of the five operations a parent has (`POLICY_OPS`, `access.js:28`; `VALID_POLICY_OPS`, `parser.js:3385`), so `Comment @@allow('create', check(block, 'update'))` refuses a comment-level guest; `probes/q4/comment-level.ts` still prints both spellings refused at parse (*crosses two relations*, *tests rows two relations away*). Red test's assertion: a guest granted `comment` creates a Comment on her page (today 403), while a `read` guest still cannot.
  - **A** — `@@allow` takes a declared extra operation: `Page { @@operations('comment') @@allow('comment', access.some(userId == auth().id && level in ['comment','edit','full'])) }`, and a child says `check(block, 'comment')` (Block forwards with `@@allow('comment', check(page, 'comment'))`). The operation is a name a model declares, never a sixth CRUD verb; undeclared names stay a parse error, and the grade stays at the Data boundary.
  - **B** — A policy may cross two relations when every hop is a `check()`-style delegation to a rule the far model already states: `check(block.page, 'comment')`. No new operation, but it breaks the one-hop rule (`FJS-499`) and the EXPLAIN-no-automatic-index guarantee.
  - **C** — The level becomes a first-class value, `@@level('Page', access.some(...) → level)` ordered by an `enum` (`read < comment < edit < full`), and `check(rel, level >= 'comment')` compares. One mechanism for every ordered grant, but it coins a noun and a second grading axis beside the operations.
  - **D** — No change: the child carries the grant (a copied `pageId` plus a denormalized `commentable Boolean` the owner of the page maintains in the same transaction). Stays inside four operations; the denormalized flag is a trigger-shaped hazard the schema cannot enforce.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the smallest spelling that keeps Invariant 6 (access declared in the schema), reuses `check()`'s one-hop delegation, and leaves the operation set closed unless a model opts in; `FJS-1245`'s *named move more permissive than an edit* reads as the same shape (a declared name on the parent) and could ride the same keyword.

- ~~**FJS-D1098 — What is the wire spelling and the envelope of a tree read: one structured `$recursive` directive graded at the Data boundary, a flat family, or no directive and a built-in read method?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-18 (`FJS-D1098`): A — one `$recursive` row in `DIRECTIVES` (`asIs`, OPAQUE schema, as `$orderBy`): the value is `{ direction, maxDepth, nested, via }`, travels as a structure (`$recursive[direction]=ancestors`, `FJS-D125`) and litestone grades the keys and refuses the unsupported combinations by name (`count`, `aggregate`, `limit` under `nested`). A tree read skips the default page: the page is `maxDepth`, and the envelope answers `total = data.length`, `limit`/`offset` absent. One origin for the grammar, the walk and its refusals stay where relations.md puts them. Cost: the first structured value in the table that the bridge does not read.** Found fixing `FJS-1748`. Red assertion: `unknownDirectives({ $recursive: 'ancestors' })` answers `['$recursive']` and `parseDirectives` answers `{}`, so the bridge refuses the read by name. Two things the candidate leaves open: `find` always sets `limit` (`paginate.default` 20, `max` 100) while litestone's `nested: true` REFUSES `limit`/`offset` and `count` refuses `recursive`, so a nested tree read over the wire would be refused by the default page and a flat one cut at 20 rows; and the `{ total, limit, offset, data }` envelope has no total a tree read can compute except `rows.length`.
  - **A** — one `$recursive` row in `DIRECTIVES` (`asIs`, OPAQUE schema, as `$orderBy`): the value is `{ direction, maxDepth, nested, via }`, travels as a structure (`$recursive[direction]=ancestors`, `FJS-D125`) and litestone grades the keys and refuses the unsupported combinations by name (`count`, `aggregate`, `limit` under `nested`). A tree read skips the default page: the page is `maxDepth`, and the envelope answers `total = data.length`, `limit`/`offset` absent. One origin for the grammar, the walk and its refusals stay where relations.md puts them. Cost: the first structured value in the table that the bridge does not read.
  - **B** — a flat family: `$recursive=ancestors|descendants` plus `$maxDepth`, `$nested`, `$via`, each with its own `read` in the table. The bridge grades every one, as it does the flags. Cost: four names for one feature, three of them meaningless alone, and a second place the combination rules live.
  - **C** — no directive: a model service gets built-in `tree` and `trail` read methods (`{ read: true }`) that call `findMany({ recursive })`. No grammar to grade, the envelope is the method's own. Cost: the browser client and `resource.find` cannot ask for a tree read as they ask for every other `$` shape, and each model service carries two methods the schema never named.
  - **Recommend A** — the grammar is already written and graded at the Data boundary, so the table row only carries it; B restates it field by field and C is the custom-method-per-model spelling `FJS-1748` was filed against.

- ~~**FJS-D1100 — Under `tenancy { strategy row }` does the framework name the difference between a MEMBER and an ADMITTED caller (a roster row with a lesser role, a bearer link holder who must carry the tenant claim), or does each app keep a role list in every policy?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-18 (`FJS-D1100`): A — Roles `membershipClaim` marks admitted-only: `membershipClaim({ …, standing: 'role', guests: ['guest'] })`. A guest row emits NO tenant claim as a policy-visible claim but still passes the tenant deny, by carrying the tenant on a second, admission-only claim the desugared `@@deny` reads; `auth().workspaceId` is then null for a guest and every `workspaceId == auth().workspaceId` policy answers false until the app grants the guest a narrower rule. A bearer link is admitted-only by construction (`bearerClaim` emits the tenant on the admission claim). Fail-closed: the common policy stays correct, the fix is one declaration, and widening for guests is opt-in. Cost: two claim names for the tenant (admission vs. membership) in the desugar and the principal, and the `tenancy { claim }` declaration must say which is which.** Found fixing `FJS-1732`. Three apps measured the same hole (notion guest, serpgrid share link, maidtech portal link): the row-tenancy desugar is a `@@deny` on the tenant claim, so any caller carrying the claim is admitted, and `workspaceId == auth().workspaceId` — the documented common policy — reads that admission as membership. `membershipClaim` (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts`) emits the tenant claim plus the standing under `standingAs` for ANY row it finds, and `bearerClaim` must emit the tenant claim beside its grant claim (`FJS-1731`). Red assertion: a principal `{ workspaceId: 'W1', memberRole: 'guest' }` reads every row of a model with `@@allow('read', workspaceId == auth().workspaceId)`. Today the app writes `memberRole in [...]` in every policy, and a model with no read `@@allow` is readable by every admitted guest.
  - **A** — Roles `membershipClaim` marks admitted-only: `membershipClaim({ …, standing: 'role', guests: ['guest'] })`. A guest row emits NO tenant claim as a policy-visible claim but still passes the tenant deny, by carrying the tenant on a second, admission-only claim the desugared `@@deny` reads; `auth().workspaceId` is then null for a guest and every `workspaceId == auth().workspaceId` policy answers false until the app grants the guest a narrower rule. A bearer link is admitted-only by construction (`bearerClaim` emits the tenant on the admission claim). Fail-closed: the common policy stays correct, the fix is one declaration, and widening for guests is opt-in. Cost: two claim names for the tenant (admission vs. membership) in the desugar and the principal, and the `tenancy { claim }` declaration must say which is which.
  - **B** — A named standing on the principal: `membershipClaim` emits `auth().isMember` (true unless the row's role is in `guests`), and `fli advise` flags a bare `<tenant> == auth().<tenant>` comparison on a scoped model with no `isMember`. Policies stay as written, the app adds `&& auth().isMember`. Fail-open until the advice is followed, which is the failure the row measured three times.
  - **C** — Keep it the app's: close the row as a measurement, document the role-list policy in `access-control.md` § Tenancy and add only an `fli advise` rule for `memberRole != null` and a read-gate-less scoped model. Zero language change; every app still restates the list in every policy, and a policy added later defaults to the guest's.
  - **Recommend A** — admission and membership are two facts and the claim carries one, so a guest or link holder is read as a member by construction; A makes the default policy correct and the widening explicit (Invariant 6, access declared in the schema), where B and C leave the unsafe spelling as the default and rely on an advisory.

- ~~**FJS-D1101 — In an untrusted schema, does `@secret` refuse, or does the host hand the client an audit sink it owns?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-21 (`FJS-D1101`): A — Refuse: `untrustedSchemaOffenses` adds `@secret` by name ("`@secret` logs to a trail database, which a row cannot declare — spell it `@encrypted @guarded`"). Zero new option; `packages/litestone/docs/encryption.md` § @secret says `@secret` is app-written only. Transit's landing schemas already spell it out.** Found fixing `FJS-1655`. `createClient({ schema: 'model Source { id Int @id; token String @secret }', db: ':memory:', untrusted: true })` builds with no `@trail` on `token` and no word anywhere: the parser's `@secret has no trail database declared` warning (`parser.js` ~6161) lands in `parse().warnings`, and `createClient` never reads `parseResult.warnings`. `refuseUntrustedSchema` refuses every `database` block (`FJS-1633`), so a row-held schema can never declare the trail database `@secret` promises. Red test: `createClient({ schema, db, untrusted: true })` on that schema throws today's-silence → asserts it throws naming `@encrypted @guarded`, or (B) that a write to `token` lands in the host's sink.
  - **A** — Refuse: `untrustedSchemaOffenses` adds `@secret` by name ("`@secret` logs to a trail database, which a row cannot declare — spell it `@encrypted @guarded`"). Zero new option; `packages/litestone/docs/encryption.md` § @secret says `@secret` is app-written only. Transit's landing schemas already spell it out.
  - **B** — Host sink: `createClient({ untrusted: true, trail: <sink> })` hands the client a trail database the host owns, and `@secret` binds to it. A new option and a new noun on the one surface `FJS-1633` shut; the audit lands where the app chose, so a tenant-authored `@secret` is audited.
  - **C** — Keep it silent-by-warning, but make `createClient` surface parse warnings (trusted too). Fixes "without a word" and leaves an untrusted `@secret` that promises an audit trail it can never have.
  - **Recommend A** — an untrusted schema's allow-list is "what grades and shapes rows in the one file", and a trail is another database; refusing by name keeps `@secret` meaning one thing (audited) wherever it parses, and B can be added later with a real caller.

- ~~**FJS-D1102 — Which one namespace do the framework's env vars live in, and which one name says where an origin is?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1102`): C — `FRONTIER_` prefix for everything the framework owns including origins (`FRONTIER_API_URL`), bare names left to the app.** Found fixing `FJS-2974`: the framework's own vars have two prefixes (`FLI_PORT_*` in 40 files, `FJS_SOURCE` in one) and the rest are bare; `PORT` and `FLI_PORT_BE` both set the API port (`packages/junction/src/config/index.ts` `envOverrides`), and an origin is named four ways — `API_URL` (49 files), `APP_URL` (21), `WEB_URL` (8), `VITE_API_URL` (32, the build-time inline). No red test yet: the defect is the absence of a rule, and a test asserts whichever spelling is ruled.
  - **A** — one `FJS_` prefix for what the framework reads or hands out (`FJS_PORT_BE`, `FJS_SOURCE`); a deploy's own words stay bare (`PORT`, `DATABASE_URL`, `DEBUG`) and junction reads `PORT` only; origins are `API_URL` (where a client reaches the API) and `APP_URL` (where a person reaches the SPA), `WEB_URL` deleted, and `VITE_API_URL` stays only as the name Vite exposes to a browser bundle, set from `API_URL` by Sierra.
  - **B** — keep `FLI_PORT_*` as the broker's namespace and rename only the stray: `FJS_SOURCE` → `FLI_SOURCE`, so the cli owns one prefix; junction reads `PORT` only; origins as in A. Smaller change, but a var the app or Sierra reads (`FLI_PORT_BE` in a deployed app's config) still carries the cli's name.
  - **C** — `FRONTIER_` prefix for everything the framework owns including origins (`FRONTIER_API_URL`), bare names left to the app.
  - **Recommend A** — the broker's var and the deploy's var stop shadowing each other because only one is read, and the origin names that remain are the two nouns an app has (API, SPA). Order it after `IDEAS/lockdown.md` 3.3 (junction reads the port once) and do the rename in 5.3 as one sweep.

- ~~**FJS-D1103 — How does `litestone restore` learn where a layout file goes: the copy names it, or the live tree does?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1103`): A — The copy carries a manifest. `backup` writes `db-layout.json` (`{ sqlite: [{ name, rel }], files: [rel] }`), `replicate` writes the same file to `<url>/db-layout.json`; restore reads it, so the hyphen ambiguity (`db-a-b.db`) and the unlistable bucket both go away. `db-files/` is put back only where nothing is at the path, and `schema.lite`/`migrations/` are skipped by name. One new file in the copy format and a replicate step that must rewrite it when the layout changes.** Found fixing `FJS-1509`. `restoreFromBackup` (`cli.js` ~4416) finds a backup's `db-<path>.db` by the name the LIVE walk gives a file, so on a fresh volume (empty `db/`, the case a restore exists for) `db-jobs.db` lands in `unplaced` and the run is refused; `db-files/` is never put back (it holds `schema.lite` and `migrations/` beside stored bytes, which are the repo's); and the replica path (`cmdRestore`) drops `layout` from `copyTargets` entirely, since litestream cannot list a bucket and nothing in the replica says which `db-*` names exist. The red test: restore a `backup` of `{db/schema.lite, db/jobs.db, db/public/a.png}` into an empty `db/` with `--force` — today it exits 1 at "nothing on this machine is at the path it came from", and no `jobs.db` or `a.png` exists after.
  - **A** — The copy carries a manifest. `backup` writes `db-layout.json` (`{ sqlite: [{ name, rel }], files: [rel] }`), `replicate` writes the same file to `<url>/db-layout.json`; restore reads it, so the hyphen ambiguity (`db-a-b.db`) and the unlistable bucket both go away. `db-files/` is put back only where nothing is at the path, and `schema.lite`/`migrations/` are skipped by name. One new file in the copy format and a replicate step that must rewrite it when the layout changes.
  - **B** — No manifest; read the source. A backup restore lists `db-*.db` and `db-files/` from the directory and places `db-<rest>.db` at `<root>/<rest>.db` (hyphens literal, so a nested `a/b.db` comes back as `a-b.db` — refuse a name that matches no flat file and say so); a replica restore names only what a flag lists (`--include jobs`). No new noun, but a nested `*.db` is a guess and a replica restore needs the operator to know the layout.
  - **C** — Keep restore scoped to the declared set and make the gap loud: `restore` prints every `db-*` / `db-files` it found in the backup and did not place, exits non-zero without `--partial`, and `docs/replication.md` names the by-hand copy. Zero format change; the gap stays, now refused rather than a footnote.
  - **Recommend A** — `FJS-D493` made the layout the list, and a list that can only be read off the tree being replaced is empty exactly when it is needed; a manifest is the smallest thing that lets the source say where its own files go, and it covers the replica, which B cannot.

- **FJS-D1104 — Does the trail get a read grain at the DATA OWNER's level (a count per row per period, no actor, no clock, no correlation id), or does that stay an app-written `@@anonymous` counter?** Found fixing `FJS-1424`. `@@trail(audit, reads: true)` writes one entry per read carrying `actorId`, `createdAt` and `correlationId` (parser `trail` case, ~2652), so a search that reads Joe's lens leaves a per-person clock of every search that joins to the query text in each provider's log (`FJS-1413`); `@@anonymous` (`FJS-D349`) turns the model's logging off whole. Between the two there is no spelling for *Joe's golf lens was used N times this month*. The red test would assert a read of a lens by a grantee leaves a row naming the lens and a count and no actor, clock or correlation id; there is no spelling to write it in until this is ruled.
  - **A** — A third value of `reads`: `@@trail(audit, reads: counted)`. The trail keeps one row per (model, record id, UTC day) holding `n`, upserted on each read; no actor, no per-read clock, no correlation id. One new value on an existing option and one new trail-side table. Leaves the single-grantee hole (the count is that grantee's topic frequency), so it needs a floor (`min`) or a documented refusal for a model with one reader.
  - **B** — No framework change. The app keeps an `@@anonymous` counter model (Portal's `Usage`) and increments it in the service that reads; the trail stays reader-grained and the doc says which one to use. Zero new nouns; the cost is each app re-deriving a counter, and nothing stops it being written beside an attributed read in one transaction (the `FJS-D349` check refuses only a logged WRITE beside it).
  - **C** — Coarsen the existing entry instead: `reads: true` plus a word that drops `actorId` and `correlationId` and rounds `createdAt` to the day. Reuses the trail table, but a coarse per-read row is still one row per read, so rowid order re-attributes it exactly as `FJS-D349` describes; it only works if rows are folded, which is A.
  - **Recommend B** — the row's own measurement says it works today and that the unsolved part (a lone grantee's count is their topic frequency) is a question of grain no attribute fixes; A earns its place when a second app needs the counter, and then as `reads: counted` with the floor decided against two real cases rather than Portal's one.

- **FJS-D1105 — Should a fresh app root carry four markdown files (README, AGENTS, AGENTS.project, CLAUDE), or fewer?** `appAgentsMd`/`appAgentsProjectMd`/`appClaudeMd` in `packages/cli/core/app-config.js` write three agent files that import each other (`CLAUDE.md` → `AGENTS.md` → `AGENTS.project.md`) beside the README; the layering keeps the framework's text and the app's own apart, and `AGENTS.project.md` is a seven-line stub until somebody fills it. Found fixing FJS-2972.
  - **A** — Keep all four: the import chain is why framework text can be rewritten without touching what the developer wrote.
  - **B** — Drop `AGENTS.project.md`: `AGENTS.md` ends with a `## This app` section the developer owns and the framework never rewrites after `fli new`; `CLAUDE.md` still imports `AGENTS.md`. Three files.
  - **C** — Drop `CLAUDE.md` too and name `AGENTS.md` in the README for Claude Code users. Two files, but Claude Code never opens `AGENTS.md` on its own.
  - **Recommend B** — the stub is the only file a fresh app carries with no content of its own, and nothing the framework writes afterwards touches either half.

- **FJS-D1106 — Should a nested hasMany/m2m create write its children as one `createMany` batch, or keep each child a full `create` verb?** Found fixing `FJS-3002`. `processHasManyNested` (`client.js` ~5601) awaits `tbl.create` once per child. Measured on `:memory:`, 200 children under one parent: nested 7.75 ms, the same rows through `createMany` 1.52 ms (38.7 µs vs 7.6 µs per child). The saving is the per-verb overhead, not statements: `createMany` also runs one INSERT per row, and the `onQuery` tap reports it once per shape. What a batch changes is what each child sees: `beforeCreate` gets one call with a `data` array instead of one call per row, the write event is one `collection` event instead of N `row` events (`FJS-307`), and a child carrying its own nested ops cannot be batched at all.
  - **A** — Close as designed. A nested child is a full create so every plugin hook, `$tapEvents` consumer and live store sees the same N row events whether the caller nested or looped; the 5x is the price of that. Document it in the nested-writes doc.
  - **B** — Batch only the flat case. A hasMany `create` whose children name no nested ops of their own goes through `prepareCreateMany` + `createManyPlan` inside the parent's unit; anything else stays per child. The event for a nested child becomes one collection event, so the batch is announced the way `createMany` is, and the doc states that nesting and looping no longer announce alike.
  - **C** — Batch flat children and keep per-row events: `createManyPlan` runs, then the announcement is split back into one `row` event per child. Same speed as B, same hook shape as B for `beforeCreate`, but no consumer sees a difference on the event side. Costs a second announce path beside the owner (Invariant 4).
  - **Recommend C** — a consumer of write events should not be able to tell a nested create from a loop, so the gain has to land without moving the event; the `beforeCreate` array-vs-row difference is the one visible change left, and `createMany` already sets that precedent for every bulk write.

- ~~**FJS-D1107 — Which arithmetic does the expression language gain — `*` alone, `+ - *`, or `+ - * /` — and in which sites?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-21 (`FJS-D1107`): A — `+ - *` over numeric operands, with `*` binding tighter than `+ -` and both tighter than a comparison; the existing `now() ± duration` stays its own node. Valid in every site that parses an expression (`@derived`, `@@scope`, `@@allow`), the type check refuses a non-numeric operand by name, and a null operand makes the result null, which the existing *null is false* rule then grades. No `/`, so no integer-division or divide-by-zero semantics to rule on; a discount is `sum * 90` against a scaled bound, a tax needs `@computed` until `/` is asked for by a third caller.** Found fixing `FJS-2710`. The lexer has no `*` or `/`, and `+`/`-` parse only as a duration after `now()` (`toolbelt/src/predicate/parse.js` `durationOffset`), so `@derived(a * b)`, `a + b` and `a - b` all fail `createClient` with *schema.lite has errors* (probed). `FJS-D950` already rules that `underRatio Boolean @derived(adults * eventRatioAttendees < eventEnrolled * eventRatioAdults)` is how eventmark states its ratio, so `*` in `@derived` is settled; the operator set, the sites and the null/zero semantics are not. Every node lands in both `compileSql` and `evaluate` (held by `test/policy-interpreters.test.ts`), and JS `null * 3` is `0` where SQL's is `NULL`. The red test: `createClient` over `c Int @derived(a * b)` builds, and `where: { c: 12 }` finds a row with `a: 3, b: 4`.
  - **A** — `+ - *` over numeric operands, with `*` binding tighter than `+ -` and both tighter than a comparison; the existing `now() ± duration` stays its own node. Valid in every site that parses an expression (`@derived`, `@@scope`, `@@allow`), the type check refuses a non-numeric operand by name, and a null operand makes the result null, which the existing *null is false* rule then grades. No `/`, so no integer-division or divide-by-zero semantics to rule on; a discount is `sum * 90` against a scaled bound, a tax needs `@computed` until `/` is asked for by a third caller.
  - **B** — `*` alone, in `@derived` only. Smallest step that closes `FJS-D950`'s ratio; `FJS-2710`'s discount-plus-sum stays `@computed`, and the same text in `@@scope` stays refused.
  - **C** — `+ - * /` in every site, `/` as SQLite's with a zero divisor reading null. Closes the ELA tax case too, at the price of SQLite's integer division (`7 / 2` is `3` on two `Int`s) in a language whose JS half would have to copy it.
  - **Recommend A** — both callers need `*`, the ELA discount needs `+ -` beside a sum, and `/` is the one operator whose SQL and JS answers differ for ordinary inputs, so it waits for a caller that cannot go without it.
- **FJS-D1108 — What exactly does `FJS-D908`'s zone-aware `where` operator mean, and how does the sync where-builder learn the zones present?** Found fixing `FJS-1234`. `FJS-D908` ruled `{ startsAt: { localTime: { atOrBefore: '08:00', zone: 'inviteeTimeZone' } } }` lowered to `(zone = ? AND startsAt <= ?) OR …`, and left three things the lowering has to decide. (1) `08:00` on WHICH day: `atOrBefore` reads as a time-of-day test on `startsAt` ("starts at or before 08:00 local" — the `tooLate` rows), while the reminder the row needs is "08:00 on the morning of `startsAt`'s local date has passed", an instant per row. (2) `buildWhere` (`query.js`) and `buildWhereWithEncryption` (`client.js`) are sync and called from count, update and delete as well as `findMany`, and `SELECT DISTINCT zone` is a query, which in the worker engine is async. (3) The row's own measured case is a zone two relations away (`Shift → Employee → User`), which A does not reach. Red test: `findMany({ where: { startsAt: { localTime: … } } })` on a `@zone`-column model throws the unknown-operator error today and should return only the rows whose zone-local reminder instant is at or before `now`.
  - **A** — the operator is "the wall-clock `<time>` on `startsAt`'s own local date, in the row's zone, has passed `<now>`": `{ startsAt: { localTime: { reached: '08:00', zone: 'inviteeTimeZone', at: now } } }`, name it for the reminder question rather than `atOrBefore`. The zones come from one awaited `SELECT DISTINCT zone` before the SQL is built, so the builder stays sync and the read path gains one pre-step (the extensible/encrypted rewrites' slot); it is refused on `count`/`update`/`delete` by name until a caller needs it there. Same-row zone only, as ruled.
  - **B** — as A, but enumerate rather than read: group `Intl.supportedValuesOf('timeZone')` by their current offset and lower to `(zone IN (…) AND startsAt <= ?) OR …`, one branch per distinct offset. No extra query and the builder stays sync everywhere, at the cost of a wider OR and a zone name ICU does not list silently matching nothing.
  - **C** — keep `atOrBefore` as the time-of-day test it reads as (the `tooLate` question) and answer the reminder question with `FJS-D963`'s derived instant column instead, so no operator is added and the two-relation case is solved by the same mechanism. Closes `FJS-1234` by closing `FJS-D963`; the `where` stays instant-only.
  - **Recommend C** — the row's measured case is two relations away, which A and B both leave open, and `FJS-D963` already asks litestone to derive the instant a wall clock and its zone resolve to; that is a column `where` can index, the zone-correction staleness it names is the same defect, and it avoids the sync-builder question altogether. If `FJS-D963` is refused, A.

- **FJS-D1110 — Does `FJS-1980`'s sighting reopen `FJS-D817`: where does an enum member's tone live so `<Cell>` can draw it?** Found fixing `FJS-1980`. `FJS-D817` refused `@tone` in `.lite` ("a `.lite` file names no UI word") and named `FJS-1980` as the sighting that reopens it; the settled home is the resource's `<script module>` (`ui-hazards` resources-and-forms). `Cell.mesa:219` draws an enum as `<Badge>{enumLabel(value)}</Badge>` with no tone, so ela keeps `TaskStatus`/`AssetStatus`/`InvoiceStatus` maps in the template. A red test would assert a Cell over an enum field with a stated tone renders `.badge.<tone>`; today it renders none.
  - **A** — keep `FJS-D817`: no tone in the schema or in `<Cell>`; apps keep a template map. Close `FJS-1980` as accepted cost.
  - **B** — the resource's `<script module>` states a tone map per enum field and `<Cell>` reads it from the resource, so the map lives once and `.lite` stays free of UI words.
  - **C** — reopen `FJS-D817`: `@tone(<ident>)` on a member beside `@label`, emitted as `x-tones`, folded into `rule.options` by `buildFieldRules()`; the tone list moves to toolbelt so the parser refuses an unknown one (the withdrawn paper's option B).
  - **Recommend B** — it removes the repeated map without undoing the ruling that a schema names no UI word, and `<Cell>` already receives the resource.

- **FJS-D1111 — Does `upsert`'s `update` half take an atomic operator (reopening `FJS-D27`'s refusal), and does the one-statement fast path learn a composite unique `where`?** Found fixing `FJS-1498`. `extractWriteOps(updateData, { where: 'upsert' })` (`client.js` `planUpsert`) refuses `{ count: { increment: 1 } }` because `FJS-D27` judged the fast path (SET from `excluded`) and the slow path (`update()`) could not both apply it. Two facts cut against that: `upsertPlan` builds its SET itself, so it can emit `"count" = "count" + ?` as `updateMany` does, and the slow path's `this.update(...)` already applies operators. The row's own case, `where: { source, quarter }`, never reaches the fast path anyway: `planUpsert` returns null unless `where` names ONE column in `_upsertUniqueCols()`, so a pair falls to findFirst, then create, then update after a caught conflict. Red test: `upsert({ where: { source, quarter }, create: { …, count: 1 }, update: { count: { increment: 1 } } })` throws today; it should leave `count` at 2 after two calls, with no lost bump under concurrency.
  - **A** — Operators allowed in the update half only (create still refuses: nothing to change). The fast path emits the operator in its SET, the slow path passes it to `update()`, and the fast path accepts a `where` naming every column of a declared `@@unique` pair as its `ON CONFLICT(a, b)` target. The counter becomes one statement. Rewrites `FJS-D27`'s upsert clause; the `@gte`-style refusal on the column stays.
  - **B** — As A without the composite target. The operator is accepted and both paths apply it, but a pair-keyed counter stays findFirst + create + update-on-conflict. Correct under concurrency through the existing retry, still three round trips, so `FJS-1498`'s title is not met.
  - **C** — Keep `FJS-D27` as ruled and make the refusal name the way out (`updateMany` with the operator, then `create`, retrying `updateMany` on a unique conflict). Smallest change; every aggregate table keeps writing the three-statement loop.
  - **Recommend A** — the refusal's reason (one path could not apply an operator) does not hold, and without the composite target the operator buys nothing for the pair-keyed aggregate that motivated the row.

- ~~**FJS-D1112 — How does the browser evaluate a `@generated` expression and a `@from` filter over a draft?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-21 (`FJS-D1112`): D — the quote says which, as `parseGenerated` already rules for SQL against a template (`parser.js` `parseGenerated`): an UNQUOTED `@generated(...)` is the policy expression language (`FJS-D1107`'s `+ - *` and `round()`), emitted as `x-generated` and previewed by `evaluate`; a backtick template is previewable too; a `"SQL"` string stays legal and server-only (`readOnly`, as today). Of the 13 `@generated` in the tree, 4 numeric and 3 templates preview, 6 keep SQL the language lacks (`coalesce`, `nullif`, `trim`, `replace`, `CASE`, a column ratio). Django's `GeneratedField` is the same shape: ORM expressions, with `RawSQL` as the escape.** Found fixing `FJS-2747`. Probed: `@generated("cast(round({unitPrice} * {quantity} / 100.0) as integer)")` is a raw SQL string the client never receives (`jsonschema.js` emits only `readOnly`), and `@from(..., where: "status != 'lost' and per = 'week'")` is a SQL-ish filter; ela's `quote.js` restates both by hand (`lineAmount`, `sumsOf`) and `document-editor.test.ts:162` pins `lineAmount` to the server's `amount`. The policy expression language (`toolbelt/src/predicate`) already has `compileSql` + `evaluate` held together by `test/policy-interpreters.test.ts`, but no `*`/`/` (`FJS-D1107`) and `@generated` does not use it. Red test: a resource over a draft `{ unitPrice: 1250, quantity: 250 }` reports `amount` 3125 without the app writing the formula.
  - **A** — `@generated` and `@from ... where` move onto the policy expression language (needs `FJS-D1107`'s `+ - *` and a rounding/cast word); the schema emits the parsed expression as `x-generated` / `x-from`, and Sierra's resource gains `resource.preview(draft, lines)` calling the existing `evaluate`. One language, two interpreters already held in step; the cost is that raw SQL in `@generated` stops being legal (every existing `@generated` is rewritten, no back-compat needed pre-alpha).
  - **B** — keep `@generated` as SQL, emit it as `x-generated`, and ship a small SQL-subset interpreter (`cast`, `round`, `+ - * /`, comparisons, `and/or`, column refs) in toolbelt with SQLite's integer-division and half-away rounding copied. Least schema churn; a second expression language to hold against SQLite forever, and anything outside the subset must be refused at parse.
  - **C** — evaluate with the real engine: litestone already runs in a browser (`FJS-D305`, wasm over opfs), so a draft is inserted into a throwaway in-memory db and read back. Zero drift by construction; an async, heavy path (sqlite wasm in a live-typing preview), and it needs a draft's `@from` children copied in as well.
  - **D** — the quote says which, as `parseGenerated` already rules for SQL against a template (`parser.js` `parseGenerated`): an UNQUOTED `@generated(...)` is the policy expression language (`FJS-D1107`'s `+ - *` and `round()`), emitted as `x-generated` and previewed by `evaluate`; a backtick template is previewable too; a `"SQL"` string stays legal and server-only (`readOnly`, as today). Of the 13 `@generated` in the tree, 4 numeric and 3 templates preview, 6 keep SQL the language lacks (`coalesce`, `nullif`, `trim`, `replace`, `CASE`, a column ratio). Django's `GeneratedField` is the same shape: ORM expressions, with `RawSQL` as the escape.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only option with one language and one definition of each word, and `FJS-D1107` is already open on the arithmetic it needs; B builds a second SQL dialect to keep honest, C makes a keystroke pay for a database.

- **FJS-D1113 — Does a field's `@default` read a column across a to-one relation (`@default(account.taxPercent)`) at create, and what does the parent's read rule owe it?** Found fixing `FJS-2711`. Today `@default` takes a literal, `now()`, `cuid()` or `auth().<field>` (`applyAuthDefaults`, `src/core/client.js`), so ELA's `Document.taxPercent` copying `Account.taxPercent` is a service hook, and the generated create form seeds `0`, which beats the hook's fill-only-null copy. The red test's assertion: `db.document.create({ accountId })` stores `taxPercent` equal to the account's; today it stores null/refused. `@from` is the nearest word and is a LIVE read, not a copy frozen at create.
  - **A** — `@default(<relation>.<column>)`, one hop, evaluated in the create path after the FK is resolved (read as the engine, like `@default(auth().id)`); the field is optional in `create`'s JSON Schema and the form leaves it blank-to-fill, so `make()` stops seeding 0. A later change to the parent never touches the row. The parent column must be readable by the caller (`FJS-D576`: a foreign key is a read) or the copy is refused with the same `ForeignKeyError`.
  - **B** — as A, but the copy reads as `asSystem()` regardless of the parent's gate (a blind copy, like a `@system` stamp), so a caller who may attach but not read the parent still gets the value. Smaller surprise for the form, but a gate-protected parent column leaks into a row the caller can read.
  - **C** — keep the service hook and fix only the browser cost: `make()` seeds nothing for a field a hook fills (`@transient`-style "server fills" marker), so the create form omits it. No new default form; the copy stays app code and `FJS-2711` closes as accepted.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the one reading that puts the copy in the schema (Invariant 6's direction), reuses the engine-stamped-default seam the `auth()` form already owns, and keeps `FJS-D576`'s rule that a foreign key is a read instead of opening a leak path; B trades that rule for convenience and C leaves every app writing the same hook.

- ~~**FJS-D1114 — How does a host's isolation/ladder test stay green when a rig adds models?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1114`): A — the pin becomes a floor by convention: `arrayContaining` of the owned models plus `failed(report) === []`, taught by the tutor/scaffold template. Zero framework code.** Found fixing `FJS-2759`. maidtech's `api/test/access.test.ts` ends `verifyTenantIsolation` with `expect(report.filter(r => r.got === 'graded').map(r => r.model).sort()).toEqual([...24 names])`; after installing quotes-invoices the four rig models are graded `crossed on every operation` and `failed(report)` is `[]`, yet the `toEqual` fails on the extra names. Fails today: any `extend model`/import adds a name to the report and the exact-list pin refuses it. No installer exists yet (`RIGS.md` R1), so nothing owns that line.
  - **A** — the pin becomes a floor by convention: the host asserts `arrayContaining` of the models it owns plus `failed(report) === []`, and the tutor/scaffold template teaches that shape. Zero framework code; the cost is that a host which drops a model from grading (a gate removed) stops being noticed by this line, though `failed()` and the schema still cover it.
  - **B** — the report marks where a model came from (`imported: true` on each entry whose model arrives through an `import`/`extend`), and the host pins `report.filter(r => r.got === 'graded' && !r.imported)`. The exact pin survives for owned models; an `@@external`-style flag on a verifier row is a new field on a report three verifiers share.
  - **C** — the installer owns the line (`fli add` rewrites the pin's list, `fli remove` reverses it). Keeps the exact pin; makes `fli add` edit a host's test file, which `RIGS.md` R1 does not list and which breaks the moment the host reformats the line.
  - **Recommend A** — the exact pin was only ever a drift tripwire, and `failed(report)` already fails a model that is not crossed; a floor over the owned models keeps the tripwire for what the host controls without a new report field (B) or an installer that edits test code (C).

- **FJS-D1115 — Does a `findFirst` whose `where` pins one row by a unique key read as the single-row read (`@omit` present), or does `findFirst` stay list-mode?** Found fixing `FJS-2253`. `findFirst({ where: { id: 1 } })` reads with `mode: 'list'` (`client.js` findFirst, `read(_raw, { mode: 'list' })`), so `@omit` leaves `bio` out, while `findUnique({ where: { id: 1 } })` returns it; `litestone.test.ts` "`@omit`: excluded from findFirst" pins today's behavior and `docs/schema.md:149` states it. The red assertion: `'bio' in await db.user.findFirst({ where: { id: 1 } })` is false today and true under A or C.
  - **A** — `findFirst` reads as `single` when its `where` is exactly equality on a unique key or the primary key (the shape `findUnique` accepts, already classified for the fast path); any other `findFirst` stays list. The two spellings of one read agree; `findFirst({ where: { role: 'admin' } })` still strips. The mode then depends on the `where`'s shape, which one more sentence in the catalog states.
  - **B** — keep `findFirst` list-mode as written and tested; sharpen the `@omit` catalog line to say `findFirst` is a list read capped at one, and that `findUnique` is the only single-row read. No code change; the surprise is documented, not removed.
  - **C** — every `findFirst` reads as `single` (it returns one row, so it is the single-row read by its own name). `@omit(lists)` would then mean only `findMany`/`findManyAndCount`; `@omit(all)` unchanged. Simplest rule, but a `findFirst({ orderBy })` over a wide table now carries the long column.
  - **Recommend A** — the surprise is specifically the by-key `findFirst` that reads as a single-row lookup; A removes it without making a scan-and-take-one carry the heavy column, and C trades one surprise for another.

- ~~**FJS-D1116 — Which people may auth's `people` service reach — by what rule does it decide a person is inside the operator's tenant?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-16 (`FJS-D1116`): A — derive it from the app's own resolver: `aim()` re-resolves the PERSON with `app.runAs(person, { tenant: app.tenant() }, () => app.withDb((_, user) => user))`, so the app's principal resolver answers in the operator's tenant. The person is reached only where the tenancy claim it returns equals `app.tenant()`, otherwise 404 as today. `standingLevel` grades that re-resolved principal. `remove`, which deletes the whole `User`, is refused below SYSADMIN when the person holds a membership outside this tenant. One rule for both shapes: an `accountId` tenancy and a membership tenancy each answer through their own claim. No tenancy declared means no scoping, which is today's null-`accountId` operator. Uses existing seams only and adds no option.** Found filing `FJS-3016`. `aim()` (`packages/auth/services.ts`) compares `User.accountId` (`FJS-2643`) and grades `standingLevel(person)` on a session with no tenant in it. Measured over basecamp's real app: workspace A's admin reads, signs out and removes a developer of workspace B only, since the two share an Account and the person grades VISITOR(1). linear and notion leave `accountId` null and skip the check entirely. maidtech, sstime and ela tenant on `accountId` and are what the `FJS-2643` check serves.
  - **A** — derive it from the app's own resolver: `aim()` re-resolves the PERSON with `app.runAs(person, { tenant: app.tenant() }, () => app.withDb((_, user) => user))`, so the app's principal resolver answers in the operator's tenant. The person is reached only where the tenancy claim it returns equals `app.tenant()`, otherwise 404 as today. `standingLevel` grades that re-resolved principal. `remove`, which deletes the whole `User`, is refused below SYSADMIN when the person holds a membership outside this tenant. One rule for both shapes: an `accountId` tenancy and a membership tenancy each answer through their own claim. No tenancy declared means no scoping, which is today's null-`accountId` operator. Uses existing seams only and adds no option.
  - **B** — the app answers: a new `services: { reaches(operator, person) }` predicate, required when the app declares tenancy. Each app writes the rule again, beside a resolver that already knows it, and an app that forgets it gets FJS-3016's answer.
  - **C** — `people` floors at SYSADMIN whenever a tenant is in scope, and workspace admins use the app's own member service. Smallest build, but it locks the account admins of maidtech, sstime and ela out of the surface `FJS-2643` scoped for them.
  - **Recommend A** — the tenant a person is in has one origin, the app's principal resolver and tenancy block, and A reads it there. B restates it per app, and C trades a cross-tenant hole for a lockout in three live ports.

- ~~**FJS-D1117 — Does auth's User fragment keep `accountId`, once the app's tenancy declaration is what scopes an operator?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-16 (`FJS-D1117`): A — drop it from the fragment. An app whose tenant is an account declares the column and its `tenancy { column accountId }` itself, and those three writes stamp the column the app's tenancy names, read through `resolveTenancy(schema)`. Moves maidtech, sstime, ela and `fli new`'s scaffold.** Found filing `FJS-3016`; it is `FJS-1802`'s question. The fragment ships `accountId String?` as if every app has one account per person, while `references/Organization.lite` answers with a `Member` row. Under D1116-A auth stops reading `accountId` for authority, and what is left is `createUser({ accountId })`, `fli auth:create-user --account` (`FJS-1949`), the invitation stamp, and the session carrying it.
  - **A** — drop it from the fragment. An app whose tenant is an account declares the column and its `tenancy { column accountId }` itself, and those three writes stamp the column the app's tenancy names, read through `resolveTenancy(schema)`. Moves maidtech, sstime, ela and `fli new`'s scaffold.
  - **B** — keep it, stated as the column an account-tenanted app's tenancy block names. A Member-tenanted app leaves it null and nothing reads it. No moves, but the fragment still offers a second answer that a Member app has to know to ignore.
  - **C** — keep it as it is.
  - **Recommend A** — B leaves the two answers in place and only adds a sentence about them. A makes the tenancy block the one answer.

- **FJS-D1119 — Does the schema get a frequency word ("at most one row per key per window", refused at write with a 409) and what is its shape — a literal window, or a window read from another row?** Found fixing FJS-2087. ela's survey cap is "one Survey per `clientId` in `SurveySetting.minDaysBetween` days", written as a query in the batch, so a manual send, a seed or a re-run is not held to it. `@@unique` cannot say a window and `@@index(where:)` cannot read `now()` or another row's column. The red test: create two rows for one key inside the window; today both succeed, where a unique key gives `UniqueConflictError`. It needs a new `@@` word, so no source was touched.
  - **A** — `@@rate([clientId], per: 30d, at: createdAt)`: a literal window, checked inside the write's transaction (SQLite's single writer makes the count-then-insert atomic), refused with a 409 that names the key and the moment the window reopens. It cannot say ela's per-client override, which is another row's column, so ela keeps the override in its batch.
  - **B** — A, with `per:` also taking a column path (`per: days(setting.minDaysBetween)`, null meaning no cap): the window is read from the row's relation at write time. It covers ela whole, at the price of a path resolver in `per:` (`FJS-D908` already pays that for zones).
  - **C** — no word: document a bucket column (`@@unique([clientId, periodStart])` stamped by a hook) as the fixed-window answer. A rolling window stays a hand-written query, and the row stays what it is.
  - **Recommend B** — the harm measured is a rule with two writers and one of them unguarded, and the per-client override is half of that case; A would close the first half and leave the rule in the batch anyway.
  - **Also `FJS-3027`: the key may be the CALLER, not a column.** posthog's stolen `ProjectKey` posted 2,000 events in 1.99 s and ghost proposes `@@rate(create, 5 per 1min)` per principal, so the same count-in-the-write's-transaction check keyed by `principal` instead of `[clientId]`, with a `retry-after` rather than a reopen moment. Whichever shape is ruled should name both keys, or `FJS-3027` waits on a second word.

- ~~**FJS-D1120 — How does a non-root image user meet the root-owned `/db` bind mount the deploy hands it — what owns the uid?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1120`): A — image-only: `USER bun` plus `chown bun:bun /db` in the image, and `deploy:setup`'s `03-directories` creates the db dir owned by uid 1000. An already-provisioned server needs one `chown -R`, which `deploy:doctor` grades by name. The image says who runs; the host says who owns.** Found fixing FJS-2433. `makeDockerfile` in `commands/make/deploy.md` writes a runtime stage with no `USER`, so the app runs as root. Adding `USER bun` alone breaks every deployed app: `swapContainer` mounts `--volume {dbPath}:/db`, the host dir was created and written by root, and a uid-1000 process cannot open `/db`, so the migration fails, health fails and the deploy restores the previous container. The red test: the runtime stage of `makeDockerfile` has a `USER` line naming a non-root user; today it has none.
  - **A** — image-only: `USER bun` plus `chown bun:bun /db` in the image, and `deploy:setup`'s `03-directories` creates the db dir owned by uid 1000. An already-provisioned server needs one `chown -R`, which `deploy:doctor` grades by name. The image says who runs; the host says who owns.
  - **B** — the deploy states the uid: `06-swap`, `deploy:local` and rollback pass `--user $(stat -c %u:%g {dbPath})` to `docker run`, so the process runs as whoever owns the data and an existing root-owned dir keeps working. The Dockerfile still carries `USER bun` as the default for a bare `docker run`. Root survives wherever the dir is root-owned, which is every server today.
  - **C** — `USER bun` and nothing else; a server whose db dir is root-owned fails its first deploy with a health rollback, and the operator fixes ownership by hand. Smallest build, and the failure sits in the step that explains it least.
  - **Recommend A** — the owner is stated once, in the place that creates the directory, and `deploy:doctor` can grade it. B makes a root-owned dir silently keep root, which is the defect this row files.

- ~~**FJS-D1121 — How does `dev:api` learn a NEW service file exists: junction's loader, the scaffolded entry, or a message only?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1121`): A — junction's loader, dev only: under `--watch`, `fs.watch` the services dir and on a new `*.service.ts` rewrite the entry with its own bytes so bun restarts. Fixes every app with no scaffold change, and the one owner of *what a services dir holds* stays the loader; it costs junction knowing about bun's watch mode and rewriting a user's file in place.** Found fixing FJS-2431. `new.md` L245 scaffolds `dev:api` as `bun --watch run api/index.ts`; `autoloadServices` (`packages/junction/src/core/loader.ts:35`) finds files with `Bun.Glob` and `import()`s each, and bun watches only the files already imported. Reproduced in a scratch dir with that exact shape: adding `b.service.ts` restarts nothing, and the next edit to the already-imported `a.service.ts` is what finally boots with both. `fs.watch` on the directory plus `utimesSync` on the entry does NOT restart (bun ignores an mtime-only touch); rewriting the entry with its own bytes (`writeFileSync(Bun.main, readFileSync(Bun.main))`) does. The red test's assertion: a watch-mode boot that sees a new `*.service.ts` under the services dir ends up with that service registered, or says in one line that it is not.
  - **A** — junction's loader, dev only: under `--watch`, `fs.watch` the services dir and on a new `*.service.ts` rewrite the entry with its own bytes so bun restarts. Fixes every app with no scaffold change, and the one owner of *what a services dir holds* stays the loader; it costs junction knowing about bun's watch mode and rewriting a user's file in place.
  - **B** — fli's half: `fli dev` owns a directory watcher over each `api/` services dir and restarts that surface's process group (`dev-run.js` already owns the group). Nothing in junction changes, but a bare `bun run dev:api` still 404s, and `dev:api` stays the script the row's title names.
  - **C** — a message only: the loader, under `--watch`, watches the dir and prints one line (`new service X.service.ts — restart dev:api to register it`). No restart, no rewritten file; the 404 half of the row stays until the person acts.
  - **Recommend A** — the loader is the one place that knows which files are services, and the failure shows with a bare `bun run dev:api` as much as under `fli dev`.

- **FJS-D1123 — How does a deploy declare that an app answers hosts its config does not name?** Found fixing FJS-2268. `edgeRoutes()` (`packages/cli/core/edge.js`) throws `EdgeError` when no side names a domain, writes one `host`-matched route per `deploy.web.domain` / `deploy.api.domain`, and `applyEdge` sets `skip_unmapped_hosts`; so an agency's `reports.acme.com` CNAME reaches no app and Caddy fetches no certificate. A fix needs three things the edge lacks: a hostless fallback route (today refused: "a route matching every host would take over whatever else the machine serves"), a `tls.automation.policies[].on_demand` with `permission: { module: http, endpoint: <ask URL> }`, and a dev proxy that stops rewriting Host (`changeOrigin: true` in the `new.md` scaffold). The red test is `edgeRoutes({ …, tenantHosts })` returning a route with no `host` matcher and `applyEdge` output carrying the on-demand policy; none exists to write until the key's spelling is ruled.
  - **A** — a deploy key: `deploy.web.askPath: '/api/domains/ask'` (absent means no on-demand TLS, today's behavior). `edgeRoutes` adds a fallback route AFTER every named host that proxies the app's `/api/*` and `/ws` and serves the SPA, and `applyEdge` installs the on-demand policy with `endpoint: http://127.0.0.1:<apiPort><askPath>`, tagged by `@id` like the routes so a re-run replaces it. The scaffold's dev proxy drops `changeOrigin` for an app that sets the key. The ask path is config, so `deploy:doctor` can grade that it answers 200/4xx.
  - **B** — the app registers it: `app.claim('edge', { askPath })` or a manifest entry the deploy reads off the running container, so the schema/app owns "I answer foreign hosts" and `frontier.config.js` stays host-only. One more handoff for the deploy to read, and the edge then depends on a booted app.
  - **C** — no word: document the Caddy half as a hand-written admin-API call per app (`fli deploy:edge --raw`), and only fix the dev-proxy default. The edge stays pure strings over named domains, and each white-label app repeats the same snippet.
  - **Recommend A** — the edge's one-owner rule (`edge.js` header) already makes deploy config the single place hostnames are stated, and an optional key that defaults to today's output changes nothing for any app that does not set it.

- ~~**FJS-D1132 — How does a bearer read declare that it spends a budget?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-18 (`FJS-D1132`): B — a third act beside `mint`/`redeem`: `bearer.spend(db, ctx)` is the same conditional update, called once by the service method that IS the open, and the resolver stays a pure read. The count lands where the app means it, but a path that forgets to call it is still a free open, which is the defect the row names.** Found fixing FJS-3068. Vaultwarden's `Send` holds the budget as `@@check("accessCount <= maxAccessCount")`, which SQLite keeps under ten concurrent `accessCount + 1` updates, but `bearerClaim`'s resolver (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts` `bearerClaim`) reads the grant `findFirst` under `asSystem()` and counts nothing, and `redeem()` is the cookie trade for a `cookie()` link (`FJS-D340`) with no count. Red assertion: a grant with `maxAccessCount: 1` opened twice through a request carrying its token resolves both times, and a service that forgot the increment opens it a free `max+1`th time.
  - **A** — the resolver spends: `bearerClaim({ …, spends: { count: 'accessCount', max: 'maxAccessCount' } })` makes the lookup one conditional `UPDATE … SET accessCount = accessCount + 1 WHERE digest = ? AND accessCount < max RETURNING *`, and a spent grant is the same 401 sentence as an expired one. One option, no path forgets, and the budget is atomic by construction. Cost: EVERY request carrying the token spends, so a Send's open and its later file fetch are two spends; a service that wants "once per open" cannot say so.
  - **B** — a third act beside `mint`/`redeem`: `bearer.spend(db, ctx)` is the same conditional update, called once by the service method that IS the open, and the resolver stays a pure read. The count lands where the app means it, but a path that forgets to call it is still a free open, which is the defect the row names.
  - **C** — Litestone owns the declaration: `@@spends(accessCount, max: maxAccessCount)` on the grant model makes any `read` of the row through a bearer principal an atomic increment-or-refuse at the Data boundary (Invariant 6), `bearerClaim` unchanged. Access stays in the schema, but a read that mutates is a new kind of write and meets `FJS-D1054`'s `read: true` guard, so it waits on that ruling.
  - **Recommend A** — the resolver is the one place every bearer read passes through, so the budget cannot be forgotten; a Send's open is one request, and a finer "once per open" is a second word that waits for an app that needs it. C is the right end state but depends on `FJS-D1054`.

- ~~**FJS-D1133 — How does the schema say a required `@guarded` column is filled by the app under `system:`, so `advise` and `verifyGateLadder` stop calling the row uncreatable?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-21 (`FJS-D1133`): F — no new word: a *minted* column is spelt `@guarded @system`, the pair `FJS-235` already composes (invisible to a client, written by the application by naming it in `system:`). `required-guarded-uncreatable` skips a guarded column that also carries `@system`, which falls to `required-system-unfilled` instead; `verifyGateLadder` grades it the same way; and `refuseGuardedWrite` refuses a `system:` naming of a guarded column without `@system`, so plain `@guarded` means `asSystem()` only, as `packages/litestone/docs/modeling.md` already says. `Grant.lite`'s `tokenHash` becomes `@unique(global) @guarded @system`, and so do the four drafts.** Found fixing FJS-3043. `Grant.lite`'s `tokenHash String @unique(global) @guarded` is required, and `bearerClaim(...).mintOnCreate()` fills it in the `validated` stage by naming it in `system:` (`FJS-D819`) — but `required-guarded-uncreatable` (`advise.js:211`) reads only the schema, so on any host with `@@gate` below 8 it is an error, and `verifyGateLadder` (`testing.js:645`) reports the gate-0 host `not graded` at every level. Red test: `findings('import Grant … model Commenter { id Int @id  @@trait(Grant)  @@gate("0") }', 'required-guarded-uncreatable')` is non-empty today and must be empty; a plain `token String @guarded` with no marker must still fire (`advise.test.ts:57`). Four drafts carry the error (vaultwarden, immich) or split the digest onto a `@@gate("8")` row to clear it (ghost, statuspage).
  - **A** — the schema states it: a marker on the column (`@guarded(system)`, or an attribute `@minted`) that says the application fills it under `system:`. Both graders skip a marked column; `Grant.lite`'s `tokenHash` carries it; `refuseGuardedWrite` can then also refuse a `system:` naming of an UNmarked guarded column, which today is silent. One more spelling in the language, and a forgotten marker fails loud (the advise error) rather than silent.
  - **B** — the graders infer it: a `@guarded` + `@unique` column on a model that has an `@@allow('create', …)` or a `@@trait(Grant)` is treated as system-filled. No new word, but it is a guess — a model that merely looks like a grant is silenced, and `required-guarded-uncreatable` stops being a rule about the schema alone.
  - **C** — the schema stays as is and the DOC changes: `Grant.lite` and `bearer-access.md` stop recommending the on-row splice for a host below gate 8 and recommend the `@@gate("8")` grant row ghost and statuspage already wrote; `advise` keeps erroring. Loses `FJS-D819`'s point (the mint passes through the caller's own policy-graded create) for exactly the hosts that need it, which are the public-create ones.
  - **F** — no new word: a *minted* column is spelt `@guarded @system`, the pair `FJS-235` already composes (invisible to a client, written by the application by naming it in `system:`). `required-guarded-uncreatable` skips a guarded column that also carries `@system`, which falls to `required-system-unfilled` instead; `verifyGateLadder` grades it the same way; and `refuseGuardedWrite` refuses a `system:` naming of a guarded column without `@system`, so plain `@guarded` means `asSystem()` only, as `packages/litestone/docs/modeling.md` already says. `Grant.lite`'s `tokenHash` becomes `@unique(global) @guarded @system`, and so do the four drafts.
  - **Recommend A** — the fact is the app's contract with the column and has to be stated somewhere both graders can read; B hides it in a heuristic, C undoes `FJS-D819` for the case it was written for, and A's marker is the only one of the three that lets advise still catch the genuine uncreatable `token String @guarded`.

- ~~**FJS-D1134 — How does `advise` tell a bearer's bare claim from one a `membershipClaim` resolver supplies — a marker on the claim, a quieter rule, or no rule?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-21 (`FJS-D1134`): A — the claim says who carries it: a word on the declaration (`claim contactId bearer`), and the rule fires only for a claim so marked. A bare `claim` stays "the app resolves it" and is never graded. Costs one `.lite` word (catalog row, tier, axis, reference); keeps `FJS-1954`'s catch for the help-desk shape.** `allow-needs-bearer-claim-under-gate` (`advise.js:1266`) reads every `claim x` with no `from` source as a bearer's, so `claim teachingCourseIds` + `@@gate("0.4")` + `@@allow('read', id in auth().teachingCourseIds)` warns "admits nobody" (30 times on moodle, 9 vaultwarden, 6 posthog, 3 etsy) while the resolver supplies it to a signed-in caller who passes the gate. The schema holds nothing that separates the two: both are a bare `claim`, and the resolver and `bearerClaim()` are app code (`FJS-3042`, `FJS-1954`). Red test: that schema yields one finding today and must yield none.
  - **A** — the claim says who carries it: a word on the declaration (`claim contactId bearer`), and the rule fires only for a claim so marked. A bare `claim` stays "the app resolves it" and is never graded. Costs one `.lite` word (catalog row, tier, axis, reference); keeps `FJS-1954`'s catch for the help-desk shape.
  - **B** — keep the rule, state it as a condition and lower it to `info` (the `isStandingModel` precedent: a resolver is app code, so the finding is phrased "unless a resolver supplies it"). No new word; still 30 notes on moodle, so the noise moves rather than goes.
  - **C** — remove the rule. `FJS-1954`'s shape is caught only by an app's drive (chatwoot's `widget.test.ts` found it), as before the rule.
  - **Recommend A** — the fact is the app's contract with the claim and must be written where advise can read it; B keeps the false statement in softer type, C gives up the one dead-rule class the rule caught.

- ~~**FJS-D1140 — When does a header-resolved Grant get its `lastUsedAt` stamped?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1140`): B — the resolver stamps when the column is null or older than a fixed window (four minutes, a constant, no option), so a key in use shows a recent time and a hot key costs one write every four minutes. Cookie bearers inherit it too, or stay redeem-only. Answers "is this stolen key in use" to within four minutes, not the call.** Found fixing FJS-3080. `bearerClaim`'s resolver (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts:4336`) only reads; the one stamp is in `redeem()` (`:4493`), which an `authorization()` / `header()` bearer never calls (`:4468` refuses it). So a posthog `ProjectKey` used for ~50,000 creates keeps `lastUsedAt` null, and `Grant.lite` says "stamped by the exchange". The red test: a `bearerClaim({ from: authorization() })` model declaring `lastUsedAt`, one resolved call, then `asSystem().<model>.findUnique(...).lastUsedAt` is not null — null today. `FJS-D819` built "once per redeem rather than per request" on purpose, for a cookie bearer whose link is the one-time act; a key has no such act, and the resolver runs on reads.
  - **A** — the resolver stamps on every resolved request, when the row declares the column (`IDEAS/bearer-access.md` § lookup form says "touches `lastUsedAt` when it declares that"). Simplest and exact, but one `asSystem()` write per request on the read path — 50,000 calls are 50,000 writes, and a read-only replica or a GET inside a transaction now writes.
  - **B** — the resolver stamps when the column is null or older than a fixed window (one minute, a constant, no option), so a key in use shows a recent time and a hot key costs one write a minute. Cookie bearers inherit it too, or stay redeem-only. Answers "is this stolen key in use" to the minute, not the call.
  - **C** — a third act beside `mint`/`redeem`: the app calls `bearer.touch(db, ctx)` where it means "used". The resolver stays a pure read, but a path that forgets leaves the column null, which is this row's defect moved into app code.
  - **Recommend B** — the question the column answers is "is this key in use", which a minute's resolution answers at one write a minute; A buys per-call exactness nobody reads at 50,000 writes, C relies on app memory the resolver already has.
- ~~**FJS-D1145 — How does allocating an `@@extensible` slot avoid a whole-tenant scan when the slot is not the slot index's leading column?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1145`): A — the allocator records each slot it hands out, per (model, tenant, scope), in a table Litestone owns, never on the app's declaring model, which would gain rows the app never wrote. A slot that is neither in that record nor in the `taken` set the allocator already reads has never held a value, so it needs no clear. A slot in the record clears, and takes the scan, exactly as today. A tenant's first fill of its pool (every Posthog declaration) costs nothing. Reuse pays one scan per reuse. The record is written by the one path that allocates, not by the delete, so a declaration deleted outside the client still leaves its slot recorded and clearing on reuse. A slot STATED on a raw insert skips the allocator, so it goes unrecorded unless it is still taken. Invariant 1 holds.** `FJS-3141`. `allocateExtSlot` (`packages/litestone/src/core/client.js`) clears the freed slot with `UPDATE … WHERE "tN" IS NOT NULL AND <tenant> = ? [AND <scope> = ?]` on every allocation, because nothing records whether the slot ever held a value. `FJS-3134` made that clear seek the slot index, but only `t1` FOLLOWS the tenant and scope columns directly in `(tenant, scope, t1, t2, n1, t3…)`. For `t2`, `t3` and the rest, SQLite recomputes the VIRTUAL column from the row (DeferredSeek, then Column+json_extract on the table cursor), so the plan is `projectId=?` and reads every row: 425–464 ms at 2M rows, and 55–116 s for each later Posthog declaration at 10M. Red assertion: `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` of the clear for the SECOND text declaration names the slot column (`… AND t2>?`), or no clear runs at all; today it reads `projectId=?` only.
  - **A** — the allocator records each slot it hands out, per (model, tenant, scope), in a table Litestone owns, never on the app's declaring model, which would gain rows the app never wrote. A slot that is neither in that record nor in the `taken` set the allocator already reads has never held a value, so it needs no clear. A slot in the record clears, and takes the scan, exactly as today. A tenant's first fill of its pool (every Posthog declaration) costs nothing. Reuse pays one scan per reuse. The record is written by the one path that allocates, not by the delete, so a declaration deleted outside the client still leaves its slot recorded and clearing on reuse. A slot STATED on a raw insert skips the allocator, so it goes unrecorded unless it is still taken. Invariant 1 holds.
  - **B** — move the clear off the declaration: the delete marks the slot dirty and clears it in rowid-chunked transactions outside the declaring write. The allocator skips a dirty slot until its clear finishes. The writer is never held, even on reuse, but Litestone owns no job runner (Caravan sits above it, Invariant 1). So the chunking is a new resumable loop inside Litestone, and a crash mid-clear needs the dirty mark to survive and resume.
  - **C** — one index per slot, `(tenant, scope, tN)`, so every slot leads one and the clear seeks as `t1`'s does today (0 ms). No new record or state, but each extensible write maintains one index per slot (10 in Posthog's pool, not 1). It also drops the composite index's one-term reach that `allocateExtSlot`'s "in INDEX order" comment is built on.
  - **Recommend A** — the measured cost is all first-fill, where a slot provably never held a value, and A makes exactly that case free with one record written by the owner that already exists (`allocateExtSlot`). B pays for a job loop to fix reuse, which nothing measured yet. C taxes every write to fix allocation, which is rare. A's guard is that a reused slot still never carries the old key's values: `FJS-1752`'s reuse test keeps it red-capable, and `FJS-3134`'s EXPLAIN test extends to assert that a first fill issues no clear.

- ~~**FJS-D1149 — How does a `@@tenant` view's tenant term reach the tables its `@@sql` reads?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-17 (`FJS-D1149`): A — Litestone narrows the sources: a tenant read of a `@@tenant` view inlines its `@@sql` as a subquery under CTEs that shadow each row-tenanted model's table with `SELECT * FROM main."<table>" WHERE "<column>" = ?`, bound to the reader's tenant. A `@@tenant(none)` model is not shadowed. There is no author syntax, nothing is derived from reading `@@sql`, and the read deny stays as the backstop. The cost is that a view which deliberately aggregates across tenants (a benchmark) answers over the reader's rows only, and a `@@tenant(via:)` model's shadow needs the parent join.** Found fixing FJS-3140. A tenant read of a view is `SELECT … FROM "view" WHERE <tenant> = ?` (the row-tenancy read deny, compiled against the view's declared columns, `FJS-970`), and SQLite 3.53 pushes that WHERE into a view through at most ONE aggregate or window level. Probed with `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN`: a single `OVER (PARTITION BY projectId, …)` gets `SEARCH event … (projectId=?)`, but a `GROUP BY projectId` over a subquery, a window under a `GROUP BY`, and posthog's `checkoutFunnel` (window → window → GROUP BY → GROUP BY) all `SCAN event` with no tenant term below the outermost scan. So a tenant's read computes every tenant's rows: 122 s for the 500,000-event tenant, against 2.7 s with the term inside the SQL. The author cannot place it, because the view's SQL has no way to name the reader's tenant.
  - **A** — Litestone narrows the sources: a tenant read of a `@@tenant` view inlines its `@@sql` as a subquery under CTEs that shadow each row-tenanted model's table with `SELECT * FROM main."<table>" WHERE "<column>" = ?`, bound to the reader's tenant. A `@@tenant(none)` model is not shadowed. There is no author syntax, nothing is derived from reading `@@sql`, and the read deny stays as the backstop. The cost is that a view which deliberately aggregates across tenants (a benchmark) answers over the reader's rows only, and a `@@tenant(via:)` model's shadow needs the parent join.
  - **B** — the author places the term: `@@sql` may name the reader's tenant as a bound parameter (for example `:tenant`). A view that names it is inlined at read instead of being a `CREATE VIEW`, since SQLite refuses a parameter in a view. This is new syntax, and the scan comes back whenever an author forgets it.
  - **C** — no mechanism, just a `data-hazards` entry plus an `advise` finding: a `@@tenant` view whose `@@sql` nests an aggregate or window over a row-tenanted model is a full-table read per tenant read. The answer is `@@materialized @@refreshOn([…])` or a service method's own query.
  - **Recommend A** — the tenancy block already states which tables are tenant-scoped and by which column, so the narrowing can be derived rather than restated per view. It also makes *a tenant read never computes over another tenant's rows* true inside the view's SQL. Today only the projected rows are filtered, so an aggregate over other tenants still reaches the reader. The guard is an `EXPLAIN` test (the `FJS-499` precedent) asserting that a tenant read of a nested view `SEARCH`es the base table by the tenant column.

- ~~**FJS-D1233 — How does the kit draw a record page (a fixed aside beside a fluid main, the aside collapsible to nothing with a « toggle, the actions ruled off at the main's top end): a new vocabulary term, a modifier on an existing one, or no CSS at all?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-18 (`FJS-D1233`): A — a new Page-tier term (working name `Record`: `<div class="record">` holding `.record-aside`, `.record-main`, `.record-actions`), in `vocabulary.js` with ANATOMY and a wizard route. The aside width is `--record-aside-width`, a published knob, and collapsed is read from the toggle the app already owns: `.record:has(> .record-aside-toggle[aria-expanded="false"])`, with the `«` glyph flipped by the same attribute. Costs a ninth Page-tier noun and a guide page, and FJS-2687's ribbon would want to be a part of it.** Found fixing `FJS-2686`. `.split` is a one-axis flex row with no widths, `.shell` positions the app frame only, and `.pane` is a page section with no columns, so nothing in `packages/css/src` lays two columns with a collapsed state; the red test is `vocabulary.spec.js` plus a `frame.spec.js` case asserting a `1fr` main and a fixed-width aside that reads `0` inline-size when collapsed, and any answer adds a term or a rule the vocabulary must name (`New vocabulary is a design decision, not a class`). The collapse state is the second fork: the kit ships no JS, and `.sidebar-toggle` already settles that opening is the app's job, so CSS can only read a state the app sets.
  - **A** — a new Page-tier term (working name `Record`: `<div class="record">` holding `.record-aside`, `.record-main`, `.record-actions`), in `vocabulary.js` with ANATOMY and a wizard route. The aside width is `--record-aside-width`, a published knob, and collapsed is read from the toggle the app already owns: `.record:has(> .record-aside-toggle[aria-expanded="false"])`, with the `«` glyph flipped by the same attribute. Costs a ninth Page-tier noun and a guide page, and FJS-2687's ribbon would want to be a part of it.
  - **B** — no new term: `Pane` gains a `.aside` modifier (`.pane.aside`, children `.pane-aside` / `.pane-main`). Reuses the noun, but a Pane is `<section aria-labelledby>` with one heading and the record page is a page inside the Screen, so the modifier changes what the element IS (the "treatment listed on the wrong element" trap, `CLAUDE.md`).
  - **C** — no CSS: write the recipe in the guide (a `.grid` with `grid-template-columns: var(--aside) 1fr`, a `.bar` for the action row, `aria-expanded` on a `.btn`) and ship nothing. Zero surface and no noun, but the collapsed state is exactly what the row names as hand-built per app, and the recipe is the restatement every app maintains.
