  - **B** — Keep the booleans (they are exclusive, short and already ranked) and rename only `keygen --env` so `--env` names no thing but an environment; document `--flag=value` as the one example spelling, with `--as <person>` as the stated exception.
  - **C** — Keep all three as they are and only fix the examples to `--flag=value`; the row closes as a documentation pass.
  - **Recommend A** — one name for one thing: `--production --stage` can both be passed and need a priority rule (`deploy/_module.md:81`), where `--env <name>` cannot be contradictory and `--env` stops meaning a file.

- **FJS-D1271 — Where does a body's encoding live when one API takes JSON and raw MIME on the same host and credential: per call, a derived sibling target, or stay per target?** Found fixing `FJS-2700`. `TargetDescriptor.encoding` (`packages/conduit/src/types.ts:167`) is read once per send from the descriptor (`transports/http.ts:460`), and its comment rules that a provider wanting two encodings is a second target. Graph `/sendMail` takes JSON, and the same endpoint takes base64 MIME as `text/plain`, so ELA declares two targets with the same address and `ref`. The red test: a send to a `json` target with `encoding: 'binary'` on the call still goes out as `application/json`.
  - **A** — A per-call `encoding` on the send request, defaulting to the target's. Serialization stays inside the transport, so the signer still hashes the sent bytes (`FJS-D153`). It adds an option and loosens "a fact about who is on the other end", and the idempotency-field and `binary` checks in `http.ts` then depend on the call as well as the target.
  - **B** — No new option on a send. A target may declare `extends: '<id>'` (or the connector helper spreads the descriptor), so the sibling restates only `encoding` and the address/auth/headers/resilience stay one origin. This keeps the per-target rule and fixes the duplication, but it coins a relation between targets.
  - **C** — Keep it as is. The second target is the stated design, and `ref` already shares the credential cache, because `withCache` keys on the ref. `FJS-2700` closes as won't-fix, with the two-target shape recorded in the conduit hazards. The cost is the duplicated declaration, which ELA has already paid.
  - **Recommend C** — the second target costs one declaration and no token, since both name the same `ref`. A and B each add a surface to a rule that was written down on purpose, and nothing else has needed it. Build B only if a third connector (gmail batch is the next, per `IDEAS/conduit-connectors.md:254`) repeats the duplication.

- **FJS-D1272 — How does an app decorate the document title the router writes: a title function, a prefix signal, or leave it to `afterNavigate`?** Found fixing `FJS-2812`. `_applyTitle` (`packages/sierra/src/router/index.js:638`) assigns `document.title` after every navigation, and `afterNavigate` hooks fire after it (`:1069`, `:1076`), so an app can re-apply a prefix there — but it cannot see the router's undecorated title, only `document.title`, which already carries the last prefix. The red test: with a decoration of `t => '(2) ' + t` set once, `await goto('/orders/')` leaves `document.title === 'Orders'`, expected `'(2) Orders'`; and changing the count afterwards (no navigation) must re-write the title.
  - **A** — `setTitleDecoration(fn)` exported from the router (returns an unsubscribe). `_applyTitle` keeps the undecorated title in a module variable, writes `fn(title)`, and the app calls the same setter (or a returned `refresh()`) when its count changes. One new verb, no reactivity inside the router.
  - **B** — `page.titlePrefix`, a field on the existing `page` proxy that the router composes and re-writes on change (`watchProxy` already makes `page` reactive, so an app's effect sets `page.titlePrefix = '(2) '`). No new export, but it adds a field to the page contract that is a write surface rather than a read one, and covers prefix only.
  - **C** — No router change; expose the undecorated title as `page.title` (it is read-only today and `title` stays an ordinary frontmatter key per the comment at `:633`), and the app composes in an effect plus `afterNavigate`. Smallest surface, but the app owns two writers of `document.title` racing the router's, which is the thing the row is about.
  - **Recommend A** — the router stays the one writer of `document.title` (Invariant 4's one-owner shape), the decoration is a function of the title so it covers a suffix or a replacement as well as a prefix, and a signal-driven app calls the setter from its own effect.

- **FJS-D1273 — Does `css-raw-literal` grade a static `style="…"` attribute, and what does an app say instead of `max-width: 28rem`?** Found fixing `FJS-1670`. The scaffold half is already fixed (`crud-templates.js` writes `class="container narrow"` since `49d22c9d`); what is left is the check. `css-raw-literal` (`packages/cli/core/checks.js:4020`) reads `<style>` blocks only, and its own test fixture (`checks.test.js:5016`) carries `<p style="color: #fff">` that is deliberately not graded. Red assertion: a `.mesa` with `<form style="max-width: 32rem">` yields one `css-raw-literal` finding; today it yields none. Widening it flags about 35 files in `example/` and `basecamp/` (`max-width: 28rem` on forms, `--tile-min: 12rem`, `gap: .5rem; margin-bottom: 1rem`, `list-style: none; padding: 0`), most with no class that says the same thing.
  - **A** — Grade it: scan `style="…"` in markup with the same `RAW`/`NAMED` patterns, blank `{…}` expressions (data) and exempt a custom-property declaration (`--tile-min`) the way `<style>` exempts a size token. Move the ~35 sites to `container narrow`-style terms or a `<style>` block in the same change.
  - **B** — Grade only the generated shape: leave `style` attributes alone and add a `crud-templates` test that no scaffold output carries `style=`. The scaffold stops regressing; hand-written pages keep the escape hatch.
  - **C** — Treat a static `style=` as a missing term: grade it AND require css to ship a measure/gap vocabulary first (`.narrow` exists; `.measure-sm`/`.gap-*` do not), so every finding has a class to name.
  - **Recommend C** — a check that names a fix the kit cannot spell is noise (the `FJS-1430` breakpoint lesson), and `.narrow` shows the kit already answers "what is it" for a form width.

- **FJS-D1274 — How does a sealed child hold a column the grader writes after the seal?** Found fixing `FJS-3126`. `Attempt.answers` is `@sealed` and `submit` is `@seals`; `sys.attemptAnswer.update({ data: { earnedPoints: 100 } })` throws `SealedDocumentError` at `submitted` (`sealWhereClause`, `core/client.js`), by design (`FJS-D167`: a seal is not lifted by `asSystem()`). The grade is written at `submitted -> graded`, after the seal, so the per-answer verdict has no home on the child. Red assertion: after `submit`, `sys.attemptAnswer.update({ data: { earnedPoints: 100 } })` resolves; today it throws. The seal guard is a table-wide predicate on the child, while `_selfFreezes` is per-column, so the exemption has to be per-column on the child's write.
  - **A** — A child-column argument naming the parent's later move: `earnedPoints Float? @sealed(until: grade)` (or `@unsealed(until: grade)`). The column stays writable while the parent is in the seal's states and before the named move's target, frozen from there on; every other column of the child stays sealed. `sealMap` carries the exempt keys per parent and `sealWhereClause` skips the guard when the payload names only exempt keys. It is the mirror of `@immutable(after:)` (`FJS-D568`): same closure (`frozenAfterMove`), same move-name check at parse, but on the child, where the column is.
  - **B** — A parent-side exception on the relation: `answers AttemptAnswer[] @sealed(except: [earnedPoints], until: grade)`. The document's declaration stays in one place (the parent), at the cost of a field list on a relation naming columns of another model, which a rename of the column drifts from.
  - **C** — No language change: document the `AnswerGrade` model outside the sealed set (or drop the column, as moodle does). Neither shows the student which answer lost points without a second table, and the graded document stops being one document.
  - **Recommend A** — the column is what is being annotated, so the word lives on it and a rename moves it along; it reuses `FJS-D568`'s closure and parse check, so no new noun (a second argument form on `@sealed`, which already names the children).

- **FJS-D1275 — How does `@rank` spell a flat list with no scope?** Found fixing `FJS-3062`. `parser.js:1387` refuses a bare `@rank` by name ("needs the columns it orders within"), and `@rank(scope: [])` dies in `parseFieldList` with `Expected IDENT, got ']'`, so a statuspage's plain ordered list has no spelling but an Int. The engine already copes with an empty scope (`lastRankIn` drops its `WHERE` when `scope` is empty, `client.js:3601`); the validator, `x-rank`, `rebalance()` and `resource.move({ into })` are the places to check. The red test: `parse('model Item { id Int @id  pos String @rank }')` returns `valid: false` today and should be valid with `ranks: [{ field: 'pos', scope: [] }]`.
  - **A** — Bare `@rank` is the flat list: no parens means an empty scope. `scope:` stays the word for a partition, so the attribute with an argument says something and the one without says nothing is partitioned. Cost: a forgotten `scope:` on a per-parent list now parses, and the lists merge into one global order (still correct within each parent, only the keys run longer). `FJS-D556` refused bare `@money` for the opposite reason, a default nothing stated.
  - **B** — `@rank(scope: [])` is the flat list: an empty list states "no partition" and a bare `@rank` stays refused, so the omission is still an error. Cost: a spelling nobody guesses, and `@sequence(scope:)` would need to agree on `[]` or the two siblings disagree.
  - **C** — Keep refusing both: a flat list is `@@order`'s or an Int's job. Cost: the row stays as filed, and a drag-sortable list with no parent gets an Int that `resource.move()` cannot mint between.
  - **Recommend A** — a flat list is the zero-argument case of "ordered within a scope", the engine and `resource.move({ before, after })` already handle it, and the refusal protects against a mistake whose worst result is longer keys, not a wrong order. `@sequence` takes the same bare form if it has the same row.

- ~~**FJS-D1276 — When a host model redeclares a field a trait supplies, does the parser refuse it, warn, merge the two, or keep dropping the trait's silently?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-21 (`FJS-D1276`): E — refuse a silent redeclare, and let `@override` on the host field mark a deliberate whole-line replacement: `expiresAt DateTime @override`. The refusal names the word: `Model 'M': field 'a' is declared by the model and by trait 'T' — mark it @override or remove one`. Probed 2026-10-20: 4 schemas redeclare a trait field (haven `Session.expiresAt`, immich `Share.expiresAt`, moodle `Course.titlePt`, statuspage `MaintenanceWindow.startAt`/`endAt`), every one a deliberate tightening with a comment saying so; each adds one word. TypeScript's `noImplicitOverride` and Kotlin's `override` are the shape.** `resolveTraits` (`packages/litestone/src/core/parser.js`, "Host wins") filters the trait's field out when `model.fields` already names it, and `packages/litestone/docs/traits.md` § Conflict resolution states it as the intended behavior ("dropped silently", for tightening a default's `@map`). Statuspage wrote `startAt DateTime @zoned(timeZone)` on a `Window` host expecting to ADD `@zoned` to `Window.lite`'s field; the host's line replaced the trait's whole declaration (type, `@time`/validators and all) and nothing said so. Red test: parse `trait T { a String @lower }  model M { id Int @id  a String  @@trait(T) }` and assert the override is surfaced (an error, a warning naming `M.a` and `T`, or `a` carrying `@lower`) — today the parse returns no error, no warning and a field `a` without `@lower`. Precedent running the other way: `FJS-D455` refuses an `extend model` that redeclares a field.
  - **A** — refuse by name, as `extend` does: `Model 'M': field 'a' is declared by the model and by trait 'T' — remove one`. One rule for both splice paths, no silent drop. Cost: the documented "tighten a default with a host `@map`" use is gone; a host that wants a different column name edits the trait or declares no trait.
  - **B** — keep host-wins, make it LOUD: `resolveTraits` records each drop on the schema (`model.traitOverrides: [{ field, trait }]`) and validation emits a warning `M.a overrides trait T's field of the same name (the trait's declaration is dropped, not merged)`. Nothing breaks; an intentional override carries a standing warning unless an attribute silences it, which is a new option.
  - **C** — merge: the host's field is laid over the trait's, attributes unioned (host wins per attribute kind, as `@@gate` already does at model level), so `a String @zoned(...)` ADDS to the trait's `a`. Matches what statuspage meant; cost is a second composition rule beside host-wins for whole models and a type-mismatch case (host says `Int`, trait says `String`) that must be refused anyway.
  - **D** — rule the status quo: host wins silently is the spelling, `packages/litestone/docs/traits.md` already says so, and the row is documentation. Rejected by the row itself: the override changed what the field IS and nothing reported it.
  - **E** — refuse a silent redeclare, and let `@override` on the host field mark a deliberate whole-line replacement: `expiresAt DateTime @override`. The refusal names the word: `Model 'M': field 'a' is declared by the model and by trait 'T' — mark it @override or remove one`. Probed 2026-10-20: 4 schemas redeclare a trait field (haven `Session.expiresAt`, immich `Share.expiresAt`, moodle `Course.titlePt`, statuspage `MaintenanceWindow.startAt`/`endAt`), every one a deliberate tightening with a comment saying so; each adds one word. TypeScript's `noImplicitOverride` and Kotlin's `override` are the shape.
  - **Recommend A** — `FJS-D455` already ruled that a copy which diverges silently is the failure for `extend`, and a trait is the same relationship (a fragment composed into a model); the one use A loses has a clean spelling (change the trait, or make the field the host's alone), and it needs no new option or warning channel out of `resolveTraits`.

- **FJS-D1277 — How does a policy refusal name the row or value it refused: a message template, one message per clause, or the caller's own hint?** Found fixing `FJS-3033`. `policyVerdict` (`packages/litestone/src/core/policy.js:1261`) returns the one `message` of the refusing rule — for an allow list, the first allow that has one (`:1291`) — and `checkCreatePolicy` throws it as the 403 body (`:1320`). A Review create policy of seven AND-ed clauses therefore answers six different callers with one sentence, four of them falsely. Red test today: two orders from one shop, one delivered; create a review on the undelivered one and on a smuggled line — both refuse with the identical message and neither names an order.
  - **A** — Message template: `@@allow('create', expr, "Order {order.id} is not delivered")` interpolates `{path}` read from the row being written, resolved against the declared schema at parse (an unknown path is refused by name, Invariant 8 — nothing a caller sends is ever a pattern). Cost: the message crosses the error boundary to a screen, so a path across a relation can print a value the caller may not read; it would have to be graded by the same `$readAs` redaction as a read, which is a second reader of the gate.
  - **B** — One message per clause: split the policy into one `@@deny` per failed condition (each already takes its own message), and make `policyVerdict` return the FIRST deny that fires rather than collapsing an allow list to one sentence. Cost: no language change, but an AND of seven clauses is seven denies written as negations, and the OR semantics of `@@allow` still yields one message.
  - **C** — The message stays a constant and the refusal carries the clause: `AccessDeniedError` gains `clause` (the index/source text of the failing sub-expression, server-side like `hint`), so a log and the audit trail say WHICH of the seven failed while the 403 body stays non-leaking. Cost: the person on the screen still reads the constant sentence; only the operator learns more.
  - **Recommend B** — it reuses a message slot that already exists and a verdict that already walks denies in order, so every clause names its own failure with no new interpolation grammar and no value leaving the gate; A's leak surface is a second reader of the gate (Invariant 6), and C answers the operator but not the caller the row's title is about.

- **FJS-D1278 — How does a thrown FrameworkError class carry `retryable`: a fourth constructor argument, an options object, or a subclass per meaning?** Found fixing `FJS-2987`. `FrameworkError` declares `retryable?: boolean` (`packages/junction/src/core/errors.ts:21`) but every class takes only `(message, data, cause)`, so `payments.service.ts:162` and `:278` throw `Object.assign(new Error(..), { status: 502, retryable })` and `mailer.ts:83` throws `{ kind, retryable }` beside a class set one import away. The red test, whichever spelling is ruled: a thrown `BadGateway` marked retryable has `.retryable === true` and carries it in `toJSON()`; today no class can be built that way, so the example assigns it by hand.
  - **A** — A fourth positional argument on each class: `new BadGateway(m, d, c, retryable)`. Smallest diff, but a boolean in fourth place is unreadable at the call site and every class line grows.
  - **B** — A fourth options object on each class: `new BadGateway(m, d, c, { retryable: true })`. Readable at the call site and room for a later field, but fifteen class lines change and the three-argument tail `(m, undefined, undefined, {…})` is what every caller writes.
  - **C** — A static on the base class: `BadGateway.retryable(message, data)` / `Conflict.retryable(...)` returning an instance with the flag set. No constructor change, reads as the meaning at the call site, one method on `FrameworkError` rather than fifteen edits.
  - **Recommend C** — the flag is a property of a particular throw, not of a class (`Conflict` is retryable for a moved row and not for an illegal transition, `FJS-D194`), so it belongs beside the constructor rather than in its positional list.

- **FJS-D1279 — How does a schema say a claim is read per request off the ONE membership row a request names, when the scope is not a tenant — a spelling in `.lite`, or only in `membershipClaim`?** Found fixing `FJS-3282`. `validateClaimSources` (`parser.js`, `claim … from`) refuses `claim role from Enrollment(userId)` because `userId` is not unique on `Enrollment` (a person is a teacher in course A and a student in B), and a bare `claim role` is accepted but graded as a bearer's by `allow-needs-bearer-claim-under-gate` (`FJS-D1134`). The row a request names is chosen by a value the request carries (a course id), which only app code can read; `membershipClaim({ tenantFrom, model, subject, tenant })` does exactly this for a tenant column and has no non-tenant scope. Red test: `claim role from Enrollment(userId)` with `@@unique([userId, courseId])` parses with no error and `advise` reports no bearer finding — it is refused today.
  - **A** — a schema spelling: `claim role from Enrollment(userId, courseId).role`, the last subject member being the column the request supplies; the parser accepts a composite subject that is unique together, and `membershipClaim` takes `scopeFrom` for the request's value. Costs a grammar extension, a catalog row, a validation branch, and a second resolver option beside `tenantFrom`.
  - **B** — no new spelling: generalize `membershipClaim` to a non-tenant scope (`scope: 'courseId', scopeFrom`), keep `claim role` bare in the schema, and let `FJS-D1134`'s answer (a word saying who carries a bare claim) stop `advise` grading it as a bearer's. One word total across both rows; the schema still does not say which row the claim is read from.
  - **C** — model the scope as a tenant (a course is a tenant under `strategy row`). Reuses everything, but a person in many courses is a many-tenant principal, which `FJS-D361` limits to one `@@relator` shape, and Moodle's non-course models are not course-scoped.
  - **Recommend B** — the row is chosen by a request value only app code reads, so a `.lite` spelling would describe a resolver it cannot run; `FJS-D1134` already owes the word that makes the bare claim honest, and one word serving both rows is the simpler cut. Confidence 2: A is the reopen path if a drive shows the schema must name the row.

- **FJS-D1280 — What spells 'frozen from CREATE' on a model that has a `@seals` move, where bare `@immutable` means frozen at the seal?** Found fixing `FJS-3125`. On a sealing model bare `@immutable` is writable until the seal (`FJS-D167`, `jsonschema.js` `immutable-until-seal`), and `@immutable(after: <move>)` starts the freeze at a later move (`FJS-D568`, `seal.js` `frozenAfterMove`); nothing starts it EARLIER than the seal. Red test (moodle `Attempt`: `userId`/`quizId`/`startedAt` `@immutable`, `@seals` move `submit`, row policy `userId == auth().id && status == 'inProgress'`): the sitter's own client `update({ data: { quizId: other } })` on an open attempt returns the row rewritten today; expected `ImmutableFieldError`. A row policy cannot compare old with new, so the app cannot write it either.
  - **A** — one more argument on the existing word: `@immutable(at: create)` is frozen from create on any model, sealing or not; bare keeps its two meanings. Parser gets a second key beside `after:` (the two are exclusive), the client's freeze guard skips the seal lookup for it, `jsonschema.js` emits `readOnly` + `immutable` as on a plain model, and `db:explain @immutable` describes four forms. Smallest delta; `FJS-D167` and `FJS-D568` stand as ruled.
  - **B** — retire the context-dependent meaning: bare `@immutable` is frozen at create everywhere, and the seal-scoped freeze is spelled `@immutable(after: <seal move>)`, which `FJS-D568`'s closure already computes identically to the seal's. No new argument and one meaning per word, but it reverses `FJS-D167`'s pricing, every sealing model's bare `@immutable` (the three `@seals` schemas in this tree, `example` invoices among them, and the linked prototypes) must name its move, and a document with many frozen columns repeats the move name on each.
  - **C** — a second attribute (`@frozen`) for the create-time freeze. The word D568 declined (option B there); a new noun beside one that already means "frozen".
  - **Recommend A** — it adds the missing spelling without reopening two rulings or touching any existing schema; B is the cleaner language and the reopen path if a third spelling of "frozen" is ever asked for. Confidence 2.

- ~~**FJS-D1281 — Does a locked child (`@@gate delete 9`) travel with a parent's `onDelete: Cascade`: refused, allowed and spelled, or left as it is?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-19 (`FJS-D1281`): A — the lock travels: a parent delete (`delete`, `deleteMany`, hard `remove`) is refused with `ForeignKeyError` naming the locked child, as Restrict would, whenever the cascade would take a row whose model grades LOCKED (9) at delete for this caller. `asRestrictedDelete`'s walk already follows the Cascade edges, so the check is one more pass; the parent becomes undeletable while any post exists, which is the append-only reading taken literally.** Found fixing `FJS-3120`: statuspage's `IncidentUpdate` is `@@gate('0.4.9.9')`, so `'IncidentUpdate.delete'` is LOCKED for staff, admin and `asSystem()`, yet `admin.incident.delete({ where: { id } })` removes the incident and every post on its timeline (posts left: 0), because SQLite runs the cascade inside the parent's DELETE. The trail records each child row, so it is not silent. Today's rule is `FJS-D845`'s reading for field cascades — a cascade is integrity, the parent's gate authorized the cause, the child's gate is not checked — which nothing in the schema tells a reader of the child. The red test's assertion: after the parent delete, `IncidentUpdate` count for that incident is 0 where the gate says 9 (or the delete throws, under A).
  - **A** — the lock travels: a parent delete (`delete`, `deleteMany`, hard `remove`) is refused with `ForeignKeyError` naming the locked child, as Restrict would, whenever the cascade would take a row whose model grades LOCKED (9) at delete for this caller. `asRestrictedDelete`'s walk already follows the Cascade edges, so the check is one more pass; the parent becomes undeletable while any post exists, which is the append-only reading taken literally.
  - **B** — the cascade stays integrity and the schema says so: a relation spelling (`onDelete: Cascade` plus a declared `@@gate` note, or `@relation(..., cascadeFrom: parent)`) that marks the child "append-only except through its parent", and `litestone check` warns on a locked child under a Cascade relation that lacks it. No behavior changes; a reader of the model can tell.
  - **C** — leave behavior and add the sentence to `data-hazards` § gates: a locked `@@gate delete 9` grades the verb, not the effect; use `onDelete: Restrict` for a child that must outlive its parent.
  - **Recommend A** — the gate reads "nothing may remove a post" and a lock the parent walks around is a lock in name only; `FJS-D845` was about a parent WRITE reaching a child the caller may not write, where refusal vetoed legitimate work, whereas here the author declared the child undeletable and Restrict is the spelling they would have to reach for otherwise. Confidence 2.

- ~~**FJS-D1282 — What stops a model named `<Other>Update` (or `Create`, `Where`, `OrderBy`, `Relations`) from colliding with the generated types of `<Other>` in `schema.d.ts`?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-19 (`FJS-D1282`): A — refuse: `generateTypeScript` (and `litestone check`) throws naming both declarations when two generated names are equal, e.g. "model `IncidentUpdate` collides with `Incident`'s update type". Smallest delta, no consumer moves; the author renames the timeline model (`IncidentPost`), and an ordinary noun stays unusable as a suffix.** Found fixing `FJS-3118`: `typegen.js` spells a model's row `pascal(name)` and its write/read shapes `pascal(name)` + `Create`/`Update`/`Where`/`OrderBy`/`Relations` as sibling top-level declarations, so statuspage's `Incident` + `IncidentUpdate` both emit `export interface IncidentUpdate` and TypeScript merges them (`incident.update({ data: { status } })` fails TS2739 for `incidentId`, `body`, `postedAt`). Red test's assertion: `generateTypeScript` on a schema with `Incident` and `IncidentUpdate` declares `interface IncidentUpdate` once; today twice. No suffix is collision-free while the row owns the bare name, and `XUpdate` is an ordinary noun (an append-only timeline).
  - **A** — refuse: `generateTypeScript` (and `litestone check`) throws naming both declarations when two generated names are equal, e.g. "model `IncidentUpdate` collides with `Incident`'s update type". Smallest delta, no consumer moves; the author renames the timeline model (`IncidentPost`), and an ordinary noun stays unusable as a suffix.
  - **B** — namespace the shapes: `Incident` stays the row and gains `Incident.Create` / `.Update` / `.Where` / `.OrderBy` / `.Relations` (an interface and a namespace of one name merge legally). A collision becomes impossible, but every importer of `IncidentCreate` etc. moves in the same change (`typegen.js` client map, `testing.d.ts`, `cli` templates, junction, the linked prototypes).
  - **C** — a longer, less-nouny suffix (`IncidentUpdateInput`). One rename across callers, but the collision is only rarer and a model could still take it.
  - **Recommend A** — it ends the silent merge with a message that names the fix and moves no caller; B is the right shape if renaming a timeline model proves common. Confidence 2.

- **FJS-D1283 — How does a store learn that a write it already uploaded for was refused: a new Observer hook on `LitestonePlugin`, an upload moved after the INSERT, or no engine fix?** Found fixing `FJS-3095`. `ExternalRefPlugin.onBeforeCreate` calls `serialize` (so `provider.put`) before `prepareCreate` finishes and before the INSERT; the plugin protocol has no hook for a write that fails after that, and `onAfterWrite` fires only on success. Probe: a `Doc` with `@@unique([sha])` and a `File?` column, two creates of the same `sha` through a recording `ExternalRefPlugin` — the second is refused (`sha "a" is already taken`), `puts` is `["one", "two"]`, `cleaned` is `[]`. The red test asserts `cleaned` holds the second ref after the refusal. An update that serializes a new value and is then refused leaves its new object the same way.
  - **A** — a new Observer hook `onWriteFailed(model, operation, args, error, ctx)` on `LitestonePlugin` (tier per `FJS-D06` §1: receives, cannot act; a throw in it is swallowed so the caller still sees the original error). The table calls it from the create/update paths when the write throws after `beforeCreate`/`beforeUpdate` returned. `ExternalRefPlugin` records each ref it serializes in a flavor-keyed stash (the shape its update stash already has, `FJS-722`) and its `onWriteFailed` runs `_cleanupRef` over them. One new word on a published base class; covers create and update.
  - **B** — move the upload after the INSERT: the row is written with a reference computed from the key, and `onAfterWrite` does the `put`. No new hook, but a failed `put` leaves a row naming an object that does not exist (worse than an orphan, and `onAfterWrite` is outside the write's transaction, `FJS-D588`), and `FileStorage` could no longer hand `serialize` a value the INSERT needs (size, content type, a hash the key pattern uses).
  - **C** — no engine fix: the orphan is a storage-root reconciliation the app runs (list provider keys, diff against rows), and `data-hazards` § storage plus `FileStorage`'s docs say a refused write leaves its object. Nothing coined; the storage root still grows by one object per refused write.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only option that deletes the object at the moment the engine knows it is unowned, and the stash/cleanup machinery it needs already exists in `ExternalRefPlugin`; B trades an orphan object for a dangling row.

- **FJS-D1284 — How does a write declare that it must cover a complete set — a schema attribute on the column, a transaction option the caller states, or no engine spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3066`. Vaultwarden's account-key rotation replaces `User.key` AND every `Cipher`, `Folder` and `Send` of that user in one write, refused if any row is missing; nothing in `.lite` or the client says a batch must name a complete set (`@@check` reaches one row, `@@transitions` one column), so the only spelling is `$transaction` (`client.js` `$transaction`) plus a hand count in the service. Red test, written once a shape is ruled: inside `db.$transaction`, update `user.key` and rewrite two of a user's three ciphers — today it commits; it must refuse and roll back, leaving `user.key` unchanged.
  - **A** — a schema attribute on the column: `key String @sealed @rewrites(Cipher.userId, Folder.userId, Send.userId)`; a write to `User.key` is refused at commit unless every row of each named set, by that relation to the user, was written in the same transaction. Declared once, enforced at the Data boundary (Invariant 6), visible to `db:explain`; costs grammar, a catalog row, a parser validation branch, and a per-transaction written-row ledger the client does not keep today.
  - **B** — a caller-stated option on the transaction: `db.$transaction(fn, { covers: { cipher: { userId }, folder: { userId }, send: { userId } } })`; at commit the client counts the rows written per model under that filter against rows existing and refuses a short set, rolling back. One owner of the count, no grammar, and the service says in one line what it was hand-counting; but the schema does not say the set must be covered, so a second service that forgets the option rotates a partial vault.
  - **C** — no engine spelling: `data-hazards` names `$transaction` plus a count as THE spelling for a complete-set write, and Junction may offer a `ctx` helper that does the count. Nothing coined; the rule lives in each service, which is the status quo the row calls a gap.
  - **Recommend A** — every sibling guard on a write (`@@transitions`, `@@commitment`, `@immutable`, `@@check`) is a schema attribute enforced at the Data boundary, and B's rule lives in the caller, so a second service that forgets the option rotates a partial vault with nothing reporting it (§ V 9); B is the reopen path if the ledger A needs proves too costly. Confidence 2.

- **FJS-D1285 — What declares that a recurring wall-clock rule fires exactly once per occurrence across a DST gap and overlap: a schema attribute that owns the once-ness CAS, a caravan schedule read from a row, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3060`: statuspage's `MaintenanceWindow` (weekly 01:30 in `Europe/Lisbon`) is a recurring rule with no row per occurrence, so `@@transitions`' compare-and-swap has nothing to swap and `@@commitment` fires on a column's instant, not a rule. Measured 2026-10-16 (`measure/dst-window.mjs`, `TZ=America/Denver`): both hand-written spellings open once on spring, ordinary and autumn Sundays — (a) a caravan cron `30 1 * * 0` with `timeZone`, (b) a per-minute sweep CASing a `lastOpenedOn @date @zoned @system` column — so once-ness is reachable today and only the declaration is missing. Red assertion owed: a schema stating the rule once opens one occurrence on each of the three Sundays with no hand-written `lastOpenedOn` column. `FJS-D143` refused the general RRULE and any subset must earn its name; `FJS-D312` made the calendar Caravan's.
  - **A** — a litestone attribute on a model, `@@recurs(on: <wall-clock fields>, zone: <path>, opens: <move>)`, whose firing litestone owns: it keeps the per-model last-fired occurrence (a framework-owned `@system @date @zoned` column added the way `@@hasTemplates` adds its own) and CASes it, then runs the named move as `asSystem()`. Closest to `@@commitment`; cost is a second clock mechanism in the language and the first recurrence grammar (weekly + time-of-day), which `FJS-D143` said must be a named subset, not RRULE.
  - **B** — no litestone grammar; Caravan reads the rule off a row: `handle({ cron, timeZone })` is given a per-row source (`schedules(model, { cron: 'cronField', timeZone: 'zone.path' })`) and its existing `cron:<job>:<epoch-minute>` key is the once-ness. The CAS stays Caravan's, the schema only marks which columns are the cron and the zone (`@cron` validator beside `@zone`). Smallest new surface, keeps `FJS-D312`'s one owner; cost is that the wall-clock gap (spring's 01:30 fires at 02:00) is Caravan's rule and the schema cannot state "skip" versus "slide".
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` § transitions names the hand-written `lastOpenedOn` CAS or the fixed-time cron as THE spelling, with the Lisbon measurement as its proof, and the Window trait gains the missing duration form (`opensFor`) so a 01:30–03:00 window is not 1h and 2.5h by accident. Nothing coined; every app keeps writing the same 20 lines.
  - **Recommend B** — `FJS-D312` already put the calendar in Caravan and the measurement shows its key gives once-ness on all three Sundays, so the missing piece is only the row-to-cron binding; A builds a second scheduler inside the Data realm for one grammar. Confidence 2: if a second consumer needs the occurrence as a queryable row, A wins.

- **FJS-D1286 — How does a schema say "this move is refused while a related row is in a state" — a guard on the move, not on the state the write lands in?** Found fixing `FJS-3059` (statuspage Q3 / C4): `@@deny('post-update', status == 'resolved' && component.lastCheckOk == false)` guards the move `resolve`, but grades the state the write lands in, so retitling an already-resolved incident while the component fails again is refused with the deny's message. `@@commitment`'s `while:` reads this row alone and fires a move rather than refusing one. `@@transitions` (`parseTransitionsArg`, `parser.js:3112`) takes only `declaredBy:`/`scope:` arguments and `name: from -> to` entries, so there is no place a per-move guard could be written today. Red assertion owed: with `Incident` `resolved` and its component failing, `db.incident.update({ title })` succeeds while `db.incident.transition(id, 'resolve')` from `open` is refused. Costs 2–4 of the row (same 403 code as a seniority refusal, a stale copy column `lastCheckOk` because the monitor is two hops away, 21 s flip) stay under A and B unless the guard reads the relation live.
  - **A** — a guard clause on a `@@transitions` entry, `resolve: open -> resolved while: component.lastCheckOk != false` (or `unless:`), graded only when the CAS moves the column from `from` to `to`, reading this row and ONE hop (the `FJS-D356` one-hop reach of policy paths, `FJS-499`). Refusal carries its own code (not `ACCESS_DENIED`), `transitions(row)` reports `refusedBy: 'guard'`. Cost: a second predicate position beside `@@commitment while:` with different reach rules, and a new refusal code through the Data→API error owner.
  - **B** — no new grammar: give `@@deny` / `@@allow` a move-aware term, `moving(resolve)` (true only when the write is that transition), so `@@deny('post-update', moving(resolve) && component.lastCheckOk == false)` says the rule once in the language the schema already has. Reuses the policy grader and its one-hop paths; cost: a function that reads the write's shape, which no policy term does today (`auth()`, `now()`, fields), and the refusal is still `ACCESS_DENIED`.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` § transitions names the post-update deny as THE spelling, with the retitle-while-failing refusal as its stated cost, and the app guards the retitle with a second `pre-update` allow. Nothing coined; the staleness and the state-grading stay the app's.
  - **Recommend B** — the policy grader already owns one-hop reads and `asSystem()` and `transitions(row).refusedBy:'policy'` already greys the button, so the missing piece is only "this write is that move", which a term supplies without a second predicate grammar; A is the stricter reopen path if a refusal code distinct from `ACCESS_DENIED` is wanted. Confidence 2: if a second consumer needs the guard to be listed in `x-transitions` for the client, A wins.

- **FJS-D1287 — How does a schema say a move posts a child row, so the column and its timeline are one write by construction, and what stops a direct post that disagrees with the column?** Found fixing `FJS-3058` (statuspage C3): `Incident.status` is stored and moved by `@@transitions`, `IncidentUpdate` carries a copy of the status after each move, and nothing ties them. Measured: `incident.transition(id, 'identify')` alone moves the column and posts nothing; `IncidentUpdate.create({ status: 'resolved' })` on an identified incident is accepted; `postedAt` is writable at create, so a backdated post reorders the projection. A `@from(IncidentUpdate, last: true)` cannot carry the machine (the CAS needs a stored column) and a `@@check` reaches one table. The red test is `transition(id, 'identify')` leaving `updates.length === 0`.
  - **A** — a clause on the move, `identify: investigating -> identified posts IncidentUpdate(incidentId: id, status: to)`, whose row the engine inserts in the move's own transaction after the CAS wins (so a lost CAS posts nothing), with the named child columns `@system`-filled and refused on a direct `create` by the existing `@system` rule. Closes (1) by construction, (2) because `status` is no longer caller-writable on the child, and (3) if `postedAt` is declared engine-stamped like `@stamps`. The post's `body` comes from the call: `transition(id, 'identify', { post: { body } })`, required when the clause has a non-defaulted column.
  - **B** — the child declares the tie, no clause on the move: `@@follows(incident.status, copy: status)` on `IncidentUpdate` grades a create against the parent's CURRENT column (one hop, the `FJS-D356` reach), and a parent move with no matching child row in the same `$transaction` is refused at commit. Closes (2) and (1) but needs a commit-time check the engine does not have, and a deferred check is a second enforcement point beside the CAS.
  - **C** — no grammar: `data-hazards` § transitions names the service's `$transaction` pair as THE spelling, adds `postedAt @system` and a `@@require` comparing the child's `status` with the parent's as the stated closure of (2) and (3), and says (1) is the service's to keep. Nothing coined; seam (1) stays open for any second caller of `transition`.
  - **Recommend A** — `@stamps`, `@system` and `@@transitions` already put every sibling guard on a move in the move's own `UPDATE`, and A is the only option where a caller using `transition()` directly cannot reach the half-written state; B adds a commit-time check beside the CAS. Confidence 2: if a second consumer needs the row posted conditionally, A's clause wants a `when:` and C looks smaller.

- **FJS-D1288 — What word keeps a readable column's value out of the audit trail when the value is not a person: a new attribute, an argument on an existing one, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3057` (statuspage C1): `Monitor.url` carries a key in its query string, staff must READ it, and the trail must not keep it. `logMarks` in `makeTable` (`src/core/client.js`) is fed only by `isProtected` (`[redacted]`, and every protected field is hidden from a non-system read), `p.personal` (`[personal]`, `FJS-D657`, a value about a person) and `p.opaque` (a digest, `FJS-D1158`, bytes a client sealed), so no combination is readable AND unlogged. The red test's assertion: a `url String @<word>` field is returned by `findMany` for a staff reader, and the `create` entry's `after.url` reads the mark and never `https://x?key=1`.
  - **A** — a new field attribute that is only a trail mark, the shape `@personal` already is: `url String @redacted` logs `[redacted]` in field entries and in `before`/`after`, changes nothing about who reads it (`@omit` and the gate stay that question), and lands as one more flag in `buildFieldPolicyMap` (`src/core/schema-maps.js`), the owner `export.js`, `testing.js` and `query.js` already read. The data map reports it as kept from the trail. It collides with nothing: `@personal` + `@redacted` logs `[redacted]`, as protected + personal already does.
  - **B** — an argument on the trail's own field attribute: `url String @trail(audit, value: false)` (alongside `reads:`/`writes:`), so the trail's whole grammar stays in the one word and nothing is coined; the entry still names the field and the mark is `[redacted]`. Cost: `@trail` is today synthesized for protected fields and parsed only to name a database, so the argument is a second meaning for a word `@@trail(audit)` also carries at the model grain, and a model with `@@trail` and no field `@trail` has no place to write it.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` § audit trail names the split as THE spelling (the key goes in `secret String @encrypted`, the URL stays clean, as the statuspage draft already does), and `FJS-1485`'s second instance is closed as an app-modeling rule. Nothing coined; a URL with an inline key still reaches the trail the first time an app forgets, and nothing reports it.
  - **Recommend A** — `@personal` is the sibling that decided this shape (a flag in the field-policy owner, a mark in the trail, no effect on who reads), and the word has to be a plain description of what the trail does because `@sensitive` is refused by name (`IDEAS/data-classification.md`, graded levels carry no behavior); C leaves the leak silent, which is the question-9 failure. Confidence 2: if the owner would rather not add an attribute whose only effect is `[redacted]`, B is the same behavior inside an existing word.

- **FJS-D1289 — How does a model state that its offline writes are PATCHES only, when its rows are created by another protocol (an upload)?** Found fixing `FJS-3055` (immich C6): `Asset.isFavorite` is toggled on the phone offline and must replay, but an Asset row is only ever created by the upload protocol, and its `File` is required. `@@sync(<policy>)` (`parser.js` `case 'sync'`, `SYNC_POLICIES`) is one collision policy per model over every operation; `server`/`refuse`/`field` make the create syncable too, so `sync-required-file` (`advise.js`) warns and the offline create is refused, while `append` is the one operation-shaped member and is wrong for an update. Today the only way to hold the patch is to give up the file's required-ness or split the toggle onto a second model. The red test: a model with a required `File` and `@@sync(refuse)` grades `sync-required-file`, and no spelling makes it say "updates only" without the warning.
  - **A** — an operation argument on the existing word: `@@sync(refuse, on: update)` (`on:` takes `create`/`update`/`delete`, default all), so the policy still comes from the closed set and the direction rides beside it as `@@sync(read)` already did (`FJS-D488`). The offline queue refuses a create or delete not listed, `x-sync` crosses as `{ policy, on }`, and `sync-required-file` and `sync-server-key-file` skip a model whose `on:` omits `create`. One word, one place to look.
  - **B** — a second policy member that names the shape: `@@sync(patch)`, "held for update only, collision policy `server`", beside `read`. Smallest parse change, but it fixes the collision policy at `server` so an Asset cannot also be `refuse` or `field`, and every later "update-only with X" needs another member — the set grows by product.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` names the split as THE spelling — the offline-toggled columns move to a sibling model (`AssetFlag`, one-to-one, client-mintable id, `@@sync(field)`) and the upload-created `Asset` stays unsyncable. Nothing coined; the cost is a join on every read of a favorite and a model that exists only to dodge the grammar.
  - **Recommend A** — `FJS-D488` already put direction on `@@sync` as an argument rather than a second word, and the collision policy and the operations are independent axes, which B collapses; C is the status quo the row calls a gap. Confidence 2: if no second app has a model created elsewhere and patched on a device, C costs less than a grammar.

- **FJS-D1290 — How does a wall clock written with a fixed offset (EXIF, ISO `+05:00`) state how it was read: can `@zoned` name an `Int @unit(min)` offset column, a new word, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3054` (immich C5): `Asset.takenOn`/`takenTime` are `@zoned(takenZone)`, but the camera wrote an OFFSET, which `@zone` refuses by design (`FJS-D143`: a day read in a fixed offset is not the same day once a rule changes), so the offset sits in `takenOffsetMin Int? @unit(min)` and no `@zoned` read resolves it. Red assertion owed: `due()` over a day-kind `@@commitment` whose `@date` is `@zoned(takenOffsetMin)` reads the day at that row's offset, where today the parser refuses the path (`@zoned(…) must be a String @zone`).
  - **A** — the same word, a second target type: `@zoned(path)` accepts an `Int @unit(min)` column as well as a `@zone` String, validated at parse (type, and a range of -720..840 minutes at write), read per row as a fixed offset by the one resolver `due()` already uses. No new noun; `@zone` keeps refusing an offset, so the IANA rule stands where a FUTURE moment is computed, and an offset is only ever the record of a moment already observed. Cost: `@zoned` then names two kinds of target and `db:explain @zoned` must say which reads which.
  - **B** — a second attribute for the second thing: `@offset(takenOffsetMin)` on the wall clock, beside `@zoned`, so the zone word keeps meaning IANA only and a reader sees which is which. Cost: two words that answer the same question (*in what frame is this wall clock read*), the restating the decision rules refuse.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` § transitions names the split as THE spelling — the app stores the instant (`takenAt`, already there) and the offset as plain data, and a day-kind commitment on an offset wall clock is not offered. Nothing coined; the cost is that EXIF/ISO wall clocks stay a column litestone describes and no read resolves, which is the row's complaint.
  - **Recommend A** — `FJS-D512` already made `@zoned` the single declaration a reader resolves a wall clock by, and an offset is the same fact in a narrower form; B is a second word for it and C is the status quo the row calls a gap. Confidence 2: if no second app records a wall clock by offset and nothing reads it but `due()`, C costs nothing and A is a parser branch with one reader.

- **FJS-D1291 — How does a schema say a column may only grow (a resume offset a replayed chunk never moves backwards): a new attribute, a before-image term in a policy, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3052` (immich C3): `Upload.received` has `@@check("received <= size")` for the ceiling, but a `post-update` rule grades the row the write would leave and never the row it replaces, so `update({ received: 10 })` over a row at 40 is accepted. Red test: create an Upload at `received: 40`, `update({ received: 10 })` — passes today, must refuse.
  - **A** — a column attribute, `received Int @monotonic` (`Int`/`BigInt`/`DateTime`, optionally `@monotonic(down)`), enforced the way `@@transitions` and the `version` column already are: the update's `WHERE` is narrowed by `AND "received" <= :new`, so a lower value matches no row and the write is refused as a named error (`MonotonicError`, 409) in the same statement, race-free under concurrent chunks. A replayed chunk is refused, not silently dropped; a `$increment`/`$max`-style write is naturally legal. `@immutable` is the sibling that froze a column outright, so this is its "may move one way" form. Cost: one more attribute, and the choice of REFUSE over CLAMP (keep the larger) is made for every user.
  - **B** — no new column word: give `@@deny` / `@@allow` a before-image term, `old.received`, so `@@deny('update', received < old.received)` says the rule in the policy language the schema already has, and also covers rules `@monotonic` cannot (*status may only go up by one*, *total never below paid*). Cost: the policy grader today evaluates a `post-update` rule in JS over the merged row (`FJS-D356` one-hop reach); a before-image term must also compile into the UPDATE's `WHERE` to stay race-free, which is the larger change, and it is a second predicate grammar if `@@transitions` stays.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` names a service `before` hook that reads the row and refuses as THE spelling, with the read-then-write race named as its cost (two concurrent chunks can both pass), and the row is closed as an app-modeling rule. Nothing coined; the one place the invariant is not at the Data boundary (Invariant 6).
  - **Recommend A** — the case is a column whose whole contract is a direction, `@immutable` and `version` already enforce their half of the same family in the UPDATE's own `WHERE`, and A is the only option that is both declared in the schema (Invariant 6) and race-free without a new predicate grammar; B is the stronger reopen if a second app wants a before-image rule that is not a direction. Confidence 2.

- **FJS-D1292 — How does a File column state the hash of its bytes, so "the stored bytes hash to the value the client claimed" is a constraint: a new attribute, an always-recorded digest on the stored ref, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3051` (immich C2): `Upload.sha256` / `Asset.sha256` is the never-send-twice identity, and `serialize` in `src/storage/file-storage.js` returns a ref of `{ key, name, bucket, provider, endpoint, size, mime, uploadedAt }` — `resolveType` sniffs the TYPE for `@accept`, and no digest is computed, so the sha256 column is whatever the client typed. Today a create whose `sha256` disagrees with its `file` stores both without complaint.
  - **A** — a field attribute naming the sibling column, `file File @digest(sha256)` (the column is a sibling `String` the schema declares, `sha256 String @digest(of: file)` is the other direction): `serialize` hashes the bytes as it streams them to the provider, the stored ref gains `sha256`, and a create/update whose sibling column is present and disagrees is refused by name (a `ValidationError` on the sibling field); absent, the column is FILLED from the bytes. Declared in the schema (Invariant 6), one hash walk, and the column is the constraint. Cost: a `.lite` word, a catalog row, and a place on the `FromPath`/inline/Blob read paths to tap the stream.
  - **B** — no new word: `serialize` always records `sha256` on the ref (`ref.sha256`), and a policy/`@@check` term reads it (`file.sha256 == sha256`). Nothing coined and every File gets content identity for free, but the hash walk is paid by every upload including ones that never asked, and the policy grammar has no term that reads a ref's member today (one-hop paths read rows, not a JSON ref).
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` names the service check (hash the bytes, compare to the column) as THE spelling and the row closes as an app-modeling rule. Nothing coined; the guarantee stays one an app can forget, which is the row's complaint.
  - **Recommend A** — the question is "which column is the hash of which bytes", a fact about the schema, and `@accept` already set the shape of a File attribute that grades what `serialize` saw; B makes every upload pay for a digest and needs a policy term that reads inside a ref, C is the status quo. Confidence 2: if no second app has a content-identity column, C costs nothing and A is a parser branch with one reader.

- **FJS-D1293 — How does a schema say two rows reference one stored object (one copy of the bytes, freed when the last reference goes): a File attribute that keys the object by digest and guards cleanup, a Blob model the app refcounts, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3050` (immich Q2/C1): a File column is the row's own — `serialize` in `src/storage/file-storage.js` mints a fresh key per upload and the cleanup at `file-storage.js:374` deletes the object when its row's ref goes — so "exactly one stored copy, two ownership rows" is one copy per row, and the draft settles for `@@unique([ownerId, sha256])`. The red test's assertion: two creates of the same bytes on two rows store two objects today, and deleting one row deletes the bytes the other still names.
  - **A** — a File attribute that makes the key a function of the bytes and guards cleanup: `file File @shared` (keyed by the `FJS-D1292` digest; two rows holding the same bytes hold the SAME ref and neither owns the object alone). Cleanup deletes the object only when no other File ref in the schema names its key — one scan of the schema's File columns, at the owner of the cleanup. The column stays the row's own, so FK-read (`FJS-D576`) never enters: there is no Blob row to be unreadable. Cost: a `.lite` word, a catalog row, a cross-column reference count in a tx, and it leans on D1292 A (a digest to key by).
  - **B** — no litestone change: the app models `Blob { sha256 @id, file File @keepVersions }` with ownership rows holding an FK to it, and a hook refcounts. Nothing coined, but a Blob keyed on the hash has no owner a row policy can name, so an ownership row's caller cannot read the Blob through `@@gate("8")` (`FJS-D576` answers it as missing) and the app reaches for `asSystem()` — the exact thing Invariant 6 says the schema should have declared.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` states one copy per row as the contract and the row closes as an app-modeling rule (`@@unique([ownerId, sha256])` is the answer). Nothing coined; storage cost grows with every owner of the same bytes, which is the row's complaint.
  - **Recommend A** — the fact is "these two refs are one object", which is a statement about the schema and not about a hook; B forces `asSystem()` around a read the schema could grade, and C is the status quo. Confidence 2: A is only worth a word if a second app has cross-owner dedup, and it should land with D1292 A or not at all.

- **FJS-D1294 — How does a schema say a column's every former value stays a readable row (every slug a post ever had, read by the router): a column attribute naming the target model, a model attribute, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3036` (ghost C4): `Post.slug` is renamed by its author and every value it ever held must still answer as a redirect, so Ghost carries a `Slug` model a `posts.service.ts` hook fills (after create/update/patch, in the write's transaction, the `Slug` create lifted with `system: ['@@gate']`). Measured there: rename x3 then back gives exactly four `Slug` rows; but `asSystem()` renaming a post records nothing, so the address it renamed TO 404s after the next rename. The words that exist each miss: `@@trail(audit)` is another file at gate 8 (unreadable by a router, `FJS-2738`), `@@effective` is a window nothing writes on change, `@keepVersions` is File-only. A write-side word is the only form that closes the seed and the `asSystem()` paths, since the hook lives in a service and a seed never enters one.
  - **A** — a column attribute naming the model that receives the values: `slug String @unique @history(into: Slug)`, where `Slug` is an ordinary model the schema declares (`value String @id`, `postId`, `createdAt`), with its own gate and `@@allow`, so a router reads it at the level the schema gives it. On create, and on any update that changes the column, the same transaction appends `{value: <old or new>, <fk to the owner>}` through the client's own write path under the system grade, so no hook, no `asSystem()` and no caller can skip it. The target's `@id`/`@unique` on the value gives "a second post renamed to a former slug is refused 409" for free. Cost: one attribute, one write-path branch beside `@updatedAt`/`@version`, and a parse rule that the target has exactly one relation back.
  - **B** — a model attribute, `@@history(slug, into: Slug)`, the shape of `@@trail(db)` and `@@effective(from:, to:)`: the same mechanism as A, but the Post model carries it and may name several columns (`@@history([slug, handle], into: Slug)`). Cost: a second way to say a thing that is a fact about one column (the restating `decision-rules` refuse), and a multi-column form has no second app asking for it.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` states the hook (`after create/update/patch` in the write's transaction, `system: ['@@gate']`) as THE spelling and the row closes as an app-modeling rule, with the `asSystem()` rename named as its cost. Nothing coined; the guarantee stays one a seed or a tinker script can skip, which is the row's complaint, and it is the one place Invariant 6 (access and history declared in the schema, not hooks) is left to a service.
  - **Recommend A** — the fact is about one column and its target ("every value this column held is a row of that model"), which is what `@updatedAt` and `@immutable` already state as column attributes, and it is the only form that is at the Data boundary (Invariant 6) so the seed and `asSystem()` paths record too; B restates it at the model, C is the status quo. Confidence 2: if no second app keeps a column's past values as rows a reader joins (history-as-readable is Ghost, and maybe a handle or a SKU), C costs nothing and A is a parser branch with one reader.

- **FJS-D1295 — Under `tenancy { strategy database }`, how does a schema say one sqlite database is marketplace-wide (a buyer, a cart, one payment that belong to no shop): a database-block option, a model attribute, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3032` (etsy C2): `TenantClient` (`packages/litestone/src/tenant.js`, the `sqliteOverrides` loop) points EVERY sqlite database at the tenant's own file and only `jsonl`/`trail` stay schema-global, so a global table runs no policy engine and has nowhere to live but a second app. Red test: a schema with `database main {}` plus `database market { path "./market.db" }` under `strategy database`, two tenants, one `Buyer` row written through tenant a and read back through tenant b — fails today, because each tenant sees its own `market` tables.
  - **A** — a database-block word, `database market { path "./market.db"  scope global }`: the registry leaves it out of `sqliteOverrides`, opens it once for the process (not once per tenant, not in the LRU pool), and the tenant client reaches it as the same `db.<model>` accessor with the full policy engine. The rule jsonl/trail already follow by driver becomes a stated word that sqlite can take too. Cost: one option, and a `$transaction` spanning a global and a tenant database is two files (`FJS-D232`), which must be refused or stated.
  - **B** — a model attribute, `@@tenant(none)` read under `strategy database` too: the model's rows go to a shared file the registry opens beside the tenants. Reuses a word `strategy row` already has, but routes by model what `FJS-D232` says is routed by database (a file), so one schema would say it two ways.
  - **C** — no spelling: the closed-world answer is a second app that peers to the first (`FJS-D1188`), and `data-hazards` states that `strategy database` holds only tenant data. Nothing coined; the cost is the row's complaint, a buyer whose cart spans shops lives in a service a gate cannot see.
  - **Recommend A** — a database is a file (`FJS-D232`), so which file is shared is a fact about the database block, and jsonl/trail already make it implicitly by driver; saying it as a word is the narrowest change and keeps B's cross-model routing out. Confidence 2: if the transaction refusal proves to need its own design, C holds until a second app asks.

- **FJS-D1296 — Under `strategy row`, how does a model say a row is the tenant's AND a non-member's (an Order the shop and the buyer both read): a `@@tenant` option, an exemption on the generated deny, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3031` (etsy Q1): the desugar emits `@@deny(read, auth().shopId == null || shopId != auth().shopId)`, a buyer holds no `shopId`, and a `@@deny` beats every `@@allow`, so no `@@allow(read, buyerId == auth().id)` can reach the row. Today the only spelling is `@@tenant(none)` plus `shopId == auth().shopId` written by hand, which forfeits the `@default(auth().shopId)` stamp, the create-time wrong-tenant refusal and the `@@tenant` conformance grading on exactly the models a marketplace is made of. The red test's assertion: under `strategy row`, a model carrying `shopId` with `@@allow(read, buyerId == auth().id)`, read by a principal with no `shopId`, returns the row; today it returns `[]`.
  - **A** — a third `@@tenant` mode, `@@tenant(shared: buyerId == auth().id)`: the stamp and the create refusal stay, and the read/update/delete deny becomes `<tenant test> && !(<expr>)`, i.e. the shop's rows PLUS the rows the expression admits. One expression per model, written where the row is, and the deny is still generated (Invariant 6, enforced at the Data boundary). Coins one word, `shared`, on an attribute that already has `none`/`column`/`via`.
  - **B** — the tenant column's own exemption: `shopId String @default(auth().shopId) @tenantOrElse(buyerId == auth().id)`. Same generated deny, but the exception rides the column, so it cannot be written on a model that has no tenant column. A new field attribute for what is a model-level fact; no shorter than A.
  - **C** — no spelling: the closed-world answer is `@@tenant(none)` and a hand-written policy (what etsy does today), or a second app holding the buyer side (`FJS-D1188`). Nothing coined; the cost is the row's complaint, the stamp and the deny forfeited on Order, OrderLine and Refund. Could be made less silent by having `fli check` report a `@@tenant(none)` model that carries the tenant column and a policy reading it.
  - **Recommend A** — the exception is a statement about WHICH rows, so it belongs on the model beside `none`, and keeping it a deny with an excepting clause preserves the rule that tenancy narrows and never widens an allow (a `@@allow` could not be the spelling, `FJS-D05`). Confidence 2: if the `Admitted` twin (`FJS-D1100`) is meant to be the one door a non-member walks through, a `@@tenant(admit: …)` reusing that claim is the alternative to weigh first.

- **FJS-D1297 — What spells retention that FOLDS (sum raw rows older than a window into a rollup row, then drop them, the chart not moving): a model attribute, a hook on the sweep, or the app's own two jobs with the language grading only the XOR?** Found fixing `FJS-3030` (posthog Q7, statuspage Q5). `runSqliteRetention` (`retention.js:120`) only DELETEs by `createdAt`; `@@expires` hides and removes nothing; so the app writes fold-then-thin as two jobs (measured: foldDay 4-16 ms a day, deleteMany of 626,400 rows 4-5 s) and "a day's rows exist in DailyUptime XOR in Check" is a cross-table invariant no `@@check` reaches. The hole is the LATE row: a check row for a published day lands in Check, the sealed rollup refuses every write (`asSystem` included, `SealedDocumentError` 409), so 1437/1439 stays published while the rows say 1437/1440. Red test: a model with a window and a sealed rollup — a late row for a folded day is accepted by the raw model today and no spelling can refuse it or route it to the rollup.
  - **A** — a model attribute `@@retention(1y, into: DailyUptime, by: day(checkedAt), sum: [ok, total])`, the `FJS-D1059` window grain plus a fold target. The sweep sums the doomed rows into the rollup in the same transaction as the DELETE, and a raw INSERT whose clock falls in a folded span is refused by name (the XOR held at the Data boundary). Costs a grammar extension (target, bucket, aggregate list), a catalog row, and a rule for sealed (`@immutable(after: publish)`) rollups: the late row is refused, not folded into a sealed one.
  - **B** — a hook on the sweep: `$retain({ fold: async (rows, tx) => … })` or a schema `@@retention(1y, fold: 'uptime')` naming an app function. The app writes the sum, litestone owns the transaction and the cutoff (sum and delete commit together), and no aggregate grammar is added. The XOR stays the app's: a late row is still accepted.
  - **C** — no schema word; the app keeps its two jobs, and litestone only adds a refusal spelling for the late row (a `@@check`-style guard on Check naming the rollup model: `@@foldedBy(DailyUptime, day(checkedAt))`). Smallest; fold and thin can still diverge between the two jobs.
  - **Recommend B** — the sum is app arithmetic with one shape per product (uptime counts, event counts by property), so a closed aggregate grammar (A) would be rebuilt as hooks the first time a product folds a percentile, while B takes the one thing only the framework can give — sum and delete in one transaction — and leaves the late-row refusal as a separate row once a second product asks. Confidence 2: `onRemoved` in `runSqliteRetention` is the sibling that already hands removed rows to the app, but `FJS-D1059` set the other precedent, a schema word at model grain, so A is the reopen path.

- **FJS-D1298 — Is a merge (two rows become one, children re-pointed, the trail naming it once with both ids) a language/client act, or the app's own service method with a row of its own?** Found fixing FJS-3029. posthog's `identify()` moves every `Event.personId` from A to B and ends A; the trail records N `Event.personId` updates and one `Person` delete (FK strategy) or one `PersonDistinctId` update (alias strategy) sharing a `correlationId`, and no row says "A became B". The red assertion: after a merge, one trail entry exists whose action names the merge and carries both ids. Today none can — the trail's actions are create/update/delete/read. chatwoot already writes the same act as a `merge` service method (`contacts/merge.ts`) under `asSystem()`.
  - **A** — a client verb `db.$merge(accessor, { survivor, loser })`: in one transaction it re-points every FK that targets the model (derived from `buildRelations()`), deletes the loser, and writes ONE trail entry (`action: 'merge'`, `before` = loser image, `after` = survivor id). Gated as a write on both rows; `@@immutable` columns the re-point touches are refused unless named `@system`.
  - **B** — a model attribute `@@mergeable` that declares which relations re-point and which side wins, so the verb is only legal on a model that said so and `fli check` can grade the declaration; the trail entry is as in A.
  - **C** — no language surface: the app keeps the service method under `asSystem()` in a transaction and writes a `PersonMerge { survivorId, loserId, at }` row of its own, which IS the "A became B" record and is queryable by `survivorId`. Litestone adds only the documented convention (the shared `correlationId` ties the N updates to that row) in `docs/trail.md`.
  - **Recommend C** — two apps (posthog, chatwoot) spell it as a service method today and differ in what a merge moves (FK rewrite vs alias row vs revoking a grant), so a closed verb (A/B) would be rebuilt as hooks the first time a third shape arrives; C moves no callers and adds no noun, and A is the reopen path once a third app wants the same re-point set. Confidence 2.

- **FJS-D1301 — Does a rollup over a tree (a category's weighted mean of its children, recursive, with drop-lowest) get a declared spelling, or does the app keep the rollup job and litestone only says when the stored aggregate is stale?** Found fixing `FJS-3023` (moodle C5). `@derived` is a row-local SQL expression (`schema.md` § `@derived`) and `@from(rel, avg)` is one level with no weight, drop or recursion; a `view` can write the recursive CTE but is not graded per caller; so the draft stores the category grade as a `Grade` row on the category's own `GradeItem`, written by a rollup job, and no word in the seed says when that row is stale. Red assertion: after a child `Grade.score` changes, the parent category's stored aggregate is reachable as stale (or recomputed) from the schema alone; today nothing in `db/schema.lite` or the catalog names the dependency.
  - **A** — a model-grain aggregate over a tree: `@@rollup(into: Grade.score, children: GradeItem.parent, weight: weight, fn: mean, drop: lowest(n))`, recomputed in the same transaction as the child write and graded as the parent's row. Costs a grammar (relation, weight column, closed list of fns, drop spelling), a recursion bound, and the same closed-grammar risk `FJS-D1297` A names: the next product folds a percentile or a curve.
  - **B** — a hook seam like `FJS-D1297` B: `@@rollup(children: GradeItem.parent, by: 'gradeCategory')` names an app function; litestone owns the walk up the parent chain and the transaction (child write and recomputed parent commit together, parents visited leaf-to-root), the app owns the arithmetic. No aggregate grammar.
  - **C** — declare only the dependency: `@stale(on: Grade.score, marks: GradeItem.parentId)` or a catalog row (`rollupSources`) so `fli check` and the client can say "this stored aggregate depends on those rows" and a read can ask `stale: true`; the job stays the app's. Smallest; recompute is still a promise.
  - **D** — no language surface: the view (recursive CTE) is the declared value and the app reads it under `asSystem()` plus its own row policy filter; the stored `Grade` row is dropped. Nothing coined, but the read is ungraded per caller (the row's own complaint) and drop-lowest per level is hand-written SQL.
  - **Recommend B** — the arithmetic differs per product (weighted mean, drop-lowest, extra credit, curve), which is the argument `FJS-D1297` made for B, while the part only the framework can give is the transactional walk up the parent chain in leaf-to-root order, which a job cannot make atomic with the child write. Confidence 2: if a second tree-rollup (org-chart headcount, bill of materials) turns out to be sum/mean only, A's closed grammar is the reopen path; C is the first step if B is judged too much for one stressor.

- **FJS-D1302 — Who hands an unpacked stranger's package an origin of its own: a second listener junction owns, a Host-dispatched wildcard on the one listener, or Outpost's inline-app listener with the unpack job only recording the URL?** Found fixing `FJS-3022` (moodle C4). `serveStatic` (`packages/junction/src/transport/static.ts`) takes `untrusted: true` and answers `attachment` for anything outside the inline allow-list (`FJS-D314`), which a SCORM player's HTML and JS cannot be; the app's single `Bun.serve` listener is ONE origin, so serving the pages inline from it puts a stranger's script beside the session cookie. `FJS-D345`'s second listener is Outpost's (`packages/outpost/src/static.js`, 8181) and serves an App's `inline` source, not an app's uploaded rows. Nothing in junction can say "this directory is a different origin", so Moodle carries `Resource.packageOrigin @url @system` written by a job that has nowhere to point it. The red test's assertion: an unpacked package requested through the app's listener answers with a different `Origin` than the app's own, and a request carrying the session cookie to the package origin does not receive it.
  - **A** — junction owns a second listener: a static mount gains `origin: { port }` (or a `app.origin(name, { root, port })` owner) that starts a `Bun.serve` of its own over `root` with `untrusted` inverted — scripts and HTML are allowed because the origin is foreign — plus `Content-Security-Policy: sandbox allow-scripts` and no cookie ever set. The unpack job writes under `root/<resourceId>/` and `packageOrigin` is `http://host:port/<resourceId>/`. A port is an origin (`FJS-D345`'s argument, taken into junction); costs one more port in `docs/PORTS.md` and a third thing `runStartPhases` starts.
  - **B** — one listener, many origins: the mount dispatches on `Host` (`<id>.pkg.example.com`) to a root with the same sandbox headers; the session cookie is host-only so a package host never receives it. Needs wildcard DNS and TLS and does not exist on `localhost` dev without a hosts entry; no extra port, but the isolation rests on the app never setting a `Domain=` cookie.
  - **C** — the unpack job hands the unpacked files to Outpost as an `inline` App (its listener already is a separate origin, `FJS-D345`) and records the origin Outpost answers; junction and litestone add nothing. Works only on a fleet-deployed app, so `bun run dev` has no package origin, and the row's lifecycle (delete a Resource, delete the App) becomes two systems' business.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only spelling that works in dev, in a single-container deploy and under Outpost alike, and it takes `FJS-D345`'s own argument (a port is an origin) rather than a new one; B needs infrastructure an app cannot declare and C splits one row's lifetime across two owners. Confidence 2: if apps are always fleet-deployed, C costs nothing and A's port is the part to cut.

- **FJS-D1303 — What spells a column holding one value per locale: a field word that expands to a column per locale, a built-in `Localized` Json type the read paths understand, or no word and a documented trait?** Found fixing `FJS-3021` (moodle C1–C2). `FJS-D12` reserved the interface tier only; in the data tier a course title is `title` + `titlePt` spliced by a trait, the fallback to the default locale is a read rule nothing declares, `Question.choices` carries `text`/`textPt` by hand, and a third locale is 16 statements. Measured in `fjs-prototypes/moodle/measure/locale.mjs`: `@@label` and `orderBy` refuse a Json column as opaque, `@@fts` over Json indexes the KEYS (`FJS-3075`), and the pt sort is BINARY in every spelling (`FJS-2500`, a separate row — no spelling here fixes it).
  - **A** — a schema-level `locales(en, pt)` (default first) plus a field word `title String @localized`, desugared like `@@tenant` to one physical column per locale (`title`, `titlePt`). Required is stated for the default only; `@@label`, `@@fts`, `orderBy` and `where` on `title` resolve to `COALESCE(<reader locale>, <default>)`. Plain columns, so sort, index and FTS need no new engine support; the cost is that a third locale is still an ALTER per field plus an FTS rebuild (now derived by the differ, not hand-written), and a localized value INSIDE a Json document still has no spelling.
  - **B** — a built-in type `Localized` over Json (`{ "en": …, "pt": … }`), usable as a column AND as a member of another `@type`. Teach the three read paths to see through it: `@@label`/`orderBy` read `json_extract(col, '$.<locale>')` with the default-locale COALESCE, `@@fts` indexes the VALUES not the keys (closing `FJS-3075`), and the default locale's presence is a `CHECK (json_extract(col, '$.en') IS NOT NULL)` (no subquery, so SQLite allows it). A third locale is a `locales` edit and zero migration; one spelling covers the column and the nested case. Cost: an expression per read (an expression index, not a plain one, for sort), and the three read paths each gain a Json-aware branch.
  - **C** — no word: ship the trait (`Titled`) and a `fli check` rule grading that every `<name>Pt` column has its default-locale sibling, and document the fallback as an app read. Nothing coined; the row's complaint — fallback undeclared, nested value unspellable, third locale a migration — stands.
  - **Recommend B** — it is the only one that answers all four complaints (column, fallback, nested document, third locale) with one noun, and the `FJS-3075` fix comes with it; A leaves the nested case and the migration cost, C leaves everything. Confidence 2: if sort speed on a localized label matters more than the nested case, A is the smaller step and B can follow on top of it.

- ~~**FJS-D1304 — With `FRONTIER_` ruled (`FJS-D1102` C), what do `WEB_URL`, `VITE_API_URL` and a bare `PORT` become, and does the sweep wait for lockdown 3.3?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-19 (`FJS-D1304`): A — Every framework var is `FRONTIER_*`: `FRONTIER_PORT_BE/FE/…`, `FRONTIER_SOURCE`, `FRONTIER_API_URL`, `FRONTIER_APP_URL`; `WEB_URL` is deleted into `APP_URL`; junction reads no bare `PORT`; Sierra sets Vite's `envPrefix` to `['VITE_', 'FRONTIER_']`, so the bundle reads `import.meta.env.FRONTIER_API_URL` and `VITE_API_URL` is deleted. Every `FRONTIER_` var then reaches the browser bundle, so a future `FRONTIER_*` secret would leak into it.** Found fixing `FJS-2974`. The tree is untouched and the sweep is `FLI_PORT_*` in 50 files, `API_URL` in 51, `VITE_API_URL` in 34, `APP_URL` in 23, `WEB_URL` in 10, a bare `PORT` read in 21 (`envOverrides`, `packages/junction/src/config/index.ts` ~548). `FJS-D1102` C names the prefix and `FRONTIER_API_URL`, and says nothing of those three, which A had answered. The red test's assertion: `envOverrides()` with `PORT=1` and `FRONTIER_PORT_BE=2` yields `out.port === 2`; today it yields 1.
  - **A** — Every framework var is `FRONTIER_*`: `FRONTIER_PORT_BE/FE/…`, `FRONTIER_SOURCE`, `FRONTIER_API_URL`, `FRONTIER_APP_URL`; `WEB_URL` is deleted into `APP_URL`; junction reads no bare `PORT`; Sierra sets Vite's `envPrefix` to `['VITE_', 'FRONTIER_']`, so the bundle reads `import.meta.env.FRONTIER_API_URL` and `VITE_API_URL` is deleted. Every `FRONTIER_` var then reaches the browser bundle, so a future `FRONTIER_*` secret would leak into it.
