  - **C** — keep refusing. The refusal already names the construct and says what to write instead. The cost is one paste and one edit, and `FJS-966` closes as answered.
  - **Recommend C** — the cost of not having it is one error message that already tells you the fix, and A adds a language rule with one source (an exporter's paste) that only DOM backends would give a meaning (`FJS-D38`). A is the reopen path if a second case appears where pasted SVG has to stay byte-for-byte. Confidence 2.

- **FJS-D1349 — Does `ServiceContextLocals` keep its index signature?** `FJS-774`: the interface declares `paginate` and `readCall` and then `[key: string]: unknown` (`packages/junction/src/core/context.ts:394-403`), so `$.locals.brandng` compiles and answers `unknown`, where `app.brandng` is an error. Junction itself writes one key the interface does not declare, `ctx.locals.tenantId` (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts:5252`, `:5393`). TypeScript's `noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature` (https://www.typescriptlang.org/tsconfig/noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature.html) makes a dot read of an index-signature key an error (TS4111) and leaves bracket access legal.
  - **A** — drop the index signature. Junction declares every key it writes, `tenantId` included, and an app declares its own by augmenting `ServiceContextLocals`, the pattern Invariant 5 prescribes for `app.own()`. A typo is an error on read and on write. Cost: an app writing an ad-hoc key today must add a declaration first.
  - **B** — keep the signature, and `@frontierjs/config`'s tsconfig sets `noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature`, so a declared key reads by dot and an undeclared one only as `$.locals['x']`. Cost: the flag is app-wide and changes how every `Record` in the app is read, to fix one interface.
  - **C** — leave it as it is, and document that a misspelled read is silent.
  - **Recommend A** — Invariant 5 already chose augmentation as the way an app types its own key, and nothing has shipped, so breaking an unaugmented ad-hoc key is the evolution policy rather than a cost. What A moves is measured: basecamp writes `workspaceId` (`packages/basecamp/api/src/core/hooks.ts:105`, `:186`, and three services), `mintedToken` and `statusFilter` (`api-keys.service.ts:184`, `:207`), `apiKeyId` (`domain/api-key/scopes.ts:151`) and a `MEMBERSHIP` key (`core/hooks.ts:185`), which makes one augmentation block in that app; `example` writes none. B fixes one interface by changing the whole app. Confidence 2.

- **FJS-D1350 — How does a one-per-key worker queue its successor while it runs?** `FJS-2287`: `unique` is held among live jobs, `status IN ('pending', 'running')` (`jobs_unique_live`, `packages/caravan/src/db.ts:130`), so a re-dispatch from inside the running handler returns its own id and the chain dies. Serpgrid loops until drained and adds a one-minute cron watcher for a dispatch that lands between the last read and the end of the job. BullMQ answers the same case with `keepLastIfActive` (https://docs.bullmq.io/api/types/v6.DeduplicationOptions.html): a duplicate added while the job is active is kept, and a new job is created when the active one finishes, so at most one active and one waiting exist per id.
  - **A** — `unique` frees its key when the job STARTS (index over `pending` only). The successor queues, but it can then be claimed while its predecessor still runs, which breaks the one-per-key limit the worker exists for (serpgrid's vendor limit is per account).
  - **B** — a dispatch option keeps one follower: while the key's job is running, a dispatch under that key writes or refreshes one pending row, the claim query skips a pending row whose key has a running job, and the index becomes one pending plus one running per key. This is BullMQ's guarantee, and the option's spelling is settled when it is built.
  - **C** — no change. The loop plus a cron watcher is the documented pattern, and `FJS-2287` closes as by design.
  - **Recommend B** — it is the precedent's answer to exactly this case, it keeps the one-per-key limit A gives up, and it deletes a watcher every drained-key worker would otherwise write. It is an option, so `decision-rules` § adding an option applies when it is spelled. Confidence 2.

- **FJS-D1351 — How are realms and surfaces colored in dev output?** `FJS-1564` asks for one color table (realm and surface to hue, ANSI and hex) with one owner, never the only signal, degrading under `NO_COLOR` and 16-color terminals. It leaves three questions open: hue family against distinct hues, what cli gets, and whether Deployment and Testing get colors. A 16-color terminal has six chromatic hues (red, green, yellow, blue, magenta, cyan), each in normal and bright, and red is already the failure color across `fli`'s output. Prior art is thin and points the other way. Turborepo passes the task's own colors through and controls only whether the `<package>:<task>:` prefix appears (`--log-prefix`, https://turbo.build/repo/docs/reference/run), and process runners such as `concurrently` and `docker compose` hand out a color per process in order. That order-assigned coloring is exactly what the row replaces (`bun run --parallel`). No tool was found that colors by what a process IS.
  - **A** — one hue per realm, and the surface named in text beside it (`api`, `web`, `cli`). Five realms take five of the six hues; Testing stays neutral so red keeps meaning *failed*. This answers all three questions: cli is a surface and gets its name, and Deployment gets a hue.
  - **B** — a hue family per realm and a shade per surface. Six of the eight surfaces are UI, so this needs six distinguishable shades of one hue, which a 16-color terminal does not have and color-blind separation will not survive.
  - **C** — a distinct hue per surface, with realms uncolored. Eight surfaces are more than the six hues, so two share one, and red has to be one of them.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only option that fits the 16-color constraint the row itself sets, and it keeps the label the row requires anyway as the thing that tells surfaces apart. The row reads as a feature rather than a defect, so the ruling can also move it to an `IDEAS/` paper. Confidence 2.

- **FJS-D1359 — What signal lets `fli check` tell a `.mesa` screen that fills a list from `service.find()` from a kept one-row lookup, join or count?** `FJS-3346`: `FJS-D1318` ruled that a rule grades a route calling `.service.find(` to fill a table, and `FJS-2968` tried the text form (`find` feeding `<Table rows>`) which flagged two kept sites and missed every `{#each}` list. `.service.find(` appears in ~25 `.mesa` files today (basecamp settings, portal, activity, servers; haven Notices; example payroll, orders), and the call text is identical whether the result is a list, a one-row `.data?.[0]`, a `.total` or a join. The red assertion: a screen with `rows = (await x.service.find()).data` read by `{#each rows}` must grade, `total = (await x.service.find({}, { limit: 1 })).total` must not, and no text rule separates them. Existing precedent is the `service.get` rule in `packages/cli/core/checks.js` (bare assignment to an outer `let`, a warning that fails open).
  - **A** — the compiler gives it: Mesa exposes a script/template analysis (which top-level bindings are assigned from which call, which bindings `{#each}` or an attribute reads), and a `fli check` rule joins *assigned from `X.service.find(`* with *read by `{#each}` or `rows=`*. Exact for the list case, and a kept lookup reads `.data?.[0]` or `.total` so never reaches a list. Cost: a new compiler surface, and Mesa is the leaf with no notion of a resource, so the join lives in the rule.
  - **B** — the resource gives it: `service.find()` returns the envelope with the rows tagged in dev (a symbol on the array), and Sierra warns when a tagged array is iterated by a list block or handed to `<Table rows>`. Runtime-exact, but it is a dev warning rather than a check, grades only a route that runs, and adds a mark to the one door `FJS-D1318` keeps as the plain escape hatch.
  - **C** — the `service.get` shape: a text rule on bare assignment of `await X.service.find(` to an outer `let`, a warning that fails open, with `.data?.[0]`, `.total` and `{ composed: true }` reads exempt. Cheapest and no new surface; it still cannot see a list built through a local, which is the miss `FJS-2968` hit.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — the signal is already in the file (a binding and its readers), only the compiler parses a `.mesa` reliably, and C's misses are exactly the `{#each}` lists the ruling wants graded. B grades by running, which is what `fli check` exists not to need. C is the sibling-precedent pick (`service.get`, same domain) and the cheaper one; it is the fallback if A's compiler surface proves larger than one exported analysis, and either way the rule is a warning that fails open.

- **FJS-D1360 — What is an orion flow template: code the host registers, a table row, or a flow marked as one?** Found fixing `FJS-1196`: the mockup's `TemplatesPage` reads a hardcoded `TEMPLATES` array and orion has no template source, so the screen has nothing to list and "use" has nothing to copy.
  - **A** — code the host registers: `orion({ templates })` takes `{ id, name, category, description, flow }` definitions, `flows.templates()` answers them beside `flows.nodeTypes()`, and "use" creates a draft `Flow` from the definition. Compiled at registration, reviewed in git, no schema change; a client cannot author one.
  - **B** — a `FlowTemplate` model in `orion.lite`: authored and shared by people, needs its own row policy and a copy-on-use path, and coins a noun beside `Flow`.
  - **C** — a flow flagged `isTemplate`: no new noun, "use" is a copy of its current version, but visibility across owners rides on a row policy that today reads only the owner and an administrator (`FJS-D296`).
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — the registry already is how a host adds what orion lists (`FJS-D269`), a template is a flow that must compile before anyone sees it, and it adds neither a table nor a policy; B or C is the later step if people need to author them.

- ~~**FJS-D1361 — Is the polymorphic pointer spelt `subjectType`/`subjectId` or `contextType`/`contextId`?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-21 (`FJS-D1361`): A, now.** `references/README.md` chose `subjectType`/`subjectId`; eleven copies of the notifications fragment now say `contextType`/`contextId`, and every new app votes that way by scaffold.
  - **A** — settled as `subject*`; the fragment renames when a migration is cheap (pre-alpha: now).
  - **B** — settled as `context*`; AuditEvent and the README move to it.
  - **C** — leave both and have `fli check` grade only the enum-on-the-type-column half.
  - **Recommend A, now** — the evolution policy says a rename is a rename; the only cost is one fragment and one drift baseline, and it stops growing by one app a week.

- ~~**FJS-D1364 — May a `@derived` name a `@from` aggregate, now that it can do arithmetic over one?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-21 (`FJS-D1364`): A — inline it under the aggregate's own rule. The `@from` subquery is spliced into the `@derived` SQL, the derived field joins `policiedAggregates` whenever an aggregate it names does (so `where`/`orderBy` naming it is refused while the target's policy applies to this caller, as `FJS-1647` refuses the aggregate), and after the read a recomputed aggregate re-evaluates the derived through `evaluate`, which now has the arithmetic. No new word, no new rule.** Found building `FJS-2710`: `FJS-D1107`'s `+ - *` and `round()` landed, and the row's own case still cannot be written. `FJS-D950` rules eventmark's `underRatio Boolean @derived(adults * eventRatioAttendees < eventEnrolled * eventRatioAdults)`, and ELA's discount is a sum over a per-group `@from`, but `compileDerived` refuses every `@from` operand (`FJS-2778`): an aggregate's startup SQL counts every row that exists and the value is recomputed under the caller's row policy after the read (`resolveFromRowRefs`), which SQL compiled into a `@derived` would skip. Probed: `under Boolean @derived(adults * ratio < 10)` over `adults Int @from(Assignment, count: true)` fails `createClient` with *'adults' is @from*. Precedent inside litestone: a `where` naming the aggregate already runs that same policy-blind SQL, and is refused only while the target's read policy applies to the caller (`FJS-1647`, `policiedAggregates` in `args.js`). Outside it: Postgres applies row security inside every subquery of the caller's statement, so a computed column over a subquery sees the caller's rows by construction ([row security](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ddl-rowsecurity.html); a view does so only with `security_invoker`, [CREATE VIEW](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createview.html)). The red test: that schema builds, `where: { under: true }` finds the shift with one assignment and `ratio: 4`, and a caller who cannot read the assignment is refused the filter by name — and, for that caller, the `under` a read returns agrees with the recomputed `adults`, since a derived value read off the policy-blind SQL beside a recomputed aggregate is `FJS-2778`'s own disagreement and nothing else would report it.
  - **A** — inline it under the aggregate's own rule. The `@from` subquery is spliced into the `@derived` SQL, the derived field joins `policiedAggregates` whenever an aggregate it names does (so `where`/`orderBy` naming it is refused while the target's policy applies to this caller, as `FJS-1647` refuses the aggregate), and after the read a recomputed aggregate re-evaluates the derived through `evaluate`, which now has the arithmetic. No new word, no new rule.
  - **B** — keep the refusal. The ratio and the discount stay `@computed`, unfilterable; `FJS-D950`'s `where: { underRatio: true }` is not written and `FJS-2710` closes as accepted cost.
  - **C** — Postgres's answer: compile the aggregate's subquery per request under the caller's read policy, in the SELECT, the `where` and the `orderBy`, so nothing is refused and nothing is recomputed. Closes `FJS-1647`'s refusal too, at the price of a statement text per policy shape (the `bench/statements.mjs` ratchet moves) and of the startup-built `subquerySql` becoming per-request.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — it extends a rule litestone already enforces rather than adding one, unblocks both callers for every caller the target's policy does not narrow (eventmark's `asSystem()` reads and an owner's own rows), and leaves C open as the later step if a policied caller ever needs the filter.

- ~~**FJS-D1365 — Does arithmetic reach an orion flow and a stored fill template, or only a schema?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-21 (`FJS-D1365`): B — extend to both now: `arith`/`round` in orion's compile and evaluator, and in `values.js`'s allow-list.** Found building `FJS-D1107`: the operators landed in the policy dialect only. A flow (`$.qty * 2`) still fails *Unexpected '*'* because orion compiles the AST to its own nodes (`orion/src/engine/expression/text.ts`) and has no arithmetic node, and a fill template (`{line.qty * line.price}`) parses and is refused by `values.js`'s allow-list as *arith is not a value*. `FJS-D271` makes them one language, so a form that parses in one place and not the other is the drift it exists to stop.
  - **A** — leave both until a caller asks; the refusals stay by name.
  - **B** — extend to both now: `arith`/`round` in orion's compile and evaluator, and in `values.js`'s allow-list.
  - **C** — fill templates only: `values.js` already answers through `evaluate`, so it is one allow-list line; flows wait.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — neither has a caller, and orion's half is evaluator work in a second package.

- **FJS-D1366 — Does a read retry on a transient failure client-side, or stay as today: no retry, the pending queue for writes, the reconnect refetch for reads?** Found reviewing fetchium against the junction client. A call is sent once (`_request`, `_wsCall`); the socket reconnect backs off 1.5x to 30s with no jitter; `reconnected` refetches every list and record with 0–2s jitter (`FJS-701`); a write goes queue-first and the queue drains on `connect`, stopping at the first `unreachable()` (no status, or 408). Prior art: fetchium and TanStack retry per attempt with backoff and a `shouldRetry(error, attempt, status)` hook; Apollo's `RetryLink` is opt-in and off by default.
  - **A** — stay. A transient read failure surfaces as the call's error; a socket drop is already answered by the reconnect refetch, and a write by the queue. Cost: a 502 from a deploy restart stays on the screen until the person acts or the socket cycles.
  - **B** — retry READS only, bounded: three attempts, backoff with jitter, on no status / 408 / 502 / 503 / 504, never a write (a write has its idempotency key and its queue). Fetchium's `shouldRetry` shape is the hook if a caller asks. Cost: a second definition of *transient* beside `pending.js`'s `unreachable()`, and a knob.
  - **C** — one owner: `unreachable()` becomes the one definition for both directions and a read waits on `connect` the way a write does. Cost: a read that waits is a screen that hangs on a drained queue.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — `FJS-D297`/`FJS-D298` already split offline by owner and `load()` falls back to the device only on `unreachable`; a retry is a second answer to the same event. Nothing moves. B is the reopen path, and the trigger is a real release restart (basecamp) showing the error to people rather than to a drive.

- **FJS-D1367 — Does a memo or `$:` refuse a write to a signal it already read this run, as Signalium throws at the line, or keep the `_running` guard and the 100-run cycle cap?** Found reviewing Signalium against the runtime. A memo clears `dirty` before `fn` (`runtime.js` ~716), so a write to a signal it read is accepted and the memo re-runs; a `$:` that wakes itself is caught by `_running` (`FJS-2652`); a true cycle stops at the hundredth run, and the message names a count, not a line. Signalium throws *A signal was dirtied after it was consumed by the current function* at the write, in dev and prod.
  - **A** — keep both guards, and make the cap NAME the line: when it trips in dev, the message carries the memo's `_src` and the signal written (the graph already holds both for devtools, `_cause`). Cost: a dev message. Nothing refuses.
  - **B** — refuse in dev at the write: a write to a signal the running memo or `$:` already read this run throws naming both; prod keeps today's guards. Cost: a `$:` that normalizes its own input (a clamp, a trim) converges today and would throw; the 21-app corpus (`bun run corpus`) has to be tallied first.
  - **C** — refuse in prod too, Signalium's answer. Cost: an app that converges today fails at runtime.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — Mesa's answer to a mistake it survives is a `[Mesa]` warning naming the line, not a refusal (`FJS-D372` catches, the drives fail on the warning); A moves no caller and B is the reopen path once the corpus says no component writes what it read.

- **FJS-D1369 — How does a route FILE carry route options — a per-route `maxBodySize` (and the `body: 'stream'` it also cannot reach) — when its exports are only the verbs?** `FJS-3132`: `autoloadRoutes` (`junction/src/core/loader.ts`) calls `app.post(url, mod.POST)` with no third argument, so `RouteOptions` (`FJS-D1159`'s `{ body: 'stream' }`) is reachable from `app.post` but never from a file under `routes/`, and `_handleRequest` parses every matched route with the one `http.maxBodySize`. Red test: a route registered with `{ maxBodySize: 2 MB }` under an app whose `http.maxBodySize` is 256 KB takes a 1 MB POST (today 413), while `/api/*` services and a WS frame stay at 256 KB. The transport half (`RouteOptions.maxBodySize` → `RouteDefinition` → the `parseBody` bound, Bun's `maxRequestBodySize` already at the max of the two) follows `FJS-D1159` and is not in question; the file form is.
  - **A** — one named export, `export const options = { maxBodySize: 16 * 1024 * 1024 }`, read by the loader and passed as the third argument to every verb in that file. One shape for every `RouteOptions` key (`body`, `middleware`, `maxBodySize`), no per-verb split; a file that wants one verb different splits into two URLs it already cannot share.
  - **B** — per-verb: `export const POST = Object.assign(handler, { maxBodySize })`, or `export const POST = route(handler, { maxBodySize })` with a `route()` helper. Exact per verb, but it coins a wrapper and the handler is no longer the bare `(ctx) => …` the README promises.
  - **C** — leave files alone: the app registers the large route by `app.post(path, handler, { maxBodySize })` in code. Adds no convention, but `FJS-D626` made the folder the place a route lives, so the one route that most needs a bound would be the one outside it.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — it moves no caller, adds one export that is the existing `RouteOptions` verbatim (so the type is the doc), and fixes `body: 'stream'` being unreachable from a file in the same stroke; B is the reopen path if verbs in one file need different bounds.

- **FJS-D1370 — Does enabling deploy on push register the hook on the forge, and with whose credential, or is pasting the shown URL the design?** `FJS-3393`: `apps.enablePush` mints a secret and URL and the App's Config tab now shows them once, but `IGit.createWebhook(repo, url, events)` is still `StubGit`'s `{ id: 0 }` — no `IGit` implementation exists, it takes no secret to sign with, and an App's `source.repo` can name any host (the seed's is `forgejo.local`, a customer's is GitHub), so registering needs a token for THAT host that nothing in the tree stores.
  - **A** — a ForgejoGit behind `IGit` (the `TODO: if (cfg.git?.url)` in `providers/index.ts`): `createWebhook` gains the secret, `enablePush` registers on Basecamp's own forge when the repo URL is that host and `disablePush` deletes the hook. One host, one installation-level token; every other host still pastes.
  - **B** — a per-workspace forge connection (a `Secret` of kind `forge_token` per host, like `provider_key` for a compute account, `FJS-D558`): `enablePush` registers on whichever host `source.repo` names through a GitHub/Forgejo/Gitea dialect, `disablePush` removes it. Coins a noun and a screen to hold the token; covers customers' repos.
  - **C** — pasting is the design: the screen shows the URL, secret and token URL once (CapRover's and Cloudflare's deploy-hook shape, `FJS-D884`), and `createWebhook` leaves `IGit` since nothing calls it. No credential held, nothing to rotate on a forge.
  - **Recommend C** (confidence 2) — a token able to write hooks on a customer's repository is a bigger secret to hold than the one it saves a paste of, the token URL already serves a forge that signs nothing, and B can be added later as a convenience without moving anything built here.

- **FJS-D1371 — How does a broker framing tell conduit a refusal is permanent, and what does conduit do with it?** `FJS-3276`: in `BrokerTransport.connect()` (`packages/conduit/src/transports/broker.ts`) a handshake that throws, a socket that errors and a close all end in `lost()` → `scheduleReconnect()`, so a revoked token redials on the capped backoff forever and logs `handshake failed` each time; `BrokerReceiver` has `opened` and `gap` and nothing that says *stop and ask the person to relink*. The red assertion: a framing whose `handshake` rejects as a permanent refusal dials once, makes no second dial after the backoff, and calls the receiver once; today it dials again. Bun's `WebSocket` does not expose the upgrade response's status (a 401 arrives as `error` then `close`), so conduit cannot read a revoked token off the dial; only the framing, which reads the Provider's own answer in `handshake` or its close code, can. Prior art: Home Assistant's `ConfigEntryAuthFailed` stops retry for that entry and starts reauth, apart from `ConfigEntryNotReady` which retries; Discord's close codes 4004 and the like are `Reconnect=false`. `FJS-D1244` kept readiness off per-person sockets and says nothing about stopping.
  - **A** — the framing throws a typed refusal. Conduit exports `BrokerRefused` (an `Error` subclass); `handshake` throws it, and `dial()` may too (a ticket mint answered 401). `connect()` marks the subscription refused, stops redialing, and calls a new `BrokerReceiver.refused?(ctx: { reason: string })` once. One new noun and one new receiver option; the framing already owns the Provider's wire, so the classification sits where the Provider's answer is read, and a plain throw stays transient.
  - **B** — the framing classifies: a `BrokerFraming.refusal?(err, close)` that is asked on every failed handshake and every close (with the close code) and answers `true` for a permanent one. Reaches the close-code case (a Provider that opens, then closes 4004) without the handshake having to throw. Adds a framing method beside `handshake` and a second path to the same stop, and a connector that forgets it redials forever with nothing reporting it.
  - **C** — no conduit change: the receiver's `opened`/`gap` stay, and a mirror app that sees its handshake fail calls `app.conduit.unsubscribe(target)` itself. Adds nothing, but conduit has no `unsubscribe` and the app learns of a refusal only by a log line, so it needs the same signal as A to act.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — the throw is how `handshake` already reports failure, so a typed error moves no connector that does not opt in, and the close-code case is a handshake the Provider answered by closing, which the framing can raise as the same error. B is the reopen path if a Provider refuses only after the handshake has been believed. Whichever stands, the stop leaves `health().connected` false and re-subscribing the target (a relinked person) is the restart, not a timer.

- **FJS-D1372 — Does Caravan hand a handler a child-process helper that kills its process group on abort, or stay signal-only and document that a handler that spawns owns its group?** `FJS-3081`: `cancel()` of a running job writes `cancelled` and aborts `ctx.signal`, but a handler that spawned `sh -c 'sleep 120 & sleep 120 & wait'` and did `child.kill('SIGTERM')` on abort loses the shell and leaks both grandchildren (reparented to init) with the row saying `cancelled`; only `spawn(..., { detached: true })` plus `process.kill(-pgid, 'SIGTERM')` empties the tree. Measured in the ci-runner stressor: group kill took 5–6ms same-instance and 288–511ms cross-instance (bound `pollInterval`), and cannot reach a child that called `setsid`.
  - **A** — signal-only, documented: `JobContext.signal` (`types.ts`) and the `FJS-1523` hazard in `packages/caravan/CLAUDE.md` say a signal reaches the handler and stops there, and that a handler that spawns owns its process group (`detached: true`, kill `-pgid` on abort, sweep on exit). No new surface; the helper stays an app concern, and Caravan stays free of `node:child_process`.
  - **B** — `ctx.spawn(cmd, args, opts)`: Caravan spawns in its own group, SIGTERMs `-pgid` on abort, SIGKILLs after a grace, sweeps on exit. Makes the row's word true for the common case, but adds a method to `JobContext`, a grace-period option, and a process supervisor to a queue; a `setsid` child still escapes, so the guarantee is partial.
  - **C** — an exported helper outside `ctx` (`spawnGroup(signal, cmd, args)`) that a handler opts into: same behavior as B with no `JobContext` change, documented beside A's hazard line. A second thing to learn, and still partial.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — Caravan is a queue, not a process supervisor, and a helper cannot make "cancelled" mean "tree gone" anyway (`setsid`, containers need a cgroup); documenting the group-kill recipe moves no caller and adds no convention. B/C are the reopen path if a second app writes the same helper.

- **FJS-D1373 — What is conduit's outbound pace: an eighth `TargetResilience` number keyed by target or by credential, held or shed, counted in-process or shared?** `FJS-2286`: `max_concurrent` bounds calls in flight and sheds the excess as `overloaded` (`Resilience.admit`, [resilience.ts](packages/conduit/src/resilience.ts)); nothing bounds calls per minute, and a vendor's limit (DataForSEO, 2,000/min per account) is per CREDENTIAL, where a target-level number cannot say it. Red test: a target with `rate: { max: 5, per_ms: 1000 }` sent 12 calls at once through a stub transport starts at most 5 in the first second and all 12 succeed; today all 12 start at once. Two sub-questions ride on it. **Keyed by:** `Resilience` keys state by target id (or origin key, `FJS-D939`); a credential is `auth.ref`, or `{tenant, principal, grant}` for `per_caller` (`FJS-D848`), so the key must come from the send, not the descriptor. **Held or shed:** `FJS-685` ruled the concurrency cap sheds because a queue only moves the pile-up; a pace that sheds instead of waiting makes every caller write its own wait, which is the app code the row measured.
  - **A** — `resilience.rate: { max, per_ms }`, an eighth number that falls back field by field like the seven (`FJS-728`), keyed by target (and origin), process-local, a send HELD until a slot frees and bounded by the existing `deadline_ms`/`signal` (past it: `overloaded` with the wait stated). Cost: a per-credential vendor limit is not expressible, so serpgrid still needs its own pacer.
  - **B** — A, plus `rate.per: 'credential'`: the key becomes the target plus the resolved ref (and `who` for `per_caller`), each credential gets its own window. Fixes the measured case. Cost: one more field and the key is computed at send time, after admission today.
  - **C** — B with the window counted in the conduit store (`stores/sqlite.ts`), shared by every process on it. Fixes "a second process doubles the rate". Cost: a table written per send; `FJS-D1193` ruled rate-limit counters in-process for junction's limiter, and `FJS-D1021` is the same question for caravan, so C should wait for that ruling.
  - **Recommend B** (confidence 2) — it is the only one that answers the measured per-credential limit; process-local matches `FJS-D1193`, with C the reopen path once `FJS-D1021` lands, and a held send (not a shed one) is what makes the number replace the app's pacer.

- **FJS-D1374 — What ends a parked receipt that will never resolve: who owns the clock, and what an expired park becomes?** `FJS-1806`: `land()` leaves a `parked` receipt unapplied (`applied_at: null`) and nothing reads it again, so a send whose response was lost parks its receipts forever and the text stays `failed` while the carrier delivered it. The red test is conduit-side: record a receipt, apply answers `parked`, advance the clock past the park's limit, and the receipt is still `applied_at: null` with no outcome (quo's pinned `telnyx.test.ts` gap is not in this checkout). Any fix adds a Receiver field and a state the store does not have.
  - **A** — `Receiver.parkFor?: number` (seconds) and a conduit-owned `sweep(now)` the plugin runs on its own interval: a receipt unapplied past `parkFor` becomes `lapsed` (a new `Receipt.lapsed_at`, one store method `lapse`), `apply` is called once more with `ev.lapsed` so the app can reconcile against the carrier, and a delivery of the same key after that is a duplicate. Conduit owns the clock; the app owns what a lapse means. Adds a field, a store column, an interval to the plugin.
  - **B** — `lapse?(ev, ctx)` on the Receiver instead of `parkFor` on a bare number: the receiver declares what to do (dead-letter, reconcile) and conduit calls it when a sweep finds a park older than a conduit-wide `parkWindow`. Same sweep and column as A; the expiry is conduit-wide, not per receiver, so Telnyx's minutes and a slower carrier's hours share one number.
  - **C** — no conduit change: `parked` stays a hold-and-redrive state, the app queries `conduit.unapplied(provider)` (a read, no clock) and schedules its own expiry on Caravan (quo's Q12). Smallest, but it is the unowned timer the row names, once per app.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — `FJS-D899` gave conduit the receipt and what a duplicate means, so a receipt that never ends is conduit's to end; a per-receiver number fits because carriers differ, and `lapsed` is a state, not a deletion, so the audit of what happened survives. C is the reopen path if a second app wants the expiry to be a job rather than a sweep.

- **FJS-D1375 — How does an app backup get asked for from a screen, run on a schedule, and restored without moving the archive by hand: does Outpost's restore root widen to the backup directory or a promote step copy across, and does the cron live on an `App` column or a `Job` row?** `FJS-3179`: `app-backups.take` is on the API and `/mcp` only, `apps.restoreVolume` accepts a path only under `OUTPOST_RESTORE_DIR` (`FJS-D943`), and `take` records a path under `OUTPOST_BACKUP_DIR`. `/hub/backups` shows its own cron with the caveat that nothing registers it. The red test would assert: after `take` succeeds, `apps.restoreVolume({ backupId })` restores it; today `backupId` is refused as an undeclared key.
  - **A** — Screen and restore only, no schedule yet: an app-detail card lists `AppBackup` rows with Back up now and Restore (`restoreVolume` takes `backupId`, resolving `location` from the row; Outpost's `/volume/restore` accepts `OUTPOST_BACKUP_DIR` as a second root, `realpath`'d as the restore root is). Smallest; a schedule follows as its own row.
  - **B** — A, plus the clock on the App: `App.backupCron` (nullable) and a `backup:app` caravan job synced by the one rule `job-schedule.ts` uses, so an edit moves the clock (`FJS-328`). One more column on `App`, and a retention number is owed (`AppBackup` rows and archives otherwise grow without bound).
  - **C** — A, plus the clock as a `Job` row of a new kind `backup`: no schema column on `App`, the schedule screen exists, but `Job` is a command run on a machine and this one calls a service, so the kind needs its own dispatcher.
  - **D** — Outpost gains `POST /backup/promote { path }` copying an archive from the backup directory into the restore directory; the restore root stays single (`FJS-D943`'s confinement untouched). A second route and a second copy of a multi-GB file on the same disk.
  - **Recommend A, then B** (confidence 2) — one root widened by a second directory is the same `realpath` check twice rather than a new route, and a schedule without a restore button is a backup nobody has tried to use.

- **FJS-D1376 — What closes `FJS-053` (email-kit never opened in a real mail client): a human Outlook check, a rendering-matrix service, or a static conditional-comment lint with the gap documented?** `FJS-053` is not a code defect: 110/110 email-kit tests pass and assert the `<!--[if mso]>` / `<!--[if !mso]><!-->` shapes against the HTML string, but Outlook's Word renderer is the one thing a string assertion cannot stand in for, and a headless session has no mail client to open. No red test exists, so the row cannot close by a fix alone.
  - **A** — A person opens `WelcomeEmail` once in Outlook 2016/2019/365, Gmail and Apple Mail; the result goes in `docs/HTML_EMAIL_TRAPS.md` as a dated checklist, and the row closes on that note. Cost: one manual session; goes stale at the next template change.
  - **B** — A rendering-matrix service (Litmus / Email on Acid) driven from a `fli prove` drive. Needs a paid account, network and a secret in CI; the drive would be `skipped` without them.
  - **C** — Add a static lint to `email-kit.test.js` that every `<!--[if` has its `<![endif]-->` and nothing nests, across every template, and re-word the row as a standing caveat in `PROJECT_STATE.md` only (close the register row). Cheap and catches the FJS-928-class regressions, but never claims Outlook renders correctly.
  - **Recommend A, with C's lint** (confidence 2) — the unconfirmed claim is "renders in Outlook", which only a real client answers; the lint is a cheap regression guard in the meantime, and B's cost buys little for a kit with one template.

- **FJS-D1377 — What is an absent optional list with `@minItems(2)`, once the DDL stores it as `'[]'`, and which realm grades it?** Red test: `createClient({ schema: 'model Survey { id Int @id @default(autoincrement()) labels String[]? @minItems(2) }' }).survey.create({ data: {} })` returns `labels: []`, a list the schema refuses. `validate` (validate.js:796) grades only a list it was handed, and the DDL (`ddl.js:311,325`) makes every array column `NOT NULL DEFAULT '[]'` — "empty array is the null state" — so `String[]?` has no absent state in storage. `FJS-3328` made junction's boundary leave the key absent, so below it the `[]` lands unchecked.
  - **A** — Litestone grades the fill: an absent key on an optional list with `@minItems > 0` is refused at create like a stated `[]`. Then `?` on such a list means nothing and `FJS-3328`'s "can be left out" is lost again (junction must put the `default: []` back).
  - **B** — `[]` is an optional list's *not stated* state: `@minItems` grades a non-empty list only, in `validate`, in `generateJsonSchema` (`minItems` stays, with `[]` allowed on an optional list) and in the boundary, so `@minItems(2)` on `String[]?` reads "none, or at least two" and the stored `[]` is honest. A required list keeps the full rule.
  - **C** — A real NULL: an optional list column becomes nullable in the DDL (migration for existing columns) and a reader meets `null`. Matches `?` exactly, moves every array reader and the `json_insert(coalesce(…))` ops (client.js:3209).
  - **Recommend B** (confidence 2) — it matches the DDL's stated null state and `FJS-3328`'s outcome, moves one rule and no column, and A or C stay the reopen path.

- **FJS-D1378 — How does `payments.start` stop its losing concurrent request leaving an unrecorded intent at the provider?** Red test: two parallel `start` calls on one order against the sink — the sink holds two intents (`pi_…`) while `Payment` names one; the loser's `createIntent` ran before its insert was refused (`FJS-3398`'s unique on open rows), and nothing ever pays or reconciles it. The row says the fix is claiming before the call, which `providerRef @unique` + `@length(3, 64)` and `start`'s own "provider FIRST" comment forbid today.
  - **A** — Claim first: `providerRef` becomes nullable (single-column `@unique` over NULL is legal), `start` inserts `pending` with no ref, then calls the provider and sets the ref; a failed call marks the claim `failed`. Costs the schema change, a `pending` row with no provider behind it, and reverses `start`'s documented order.
  - **B** — Collapse at the provider: `createIntent` states an `idempotency_key` of order reference + count of the order's earlier payments, so concurrent starts get the SAME intent back and the loser's insert names the winner's `providerRef`; a decline raises the count and a fresh intent follows. The sink already honors `Idempotency-Key` for refunds. Reverses `createIntent`'s "a key would be the wrong way" comment.
  - **C** — Leave it and say so: an unconfirmed intent moves no money and a real provider expires it; close the row as accepted, add the sentence to `start`'s comment. Costs the "never reconciled" tidiness only.
  - **Recommend B** (confidence 2) — it closes the orphan with no schema column and no row that lies about a payment existing, and the key's attempt-count part keeps the retry-after-decline case the current comment guards.

- ~~**FJS-D1379 — Is an 80-bit truncated MAC in a plus-address reply token acceptable under ASVS V11.2.3, or does `mintToken` default to 128 bits and shrink the mailbox it can tag?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-30 (`FJS-D1379`): A — Keep 16 as the default and accept it: record in `docs/ASVS.md` V11.2.3 that the MAC is truncated by RFC 5321's limit, online-only, bounded by the ULID's `ttlSeconds`; V11.2.3 becomes `covered` with that reason. `macChars` stays the knob for an app that wants more and has a short mailbox.** `FJS-3201`: `mintToken` (`packages/toolbelt/src/signature/signature.js`) defaults `macChars` to 16 (80 bits) so `<mailbox>+<26-char ULID><mac>` fits the 64-octet local part (`replyAddress` in `packages/junction/src/mail/received.ts` refuses a longer one at mint). The row's Basecamp half is wrong: `invitations.service.ts` has its own `mintToken`, 32 random bytes (256 bits), not toolbelt's. Only the reply tag is 80 bits. A forgery is online only — every guess is one delivered mail — and the tag proves receipt of our mail, nothing more.
  - **A** — Keep 16 as the default and accept it: record in `docs/ASVS.md` V11.2.3 that the MAC is truncated by RFC 5321's limit, online-only, bounded by the ULID's `ttlSeconds`; V11.2.3 becomes `covered` with that reason. `macChars` stays the knob for an app that wants more and has a short mailbox.
  - **B** — Default 26 (130 bits). The mailbox before `+` is then at most 11 characters (`support` fits, `notifications` and `customer-support` throw at mint), so most real inboxes cannot carry a tag.
  - **C** — Default 16, but `replyAddress` spends the room the mailbox leaves: it takes `macChars = min(26, 64 - 1 - local.length - id.length)` so a short mailbox gets up to 130 bits and a long one keeps at least 80, and `readToken` is told the length it was minted with (the reader knows the base from the recipient). One more thing the reader must derive, and the token length now varies by mailbox.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — 2^80 mail deliveries is not an attack, B breaks the mailboxes people have, and C buys a bit count nobody can use at the cost of a reader that must re-derive the length; A keeps the knob. Whichever is ruled, correct `FJS-3201`'s title: Basecamp invitations are not on this function.

- **FJS-D1429 — What text form do `array`, `pipe`, `let` and `match` take in the shared flow grammar, and does `match` stay in the `Expression` union when the resolver has no case for it?** Found fixing `FJS-2136`: `emit.ts` refuses all four by name and `text.ts` parses none, so a stored one shows as a document (6 pipes, 5 arrays, 3 lets in the repo's flows). The text is syntax in `@frontierjs/toolbelt/predicate` (`FJS-D271`), which `FJS-D513` grew for `template` and `object` in the flow dialect only. Choices: `[a, b]` already parses as a literal-only `list` (the right side of `in`, empty refused), so an array of expressions must not change what a list means; a pipe's step omits its first operand (`lower` is `lower($)`), which no existing syntax says; and `ExpressionResolver.resolve` (`packages/orion/src/engine/expression/index.ts`) has no `match` case, so a stored `match` resolves to `undefined` today.
  - **A** — Grow all four in the flow dialect: array `[e, ...]` read as an array when any member is not a literal (a list otherwise), pipe `a |> f(b) |> map(x => ...)`, let `let a = e, b = e in body`, match `match v { when e => e, else => e }`; a `.lite` policy refuses each by name as it does `template`. Also adds the missing resolver case for `match`.
  - **B** — Grow `array`, `pipe` and `let` (the forms the repo's flows use) and drop `match` from the union and from `PipeStep`'s neighbors, since nothing runs it; a `match` is a `cond` chain until a flow needs one.
  - **C** — Accept: these stay documents, the inspector keeps showing JSON, and `FJS-2136` closes as won't-fix. Keeps the shared grammar to predicates plus the two assembling forms.
  - **Recommend B** (confidence 2) — the three forms are in real flows and the fourth is a stored shape no engine path executes, so syntax for it would be a grammar nobody can test against behavior. A is the reopen path if a flow needs a `match`.

- **FJS-D1430 — A job dispatched under a bearer grant: does it run as the app's system with the grant named in the trail, or re-enter the grant and run at its standing?** `FJS-3076`: `dispatch()` records `host.principal()?.id` (`caravan/src/index.ts` actorId), a grant has no id, so the row's `actor_id` is null and `worker.ts` runs it `runAs(null, { tenant })` — the app's system principal. The red test's assertion: a job dispatched inside a call that resolved `bearerClaim(...)` writes a row whose audit `actorType` is `'service'`/`'system'` where the direct path writes `'bearer'`, `actorId` the grant's id, `subjectId` its subject (`FJS-D342`). The grant lives in `ctx.locals[BEARER]` (`core/bearer.ts`), which `installLogContext` reads as `bearerId`/`bearerSubject`; nothing in the jobs row, `runAs` or `RequestMeta` carries it.
  - **A** — Attribution only: a host getter reads the call's grant, the jobs row gains `bearer_id` and `bearer_subject`, the worker passes `runAs(null, { tenant, bearer })`, `RequestMeta` carries it and `installLogContext` falls back to it. The trail matches the direct path; the job still runs with the app's full standing, which `FJS-D69` rejected by name ("SYSTEM by default"), and a revoked key's queued work still runs.
  - **B** — Re-enter the grant: the row records the grant's model and id; the worker re-reads that row at run time, a revoked or expired grant fails the job permanently (the `PRINCIPAL_MISSING` path and `onMissingActor`), and the handler runs at the grant's standing (STRANGER plus the claims `bearerClaim` states) with the trail naming it. Faithful to `FJS-D69` — revocation reaches queued work — and it needs `bearer.ts` to expose a re-resolve that runs without a request, and handlers that wrote past the grant's row policy as system are refused.
  - **C** — Carry it as data: `JobContext` gains the grant (`ctx.bearer`) and the app writes the key into its own rows; the trail keeps naming `system`. Smallest, and leaves the stolen-key search `FJS-3076` names to every app.
  - **Recommend B** (confidence 2) — A files the right name over the wrong authority, which is the `FJS-D69` hazard exactly and for exactly the caller a public key is; A is B's first half (the row records the grant either way), so it is the reopen path if re-entry proves too heavy.

- **FJS-D1431 — Does a custom method on a row-tenant-scoped service get a per-method opt-out of the tenant guard, or is a platform-wide operation a model-less service (`FJS-D49`)?** `FJS-3091`: under `tenancy { strategy row }`, `tenantClaimGuard` (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts`, mounted `before: all` in `core/app.ts`) refuses every call on a scoped service when the request names no tenant, a custom method included, so an administrator's `POST /api/payouts` with `x-service-method: run` and no `X-Shop-Id` answers 400 though `run` reads nothing through the caller. The red test: a custom method on a row-scoped service called by a signed-in principal carrying no tenant claim answers 200 where it answers 400 today. `FJS-D49` ruled a tier above every tenant is a SEPARATE service, and rejected widening existing services by a caller-chosen switch; it did not consider a switch the schema's author declares on the method. `scope:` already names an API-key scope on a method (`FJS-D916`).
  - **A** — Keep `FJS-D49`: the platform-wide operation is a model-less service (`model: null`), which is what the etsy stressor's `platform.service.ts` already is. Close `FJS-3091` as accepted, add an `api-hazards` entry saying a method across tenants lives on its own service. The administrator is graded by hand there, since a model-less service has no gate floor.
  - **B** — A boolean on the custom method, `tenant: false`, read by `tenantClaimGuard` to skip the refusal for that method only. Never on a CRUD verb, so every read stays one tenant's, and the method's model read-gate floor (`FJS-826`) still grades the caller. `manifest()` and `svc.describe()` carry it, so the methods spanning tenants are listable. The method reads through `asSystem()` since a caller-scoped client with no claim matches no row.
  - **C** — `scope: 'platform'` on the method, as the row words it. One word with two owners: `scope:` is already the API-key scope a key must hold (`FJS-D916`), so a method naming it would be read as a key's standing rather than a tenancy exemption.
  - **Recommend B** (confidence 2) — the caller cannot widen anything (the author states it on the method, greppable like `FJS-D49`'s hub service), the gate floor grades the administrator instead of a hand-written check, and C collides with `FJS-D916`; A is the reopen path if a second method wants it and the extra service per operation proves the lesser cost.

- **FJS-D1432 — Does the webhooks plugin deliver through `app.conduit` when it is present (bare fetch otherwise), or keep its own fetch and gain only a metrics line?** `FJS-2924`: `attemptDelivery` (`packages/junction/src/plugins/webhooks/index.ts`) grades with `gradePublicUrl`, pins the address, fetches with `redirect: 'manual'` and a 10s `AbortSignal`, and records the result on its own `deliveries` row and six-rung ladder. No breaker per origin, no observer, no `/metrics` line, where conduit's `address_from: 'request'` target does all three per send. Junction may not import Conduit (Invariant 1), so a conduit path is a structural member on `AppConduit` (`FJS-D219`'s shape), and the ladder is the unsettled part: conduit's own `retry_limit` would multiply the plugin's six attempts.
  - **A** — Structural send: `AppConduit` gains the one member `attemptDelivery` calls; with conduit present the plugin sends through an `address_from: 'request'` target with `retry_limit: 0`, so the plugin's persisted ladder stays the only retry and conduit adds breaker, observer and metrics. Without conduit the bare fetch stays, as IMail's default does. Two paths to keep equal; signing stays `signRequest` with the `X-Webhook` prefix on both.
  - **B** — Keep the fetch and close the gap in the plugin: `app.registerMetricsSource('webhooks', …)` over the deliveries table, and a per-origin breaker the plugin keeps itself. One path, but it is a second breaker beside the package that owns the ring, which is what the row objects to.
  - **C** — Conduit required for delivery: the plugin refuses to register without `app.conduit` and drops its fetch. One path and no ladder doubling to reason about, but a webhooks-only app must install a package it never configures, and every webhooks test needs a conduit.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — it is the row's own candidate and the only option where the ring's breaker, observer and metrics reach the framework's one shipped outbound integration without making the package mandatory; `retry_limit: 0` settles the ladder question in one number. The bare-fetch path logs once at register so a missing ring is not silent, and a test holds the two paths to the same redirect, address and signature refusals.

- **FJS-D1433 — Where does `@phone`'s national-number metadata live, and how much of a region's rules does it carry?** `FJS-D874` = B rules `@phone(region: "US")` reads `(555) 123-4567` as `+15551234567`, which needs region → calling code, the trunk prefix to drop (`0` in the UK, `1` in the US) and some notion of a valid length; `@frontierjs/toolbelt` has zero dependencies (`FJS-D26`) and holds none of it. The `+` half (strip separators, require 8–15 digits, store `+<digits>`) needs no metadata. Red test: `create` with `'(555) 123-4567'` then `'+15551234567'` under `@unique` — the second must be refused as a duplicate; `'-------'` and `'+0000000'` must be refused as malformed.
  - **A** — A hand table in toolbelt: region → `{ code, trunk, lengths }` for a stated set of regions (the ~20 an app is likely to name), an unlisted region refused at parse by name. Zero deps kept and the diff stays reviewable; a number valid by a region's real plan but outside the stated lengths is refused, and adding a region is an edit here.
  - **B** — `libphonenumber-js` (its `min` metadata) as toolbelt's first dependency. Every region and real per-region validity for free; toolbelt stops being zero-dep, the transform runs in the browser bundle (~80 KB), and `FJS-D26`'s import license no longer comes free.
  - **C** — A hand table of region → `{ code, trunk }` only (all ISO regions, ~250 rows of two fields) with the one E.164 length bound (8–15 digits total) for validity. Every region nameable, no per-region plan; accepts a number a region's real plan would refuse, which a carrier then catches, as before this row.
  - **D** — Ship the `+` half now (normalize, refuse `'-------'`) and rule `region:` off until an app needs it; a national number is refused by a message naming the missing `+`. Closes the unique-per-spelling hole for every app that collects E.164; leaves `FJS-D874`'s `region:` unbuilt.
  - **Recommend C** — it keeps toolbelt zero-dep (`FJS-D26`) and names every region, and the only validity it forgoes is the carrier's own; if an app shows a number C admits that matters, A's per-region lengths grow out of C's rows without changing what a field means.

- **FJS-D1434 — Does the derived instant keep the word `@zoned` beside `FJS-D512`'s reader, and what does a nonexistent or repeated wall clock resolve to?** Found building `FJS-3381` (`FJS-D963` = A, `startsAt DateTime @zoned(date, startLocal, site.timeZone)`). Today `@zoned(path)` is legal on a `String @date`/`@time` only and says "this wall clock is READ in the zone at path" (`parser.js` `parseZoned`, validated near `parser.js` 8049, emitted as `x-zoned`, read by `due()` per `commitment.js`); `parseZoned` takes ONE path and the validator refuses `@zoned` on anything that is not a `String @date`/`@time`. The ruled form is a three-argument attribute on a `DateTime` that WRITES the column. Red test: a schema with `startsAt DateTime @zoned(date, startLocal, site.timeZone)` parses; a create with `date '2026-03-08'`, `startLocal '02:30'` under `America/New_York` stores the `compatible` instant (`2026-03-08T07:30:00.000Z`, the gap resolved to the later side); an update of `site.timeZone` re-writes the children's `startsAt` in the same transaction; an app write of `startsAt` is refused by name. Toolbelt's `fromWall(fields, zone, { disambiguation })` already answers Temporal's four modes, so the instant itself is not owed.
  - **A** — One word, one meaning: `@zoned`'s LAST argument is always the zone path, and the field's type says the rest. `String @date @zoned(site.timeZone)` is read in that zone; `DateTime @zoned(date, startLocal, site.timeZone)` is derived from two columns and that zone. The parser dispatches on argument count, the validator on the field's type, and the catalog gains the second form under the one `field:zoned` row. Matches `FJS-D963` as ruled and needs no rename; the cost is that one attribute does two jobs (a reader that annotates, a writer that stores) and a one-argument `@zoned(site.timeZone)` on a `DateTime` is refused by name rather than meaning anything.
  - **B** — Keep `@zoned(path)` for the reader alone and spell the derivation with its own word: `startsAt DateTime @instant(date, startLocal, site.timeZone)`. Two words for two jobs, so knowing one predicts nothing false about the other, and `@zoned`'s validator and `x-zoned` stay as they are. Overrides `FJS-D963`'s spelling and adds a `.lite` word (catalog row, tier, axis) and a doc page.
  - **C** — Rename the reader, keep the ruled spelling for the writer: `endsOn String @date @inZone(timeZone)` and `startsAt DateTime @zoned(...)`. Every `@zoned(path)` in the tree (`FJS-D512`, `x-zoned`, `commitment.js`, the example and prototype schemas) moves in one change with no alias; the one `@zoned` then means *derived from a zone*, at the price of the largest churn and a second ruling reopened.
  - **Disambiguation, whichever of A–C** — a wall clock in a spring-forward gap resolves with `fromWall`'s `compatible` (the later side: `02:30` on the gap day becomes `03:30`) and a repeated hour with its earlier instant, fixed in the attribute with no option; a caller that must refuse a gap states it as a `@check` over the wall clock. **Alternative** — `reject`, a nonexistent time refused at write by name: stricter (a shift booked into the gap is a typo or a stale form) but a zone write then refuses a child whose stored clock became nonexistent under the NEW zone, which blocks a legitimate correction.
  - **Recommend A** — `FJS-D963` already spelled it and the last-argument-is-the-zone reading is one sentence, so the three-argument form is the same declaration (*this column is tied to the zone at the end of its path*) rather than a second one; with `compatible` fixed, because a zone change cannot be allowed to fail a child write. Confidence 2 of 3 — B is the stronger name if a reader meets both forms in one model and misreads which one writes.

- **FJS-D1435 — How does `claimIdempotency` learn the bearer grant's id, when the resolver that parks it runs inside the data hook, after the claim?** Found fixing `FJS-2151`. `claimIdempotency` runs in `callService` ahead of the pipeline (`FJS-D451`: a replay runs no hook), and `principalOf` reads `ctx.user?.id`. A `bearerClaim` caller has `ctx.user === null`; the grant row is parked at `ctx.locals[BEARER].id` by the resolver, which `withLitestoneDb` runs through `applyClaims` inside the around hook (`core/litestone.ts` `resolveAppClaims`) — so at claim time `ctx.locals[BEARER]` is empty. Red test: a service behind `principal: bearerClaim({ from: header('x-api-key'), ... })`, one POST twice under one `Idempotency-Key` with a live key — the second must replay the first's answer and the method must have run once; today it runs twice and the server warns *no principal*.
  - **A** — Resolve ahead of the claim: when a key arrives, the caller has no `ctx.user` and the app states a `principal` (held under `PRINCIPAL_RESOLVER`), `callService` runs the resolver first and keys on `locals[BEARER].id`. The grant id is the namespace `FJS-D342` already names. Cost: the resolver runs twice on a keyed, userless write (a read plus the windowed `lastUsedAt` stamp), and a dead key is refused 401 before the claim, the same answer the hook gives.
  - **B** — Key on a digest of the credential as presented (the `bearerClaim`'s `from` header value), read in the transport before any resolver. No double resolve and no new ordering; but the key is the secret's digest rather than the grant id, so a rotated key starts a fresh namespace, and the transport must learn each resolver's `from` — a second owner of *how a request names its grant*.
  - **C** — Move the claim inside the pipeline, after the data hook. The grant is resolved once and any principal works; but a replay then runs the hooks before it answers, which `FJS-D451` rules out, and the gate-skipping price `FJS-D1309` ruled final changes shape.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the row's own wanted shape (scope to the grant id) and `FJS-D451` stays whole; the second read is bounded to keyed, userless writes, which are rare next to every other call.

- **FJS-D1436 — How does a `header()` or `cookie()` bearer's socket get graded and revoked: what carries its token on the upgrade, who asks the app's `principal` for a recipient, and what closes a revoked grant's socket?** Found fixing `FJS-1955`. Three seams, one per symptom. (1) A browser sets no header on an upgrade, and the subprotocol slot `FJS-D486` gave the session is read whole by `extractToken` (`http.ts` ~1891) as a session, so a grant rides `/ws?visitor=<token>` in the URL. (2) A broadcast has no frame, so `bearerClaim` (which reads `ctx.headers`/`meta.headers` through `requestHeaders`, `bearer.ts:53`) is never asked for a recipient: `gradeRecipients` (`channels.ts:591`) grades `conn.user == null` as a stranger unless the app hand-writes `channels({ claims })`, three apps now (chatwoot `visitor.ts`, maidtech `socketClaims`) copying `bearerClaim`'s lookup and `isLive` rule. (3) `disconnectSession` (`channels.ts:1183`) matches `conn.user?.sessionId`, and a bearer's `conn.user` is null; `reverifySeconds` re-asks only a session credential (`http.ts` `_wsReverify`). Red test, if it is wanted as a test: a `channels()` app with `principal: bearerClaim({ from: header('x-visitor') ... })`, a socket upgraded with the grant, a `messages` row the grant's policy admits, and the broadcast arrives; today it is refused for want of a claim, and revoking the grant row leaves the socket open.
  - **A** — The framework asks the resolver. `channels()` with no `claims` option defaults to running the app's `principal` list (the `PRINCIPAL_RESOLVER` held on the app) over a synthetic context built from the upgrade's `conn.data.headers`, per frame per principal as `FJS-D472` already asks `claims`, and its `PrincipalClaims` answer is the claim set. A hand-written `claims` still wins. The token for a browser rides a new subprotocol entry, `fjs.grant.<token>`, that `extractToken` does NOT read as a session and that the socket's headers expose to the resolver under the bearer source's declared `headerName` (the source already declares it for CORS and the frame allow-list). A revoked grant is closed by the same per-frame ask: a resolver that answers 401 for a dead grant ends the socket (`4001`), and `reverifySeconds` re-asks the principal for a grant-only socket as it does a session. One liveness rule, owned by `bearerClaim`; `disconnectSession` stays session-only.
  - **B** — Keep the grading app-written but export the pieces. Export `ChannelClaimsFn` (done, `FJS-2535`) and add `bearerClaim(...).socketClaims()`, returning a ready `ChannelClaimsFn` that reads the grant by digest per frame with `isLive`, plus `app.channels.disconnectBearer(model, grantId)` that closes sockets whose last resolved grant is that row. The token still rides an app-chosen carrier (query string, or a `fjs.grant.` subprotocol the app reads in `onConnect`). Smaller blast radius and no new default, but every app still installs two calls and the carrier stays unspecified, so the next stressor repeats the chatwoot `/ws?visitor=` URL.
  - **C** — A first-frame auth. The socket upgrades anonymous and the client's first frame is `{ type: 'auth', credential }`, resolved by the same `principal` list a call frame uses, with the result parked on the connection as `conn.user` for a session or `conn.grant` for a bearer. One carrier for both kinds of credential and nothing in the URL or the subprotocol (`IDEAS/pen-testing.md` option C); a revoked grant closes at the next frame or `reverifySeconds`. It moves the handshake, so `FJS-D486`'s subprotocol stays only as the session fast path, and a frame before auth is a stranger's.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — it is the row's own wanted shape ("the request's resolver asked for a recipient by the framework") and Invariant 4's one owner for the lookup and liveness rule: `bearerClaim` already knows how to read a grant and say it is dead, and A calls it rather than copying it. The subprotocol carrier follows `FJS-D486`'s reasoning that a credential never rides the URL, and is the smallest widening of it. The browser client half (`client/index.ts` `_connect` opening at a fixed `/ws` with only the session) rides the same carrier: a `grant` option naming the token, offered as `fjs.grant.<token>`.

- **FJS-D1437 — Who turns a written method's null single-row write into a refusal?** The served `@@transitions` moves are fixed (`declaredTransition` asks the caller's own read: 404 for a hidden row, 403 for a readable one, `test/declared-moves.test.ts`). A WRITTEN method is not: `return $.db.order.transition($.id, 'ship')` or `$.db.app.update({ where: { id }, data: { port }, system: ['port'] })` on a row the caller cannot read, or can read and not update, gets litestone's `null` and answers 200 with `null`; the second sighting lost a write (App.port never stored) while reporting success. Red test: a service with a written `ship()` returning `$.db.todo.transition($.id, 'complete')`, called as `stranger` (expects 404) and as `staff` (expects 403); both answer 200/null today. `test/declared-moves.test.ts` § *a written method that lifts it with { system: true }* pins "a written method answers the null as it likes" for the stranger, and `FJS-3129` pins a bare `null` at the Data tier.
  - **A** — Junction, in the call: a single-row `update`/`transition` on `$.db` that answers `null` marks the call; if the method then returns `null`, the envelope runs the same `missOrRefused` read the CRUD verbs use (404 hidden, 403 readable). One owner for the translation (Invariant 4), the Data contract unchanged. Cost: a heuristic on the method's return value; a method that swallows the null and returns something else is not caught.
  - **B** — Litestone: `update`/`transition` by id throws `NOT_PERMITTED` when the caller can read the row and the write matched nothing, and stays `null` for a hidden or missing row (the FJS-1093 oracle argument holds only there). Every caller, written method and script alike, gets the refusal with no junction rule. Cost: `update` becomes a second read on the miss path and `FJS-3129`'s "bare null at the Data tier" is reversed; `missOrRefused` in junction then reduces to the 404 case.
  - **C** — Nobody: the written method's author tests the result (`?? throw new NotFound`), and AGENTS.md § Services says so beside the `transition` example. Cost: the writer who followed the documented shape was the one bitten, twice.
  - **Recommend B** — the null is ambiguous at the one place that can disambiguate cheaply, and that is the owner of the policy; A leaves `$.db` calls outside a method (jobs, hooks) still silent. Confidence 2.

- ~~**FJS-D1438 — Where is the set of `App` columns that start a container stated: in the schema, or in `domain/release/runtime.ts` as the one reader?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-28 (`FJS-D1438`): C (confidence 2).** Found fixing `FJS-3428`. `RUNTIME_COLUMNS` (`port`, `containerPort`, `volumePath`, `healthCheck`, `cpuLimit`, `memLimitMb`) is the one hand list of a model's columns left in basecamp that no existing schema attribute can replace: `@immutable`/`@system` say who may write a column, and nothing says which columns `docker run` reads. `restated-fields` fires on it as App's own (Blueprint lacks `containerPort`, so exactly one model owns all six). `runtimeOf(row)` copies these names off an `App` into a release's snapshot and off a `Blueprint` into a new app.
  - **A** — A schema statement: a model-level attribute naming the group (for example `@@group(runtime, [port, containerPort, …])`) that `runtimeOf` reads off `db.$schema`. One declaration, and a column added to the group reaches the snapshot with no edit in `domain/release/runtime.ts`; but it coins a noun and an attribute for one reader.
  - **B** — A shared fragment: `App` and `Blueprint` both `extend` a `Runtime` fragment holding the shared columns, and `runtimeOf` reads the fragment's names. The `containerPort` gap between the two models becomes visible in the schema; but it restructures two models and their migration for a list of six.
  - **C** — Keep the list where it is, as the declared residual: `domain/release/runtime.ts` is already the named single reader, `api/test/services.test.ts` and outpost's suite hold each name to a flag, and the rule's baseline carries the one finding with its reason.
  - **Recommend C (confidence 2)** — the schema has no notion of "starts a container" to restate, so the list is the only statement of it and `domain/release/runtime.ts` is already its one owner; A or B add vocabulary for a set with exactly one reader.

- **FJS-D1439 — How does a client save a parent with its edited children as one atomic write: nested-write ops carried by `save()`, a transactional batch of calls, or an app-written service method?** Found fixing `FJS-2742`. `_changed` drops every composed key on a patch (`FJS-1576`), so `resource.save({ id, lines: [...] })` sends the document and loses the lines; ela's editor then makes N+1 calls (remove, patch, create lines, patch document) and can stop half-written. Litestone already writes a nested `create`/`connect` in one transaction (`FJS-638`), so the missing piece is the client verb and the wire shape, not atomicity at the Data boundary. The red test's assertion: after `resource.save({ id, lines: [edited, added], … })` over a parent whose baseline holds lines, ONE request leaves, and a refusal on any line leaves the parent unwritten.
  - **A** — `save()` diffs a child list against the baseline and sends nested-write ops (`lines: { create, update, delete }`) in the parent's own patch; one call, one litestone transaction, each child still graded by its own gate. Needs litestone's nested `update`/`delete` ops on a relation, which `FJS-3002`'s batching ruling (`FJS-D1106`) also touches.
  - **B** — A transport-level batch, `client.batch([call, call, …])` (and `resource.batch`), run in one `$transaction` server-side; every call keeps its full service pipeline, so the app orders writes and nothing is nested in a payload. Coins a noun and a route, and a call's result may feed the next (a created id), which the shape must answer.
  - **C** — Neither: the app declares a custom service method (`document.saveWithLines`) that opens `$transaction` and runs the writes; the framework adds a `resource.call` that carries it. Cheapest; every app re-writes the validate-then-order-then-write loop ela already wrote.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — a child list is already a composed key the schema knows, so the diff has a baseline to compare against and the gate stays per-row; B is the larger noun and C leaves the half-written case to each app.

- **FJS-D1440 — How does a URL-state list say 'no filter' when it started on filters — what spelling does an explicitly empty query have in the address?** `tethered({ query })` or a resource's `tetheredQuery.query` is what the list reads while the URL carries no filter (`tethered.js` `currentQuery()`, tested in `resource-tethered.test.js` "yield to the URL's"), and `buildUrl` drops an empty bag, so `list.apply({})` writes the address the list was already on. Red test: `tethered({ query: { completedAt: { equals: null } } })` on `/tasks/`, then `list.apply({})` — the load after it still carries `completedAt[equals]=null`; it should carry no filter and `list.query` should be `{}`. The address has no way to say "the filters were cleared on purpose", and that is the missing noun.
  - **A** — A directive `$filter=none` in the `@frontierjs/toolbelt/directives` table (Invariant 10's one place), written by `commit` when the next query is empty and the start is not, read by `currentQuery()` as `{}`, and never sent to the server. The default view keeps its clean address and a shared link of the cleared view says so. Cost: one new directive that is list state, not transport.
  - **B** — The list writes its start filters into the address on first load (a `replace`), so the URL always holds the whole filter state and an empty bag means empty. Nothing new in the table; `currentQuery()` loses its fallback. Cost: every default view's address grows filters, a link shared before a default changes keeps the old one, and `tetheredQuery` stops being "what a list starts on beneath the URL".
  - **C** — No new spelling: a start filter is the view's default and stays; "no filter" is stated as a neutral filter the app picks, or the filter moves out of `query` into app state. Document it in `AGENTS.md`, close the row as designed. Cost: the `{ id: { gte: 1 } }` workaround becomes the documented way.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — it is the only option that keeps the URL as the state and the default view's address clean, and `$`-syntax belongs in the toolbelt table already. Separately of the answer, the row's second half is a doc gap: `list.reload()` is the re-read `list.apply(list.query, list.directives)` was reaching for, and `AGENTS.md` does not name it.

- **FJS-D1441 — Does `columns()` omit a column a create policy fills with `auth().id` (`x-determined`), as it omits the tenancy stamp, or only when the read is owner-scoped?** `columnList` (`field-rules.js`) omits `x-litestone-kind: tenancy` because the boundary scoped the read, so the value repeats in every row; `x-determined` is the CREATE policy and says nothing of the read. A scaffold over `ownerId String @default(auth().id)` with an owner-only read draws Owner as the reader's own uuid in every row, with an Owner select in the filter bar (`filters()` ranks over the same list); an admin table over the same model sees many owners and wants the column. Red test: `columnList` over a schema carrying `x-determined: { ownerId: 'auth().id' }` returns `ownerId` in `columns` today.
  - **A** — Omit every determined column from the default ranking, reason in `omitted`, `only` names it back — the tenancy rule verbatim, one filter line beside it. Cost: an operator's table (`admin:generate`) loses Owner unless its generator passes `only`.
  - **B** — Omit only where the read is scoped to the caller: litestone emits what the read policy pins (a read-side twin of `x-determined`), `columnList` omits where it agrees with the create claim. Exact for both tables; cost: a new schema keyword and a policy analysis, which a plain `@@allow read all` model never has.
  - **C** — Leave `columns()` alone and let the scaffold pass `except` for the determined column: the generated page of a person's model drops it, the ranking stays a pure read of the read schema. Cost: every caller of `columns()` repeats the rule.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — it is the tenancy precedent one line wide, `omitted` names the reason so nothing vanishes silently, and `only` is the already-documented way back for the operator's table; the autoincrement `Id` and its range filter are a separate question and stay out of this ruling.

- ~~**FJS-D1442 — Who owns Web Push's browser half: Sierra's generated `sw.js` plus a notifications subscribe helper, a worker `@frontierjs/notifications` generates itself, or the app?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-29 (`FJS-D1442`): A (confidence 2).** Found fixing `FJS-3395`. `webPush()` (`packages/notifications/drivers/webpush.ts`) sends to a `PushSubscription` row, but nothing in the tree calls `pushManager.subscribe` with the VAPID public key or handles the `push` / `notificationclick` events (`rg pushManager packages` finds only `sierra/postbuild/offline-shell.js`'s unrelated register snippet). The only worker the framework writes is `swSource` in `postbuild/offline-shell.js`, written only when `offline: true`, and it deliberately answers a precached file and a navigation and touches nothing else. A push handler is a new kind of listener in that file, and a push-only app has no worker at all. The red test would assert that a build with `push: true` emits a `sw.js` whose `push` handler shows `{ title, body, url }` from the driver's payload and a `notificationclick` that opens `url`; today there is no such key.
  - **A** — Sierra owns the worker, notifications owns the call: `sierra.config.js` gets `push: true`, `swSource` adds the `push` and `notificationclick` handlers (and writes a worker even when `offline` is off, with an empty precache), and `@frontierjs/notifications/client` exports `subscribeToPush({ vapidPublicKey, save })` which registers nothing itself, calls `pushManager.subscribe`, and hands `subscription.toJSON()` to the app's `save` (its own row, per the driver header). One worker per origin, which the platform allows anyway. Cost: Sierra learns the payload shape `webPush` sends, a dependency in spirit from Sierra to notifications' `PushMessage` (Invariant 1 holds, since it is a documented wire shape and not an import).
  - **B** — Notifications owns both halves: `@frontierjs/notifications/sw` exports a `pushHandlers()` source string and `/client` exports the subscribe helper; an app that wants push writes a two-line `sw.js` importing it, or Sierra's `offline-shell` composes it in via an `extraWorkerSource` option. Keeps the payload shape in one package. Cost: Sierra still needs the composition seam, or the app hand-merges two workers, which is the problem the row names.
  - **C** — The app owns it, the framework documents the 20 lines: close the row as designed and put the worker snippet and the subscribe call in the README's Web Push section. Cost: the cryptography is shared and the other half, equally easy to get silently wrong (a `userVisibleOnly` omission, a missing `waitUntil`, a click that never focuses the tab), is rewritten per app.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — `Q4`'s assignment of worker registration to Homestead already makes Sierra the owner of the origin's one worker, so push is a second duty of the file that exists rather than a second worker; the helper stays in notifications because it is the half that knows the VAPID key and the subscription shape. Decide with it whether `push: true` implies a worker without `offline`, which is the part that coins an option.

- **FJS-D1443 — Does Harbor's port carry app-level messages, or is the port Junction-only and the docs say so?** `routeMessage` (`packages/jetty/src/define/harbor.js`) handles `init`, `service:call` and `channel:*`; its `default` branch only logs. An extension with no Junction behind it (intake) reaches the harbor over `runtime.sendMessage` with its own `onMessage`, so the port has no door for it. The red test would post `{ type: 'app:ping' }` on a port and expect a reply.
  - **A** — `defineHarbor({ onMessage(msg, { port, respond }) })`: the `default` branch hands an unknown type to the app hook and answers its return value under `response:<_requestId>`.
  - **B** — the port is framework-only. Docs say so, and say that an app message arrives by `chrome.runtime.onMessage` registered in `run()`, which already executes inside the worker.
  - **C** — `runCtx.on(type, handler)` registers a handler per type on the port router, so `run()` owns the door and `defineHarbor` takes no new option.
  - **Recommend B (confidence 2)** — intake's case is `runtime.sendMessage`, which never touches a port, so A and C would add a second door beside one `run()` can already open; the gap is that nothing says so.

- **FJS-D1452 — How does site-kit learn which prose tag draws a photo and which attributes carry its address and words, so an owner's swap is let past `FJS-D1250`'s no-new-tag guard?** Found fixing `FJS-3436`. `codeAdded` (`packages/site-kit/src/editable.js`) refuses any tag in a new body that the old body lacks byte for byte, so `<Image source="/a.jpg" text="x" />` rewritten to `source="/b.jpg" text="y"` is refused as new code; the red test is `applyEdit(body with that tag, { body: swapped })` returning `{ text }` where it returns `{ refused }` today. `source` and `text` are ksite's attribute names (`Image.mesa`), and the preset returns only `sierra, plugins, shell, rules` (`config/preset.js` `KEYS`).
  - **A** — A new preset key `photos: { tag: 'Image', src: 'source', alt: 'text' }`: `codeAdded` lets a tag through when its name matches, every attribute but those two is byte-identical to a tag the old body held, and the new `src` passes `photoOf` and the `text` is non-blank. Cost: a sixth contract on `KEYS`, and `editable.js` (which basecamp's save and the model-diff grade call) needs the preset passed in, which it takes from no one today.
  - **B** — The same rule with the names fixed in site-kit (`Image`, `source`, `text`), no preset key. Cost: ksite's spelling becomes the engine's, which `FJS-D648` split out; a second preset spelling it `src`/`alt` gets no swap.
  - **C** — Leave prose tags to the developer and close `FJS-3436` as designed: an owner swaps a photo only where front matter holds it, and a page that needs a swap moves the tag's photo into a front-matter key. Cost: Luminous Heart's 29 tags stay unswappable.
