  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the attribute names are the preset's vocabulary and `FJS-D1407` already read B as "a preset contract worth its own question"; the guard stays exact for every tag the preset does not name.

- **FJS-D1453 — How does a trial lane's copied register mint ids that cannot collide with main's or another lane's?** `fix:loop --trial` gives each lane a COPY of the register (`syncLane(lane, { linked: false })`), so `nextId` in `packages/cli/core/file.js` reads the same max in every lane: two lanes filing in one run mint the same `FJS-###` / `FJS-D###`, and a kept patch's ids are taken in main by the time it is applied (`FJS-2777`). A non-trial lane is safe already — the register is linked and `withRegisterGuard` serializes the writers.
  - **A** — A reserved floor per lane: `laneEnv` sets one variable on a trial lane, `nextId` and `nextDecisionNumber` mint above `max(register, floor)`, and the floor is main's high mark plus a block per lane (`registerHigh`). Cost: one new variable read by the id owner, and a patch still needs main's high mark to stay below the floor.
  - **B** — Renumber when a kept patch is applied: a trial patch's new ids are rewritten to the next free ones in main as it lands, citations in the same patch with them. Cost: a rewriter of ids inside prose, and nothing applies a trial patch today but a person with `git apply`.
  - **C** — No change: a trial's patches compare models and are read, never applied together; the ids are scratch. Cost: `FJS-1286`/`FJS-2650` style collisions stay for anyone who does apply two, and the row closes as designed with a sentence in `lanes.mjs`.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the collision is minted at one place, the id owner (Invariant 4), and a floor there stops it for every kind of id at once instead of repairing prose afterwards.

- **FJS-D1454 — What does a cron handler learn about the fire it was dispatched for, and where does the cron dispatch put it?** Found fixing `FJS-3078`. `index.ts` dispatches a cron fire as `caravan.dispatch(name, {}, { id: occurrenceKey('cron', name, fireMinute) })`, and the worker builds `JobContext` from the row (`worker.ts` `run`), which holds no minute, so a handler that must write the row it was OWED parses `ctx.id`. The red test is a `* * * * *` job whose handler records `ctx.firedFor` after the scheduler's tick: it is `undefined` today. The number the scheduler hands over is an instant for a wildcard schedule (epoch minute) and a naive wall-clock count for a fixed-time one (`wallMinute`, `FJS-D144`), so what the field MEANS is part of the question.
  - **A** — `ctx.firedFor: Date | null` is an instant, stored in a new nullable `jobs.fired_for` column (migrated on open). A wildcard fire is `minute * 60_000`; a fixed-time fire resolves its wall minute to the first instant that showed it in the schedule's zone, and a spring-gap minute (no instant) resolves to the instant the gap ends. Null for a hand run, so a handler writes `ctx.firedFor ?? now`. Cost: a column and the wall→instant resolution, which exists nowhere in `cron.ts`.
  - **B** — The same column, but it stores what the scheduler hands over untouched and `ctx.fireMinute: number | null` returns it, documented as epoch minutes for a wildcard schedule and a wall-clock count for a fixed-time one. Cost: the handler needs `isFixedTime(cron)` to read it, which is the trap the row names, moved from the id into the type.
  - **C** — No column: the cron dispatch passes the instant in `data` (`{ firedFor: <ms> }`), so a handler reads `ctx.data.firedFor`. Cost: the payload of every cron job stops being `{}`, `data` becomes a place the framework writes into, and a fixed-time schedule has the same wall-versus-instant question with nowhere to answer it.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — a handler wants the instant it was owed (`FJS-3060` is the same wish from litestone's side), the ambiguity belongs where `wallMinute` lives rather than in every handler, and a context field keeps `data` the caller's.

- **FJS-D1455 — Where does the notifications bell/list component live, and how does an app take it?** Found fixing `FJS-3451`. The package ships `.ts` and a `files` list of `*.ts`, `db`, `drivers`; its only UI is `examples/Notification.mesa`, a data half an app copies, and Basecamp's `_module.mesa` hand-writes the bell (a tether, `DropdownMenu`, a `Badge`, `markRead` and `markAllRead`). `FJS-D630` names the destination, "growing a real `resource/`", but no `fli add` exists to eject one, so there is no path from this package to an app's `src/resources/`. The red test is a file the package ships that an app can import or copy: `test -f packages/notifications/<the component>` fails today.
  - **A** — the package exports `./resource/*.mesa` (as `@frontierjs/ui` exports `./components/*.mesa`): `NotificationBell.mesa` and `NotificationList.mesa` take the app's `notifications` resource as a prop, so `markRead` stays the app's `mutate` and the component holds no tether of its own. Restyle by wrapping; no installer needed. Cost: `peerDependencies` on `@frontierjs/mesa` and `@frontierjs/ui`, and a `.ts` package now ships Mesa source that its own typecheck must skip.
  - **B** — a `resource/` folder in the package, copied into the app by hand now and by `fli add` later (`IDEAS/rigs.md` § Link vs eject: a bell is always restyled). Nothing is imported, so no peer dependency. Cost: every app holds a copy that upgrades never reach, which is the state this row complains about, one step better.
  - **C** — a generic `NotificationBell` in `@frontierjs/ui` fed `rows`, `onOpen`, `onMarkAll`. No dependency from notifications to ui, but ui learns a domain noun, and the tether wiring stays in every app.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — a prop-fed component keeps the write-through-`mutate` rule (`FJS-2111`) in the app and the markup in the package; B is where `FJS-D630` ends up once `fli add` exists, and A's files move there unchanged.

- **FJS-D1456 — How does a queued job's payload answer a subject request?** Found fixing `FJS-2100`. `jobs.data` holds whatever the app dispatched until `cleanupAfter` (7 d) sweeps the terminal row, and no column names whom a job is about: `actor_id` is who asked, `correlation_id` is which request, `unique_key` is a live-work lock (`DispatchOptions`, `types.ts`). The red test would be `dispatch('mail', { to }, { subject: 'candidate:42' })` then `purgeSubject('candidate:42')` leaving `list()` with no row and no `to` in `jobs.db`; today neither the option nor the verb exists. `IDEAS/forgetting.md` § 5 left the queue "not built here" and "ids, not addresses" as the nearest help.
  - **A** — a stated `subject?: string` on `DispatchOptions`, stored in a new nullable `jobs.subject` column (migrated on open, partial index), and one verb `caravan.purgeSubject(subject, { actor, reason })` that DELETES every non-running row carrying it, running rows refused by name, recorded as an operator event like `purge`. Cost: a noun and a column; the app states the subject, so a dispatch that forgets to is still unreachable.
  - **B** — a scrub, not a verb: a job definition declares `payload: 'ids'` or `scrubOnFinish: true` and caravan nulls `data` the moment the job is terminal. Closes the 7 d window for done jobs and leaves a pending or retrying job holding the value; no subject noun.
  - **C** — no framework change: the contract is a payload of ids that mints the value at send time (jazzhr Phase 3), written into the `api-hazards` jobs entry, plus the `advise` rule `forgetting.md` § 5 names. Cost: the guarantee is a convention, so the next app measures this row again.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — it is the only option that deletes a value already queued, and it matches the sibling shape `correlationId`/`findByCorrelation` (an optional stated tag plus a verb over it); B leaves pending work holding the value, and C is the workaround the row was measured against.

- **FJS-D1458 — How does an island ask to be injected on the toolbar click (activeTab) instead of registered by matches?** Found fixing `FJS-3386`. `buildRegistration` (`packages/jetty/src/island/registration.js`) throws `island "<id>": matches[] is required`, `manifest.js` unions every island's `matches` into `host_permissions` and into the island's `web_accessible_resources`, and `defineHarbor` registers all islands on every wake. A clipper that runs only when asked therefore has to declare `<all_urls>` to exist at all; `activeTab` plus `chrome.scripting.executeScript` on the action click needs no host permission. The red test: a config `islands: { clip: { on: 'action' } }` builds a manifest with no `host_permissions` entry for it, no `registerContentScripts` call for it, and `permissions` carrying `activeTab`; today `buildRegistration` throws.
  - **A** — one new island key, `inject: 'action'` (default `'matches'`): `matches` becomes optional for it, the manifest adds `activeTab` and skips the island's host permissions, Harbor skips registration and instead listens on `action.onClicked` and calls `scripting.executeScript({ target: { tabId }, files: ['islands/<id>.js'], world })`. Cost: Dock's `default_popup` suppresses `onClicked`, so an extension with both a Dock and an action island needs the island to run from a Dock/Harbor call instead; the build refuses that pair by name.
  - **B** — no config key: Harbor gets a verb `islands.inject(id, tabId)` and the app writes its own `action.onClicked` listener; the manifest adds `activeTab` whenever an island has no `matches`. Cost: the gesture wiring is hand-written in every clipper, and `web_accessible_resources` for the island still needs a rule.
  - **C** — close as out of scope: a clipper declares `hostPermissions: ['<all_urls>']` and `optional_host_permissions` is the narrower route later. Cost: the store question the row names stays unanswered by the framework.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the island file is already discovered and built, `runAt`/`world`/`css` map one-to-one onto `executeScript`/`insertCSS`, and the manifest already derives permissions from the island config, so one key keeps the declaration where `matches` already lives; B moves the same wiring into every app.

- ~~**FJS-D1544 — Where does a fleet box keep its files — one name under the FHS split, one root folder, or as it is — and does app data move from Docker volumes to bind mounts?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-29 (`FJS-D1544`): E — A, plus `/frontier-outpost/`, a directory holding only symlinks: `config -> /etc/outpost`, `data -> /var/lib/outpost`, `logs -> /var/log/outpost`. The real directories stay where systemd's `*Directory=` keys make and own them, and no code reads the link folder; it is the one place a person on the box looks (`cd /frontier-outpost/data/apps/<app>/data && sqlite3 app.db`). The links point out from the real directories, not into them, because systemd's sandbox and `StateDirectory=` would otherwise have to resolve a symlinked state directory, which nobody here has proved. Cost: `tar` and `du` do not follow a link without `-h`/`-L` (Outpost's own backup reads the real path), `rm -rf /frontier-outpost` removes only links so uninstalling stays `outpost-uninstall`, and one top-level directory is added, holding nothing but links. A bind-mounted front folder was weighed and left out: `tar` would work unflagged, but `rm -rf` on it deletes the data.** Found deploying `website/` to yuma. One box owner answers to two names today, `/etc/basecamp/` (`outpost.env`, cert and key, `litestream.yml`, `firewall.nft`, written by `installScript()` in `packages/basecamp/api/src/providers/compute/enrollment.ts`) and `/var/lib/outpost/` (static releases, backups, restores; `packages/outpost/src/config.js`), with `/var/lib/litestream/` beside them. App data has two mechanisms: Outpost's named volume `fjs-<app>-data` (`packages/outpost/src/docker.js` § deploy), which a person on the box finds only through `docker volume inspect` and which Litestream reaches the same way (`replicate.js`), and the cli's SSH deploy, a bind mount at `/apps/<app>/db` (`FJS-D1120`). `enrollment.ts` restates Outpost's paths by hand and nothing checks the two agree. Prior art: CapRover keeps everything under `/captain`, backed up as `/captain/data/` ([docs](https://caprover.com/docs/backup-and-restore)); Coolify under `/data/coolify/` with database contents still in Docker volumes ([docs](https://coolify.io/docs/databases/how-databases-work)); GitLab Omnibus splits `/opt/gitlab`, `/var/opt/gitlab`, `/etc/gitlab`, `/var/log/gitlab` ([README](https://softwarepublico.gov.br/gitlab/softwarepublico/omnibus-gitlab/raw/7.1.1.omnibus/README.md)); Dokku keeps state in `/var/lib/dokku` and recommends bind mounts under `/var/lib/dokku/data/storage/<app>` ([docs](https://dokku.com/docs/advanced-usage/persistent-storage/)).
  - **A** — the FHS split under the name the box's agent already has: `/etc/outpost`, `/var/lib/outpost`, `/var/log/outpost`, created by the unit's `ConfigurationDirectory=`/`StateDirectory=`/`LogsDirectory=outpost` and read from `$STATE_DIRECTORY` and friends. App data becomes a bind mount at `/var/lib/outpost/apps/<app>/data/`, so `ls` finds the SQLite files and Litestream, backup and restore point at a directory. Outpost exports the layout and `enrollment.ts` imports it rather than restating it. Cost: the `/etc/basecamp` lines move, the `volume-nocopy` plus throwaway-`chown` container becomes a host-side `chown`, and Docker no longer copies an image's files into an empty volume (the code already works against that copy).
  - **B** — A under the product name, `/etc/frontier`, `/var/lib/frontier`, `/var/log/frontier`. Reads as the platform's rather than the agent's, but coins a second name for the same owner while the agent is called Outpost, and every path moves, `config.js` included.
  - **C** — one root folder, `/frontier/{config,apps,static,backups,logs}`, the CapRover and Coolify shape. One path to remember, back up and remove. Cost: a top-level directory FHS reserves, and every path hand-added to the unit's `ReadWritePaths` under `ProtectSystem=strict` instead of derived from the `*Directory=` keys.
  - **D** — as it is: two names, named volumes. Costs nothing now and keeps a database findable only through Docker.
  - **E** — A, plus `/frontier-outpost/`, a directory holding only symlinks: `config -> /etc/outpost`, `data -> /var/lib/outpost`, `logs -> /var/log/outpost`. The real directories stay where systemd's `*Directory=` keys make and own them, and no code reads the link folder; it is the one place a person on the box looks (`cd /frontier-outpost/data/apps/<app>/data && sqlite3 app.db`). The links point out from the real directories, not into them, because systemd's sandbox and `StateDirectory=` would otherwise have to resolve a symlinked state directory, which nobody here has proved. Cost: `tar` and `du` do not follow a link without `-h`/`-L` (Outpost's own backup reads the real path), `rm -rf /frontier-outpost` removes only links so uninstalling stays `outpost-uninstall`, and one top-level directory is added, holding nothing but links. A bind-mounted front folder was weighed and left out: `tar` would work unflagged, but `rm -rf` on it deletes the data.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — fewest callers move (`config.js` already uses `/var/lib/outpost`), systemd derives and owns the directories, and no new name is coined. A bind mount makes the box's SQLite files plain files, which is what backups, Litestream and a person with `sqlite3` on the box all want. The cli's `/apps/<app>` is left alone: it belongs to the SSH deploy, not Outpost. Reopen for B if the SSH deploy and Outpost merge into one box agent. Proof owed with the build: a test that renders `installScript()` and asserts every path starts with the exported root, and `verify:outpost` reading the db back from the bind-mount path.

- ~~**FJS-D1549 — Should FileStorage default to local disk in production, persisted beside the database and served by junction, when no provider is bound?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-27 (`FJS-D1549`): A (confidence 2).** Found on ela's production deploy (`nela.awe.dev`): `Account.logo` is a `File?` column, `FILES_PROVIDER` is unbound in `.env.production`, the app's `files()` installs nothing (`ela/api/src/core/db.ts`), and the client's boot warning (`client.js`, `FJS-2908`) is followed by the Account form's first upload being refused. Today `FileStorage` with neither `provider` nor `endpoint` picks `local` only under `NODE_ENV=development|test` and refuses to build otherwise (`file-storage.js` § config). An explicit `provider: 'local'` in production builds, but it doesn't work. `localPath` defaults to `./storage` inside the container, and only the db directory survives a swap (bind mount `/apps/<app>/db`, `FJS-D1120`), so every deploy discards the files. `localUrl` defaults to `http://localhost:3001/storage`, and no junction route serves that directory, so every URL is dead, which matters most where a URL leaves the app (ela's survey email draws the logo by public address, ela ruling 150). Where the bytes belong is already ruled: `FJS-D493` puts everything an app persists under `db/`, and has `replicate`/`backup` copy every non-database file there byte for byte. `05-backup.md`'s comment still says only databases and jsonl/logger directories are copied, so whether that half of D493 is built is owed proof.
  - **A** — a production local default: unbound, the plugin stores under `db/files` (the one mount a deploy keeps, and D493's backup and replication scope), junction serves it read-only under `apiPrefix` (`/api/files/:key`), and `publicBase` derives from `FRONTIER_APP_URL`. The client installs `FileStorage` itself when the schema has a File column and no plugin handles it, the way it installs `CapabilityPlugin` for `@@capabilities`, so an app writes nothing. With no options, `FileStorage` reads the framework's own `FILES_PROVIDER`, `FILES_BUCKET`, `FILES_ENDPOINT`, `FILES_KEY`, `FILES_SECRET` and `FILES_PUBLIC_BASE` names, so the deploy can read the same names. The deploy preflight (`envCheck` / `deploy:doctor`) warns, never refuses, on every deploy whose schema has a File column and whose server env leaves `FILES_PROVIDER` unset. The warning names the columns, the folder on the box, and the two fixes: `FILES_PROVIDER=local` to accept the default, or `r2`/`s3` with a bucket and keys. Setting either silences it. Costs: a static route in junction, a backup that grows with uploads, a disk that is now shared by the db and the files, and served files are public by key (an unguessable `:uuid`, the same exposure as an R2 `publicBase`, but now on the app's own origin and CSP). A bound provider later re-points refs by `FJS-D875`, but bytes already on disk need copying. The plugin costs an app with no File column nothing, because it is never installed. On a model with no File column and no relation to one, each hook returns after one map lookup (`external-ref.js`).
  - **B** — A, but opt-in: `provider: 'local'` in production means the db directory and the junction route, not the container's cwd, and the no-provider case keeps refusing. The fix is the same as A's, but an app has to choose it.
  - **C** — keep refusing, and make it loud before production: `deploy:doctor` and `envCheck` refuse a deploy whose schema has a File column and whose env binds no provider, and the ui's file input renders disabled with the reason when no FileStorage is installed. Nothing is stored locally, and object storage stays the only production answer.
  - **D** — as it is: a boot warning nobody reads in a container log, and an upload refused in front of the customer.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — a one-box SQLite app already trusts the db directory with everything it can't lose, so its files can sit there too, and the cost is a route plus a backup step D493 already rules. The implicit default works, and the deploy keeps saying so until someone makes it explicit, which takes C's loudness without C's refusal. C is the right fallback if a ruling says production files never live on the box. Where the box keeps `/apps/<app>` itself is `FJS-D1544`'s, and it does not block this.

- ~~**FJS-D1598 — How does a publish keep the tree, the tags and npm agreeing — a check that names a tag npm lacks, a publish that refuses while a workspace dependency has moved past its published tag, or both?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-28 (`FJS-D1598`): C (confidence 2).** Found reviewing `FJS-3534`. Two failures in one day. First, `16bcc800` tagged `@frontierjs/outpost@0.1.6` and moved `OUTPOST_VERSION` in `packages/basecamp/api/src/providers/compute/enrollment.ts` onto it, but npm's `latest` stayed 0.1.5, and nothing reported it. `ws:pub` treats a skipped publish as one *the next run publishes* (`packages/cli/commands/workspace/publish/_steps/02-publish-all.md`). Second, 0.1.6 was then published by hand and crash-looped on yuma: `workspace:*` was rewritten to `@frontierjs/litestone` `1.1.10` exactly, and litestone's tree held 62 changed files under that same version (`lastWriteArgs`, `58f09ceb`). The immediate break is gone (0.1.7 is on npm and the pin is 0.1.7); the policy is not. Prior art: Lerna's `publish from-package` publishes exactly the versions the registry does not have ([docs](https://lerna.js.org/docs/features/version-and-publish)); Changesets bumps a dependent whose dependency is released, and `updateInternalDependencies` decides when a range moves ([docs](https://github.com/changesets/changesets/blob/main/docs/config-file-options.md)).
  - **A** — a check only: a `done`- or `snapshots`-phase check names every `<name>@<version>` tag npm does not have. Cost: it reports the first failure and does nothing about the second, the one that crash-looped a box.
  - **B** — the publish refuses: `ws:pub`, and any publish fli runs, refuses while a `workspace:` dependency's tree differs from its published tag, and names the fix, publishing that dependency first as a new version. Cost: an outpost-only change can force a litestone release, and a hand-run `bun publish` bypasses it.
  - **C** — A and B, and the enrollment pin is checked against what npm HAS: A's check also names `OUTPOST_VERSION` when npm lacks it, and an enrollment refuses by name when npm lacks `@frontierjs/outpost@${OUTPOST_VERSION}`. Basecamp's boot does not ask npm, since an npm outage would then take Basecamp down.
  - **Recommend C (confidence 2)** — A catches the silent skip, B is the only half that would have stopped the crash loop, and the pin check is the one place the cost of a skipped publish lands on a customer's box. Every half has an owner already: A calls `unpublished()` in `packages/cli/core/publish-preflight.js`, which already asks npm; B is one more entry in `publishRefusals` beside `dirty-tree` and `peer-range`, with the same `--allow-*` override, reading `pkgState(dep).affected`. A runs in `fli done`, not in the `snapshots` phase, so CI does not depend on npm being up. The forced dependency release in B is the correct cost: the published dependent already needed that code. B counts a test-only commit as moved, so it can force a patch release the override exists for. A hand-run `bun publish` still bypasses B; the reopen path is a `prepublishOnly` hook calling the same check. Publishing stays owner-run; this changes what fli refuses, not who runs it. It touches `FJS-D1568` (package host): whatever the registry is, "has it" is asked of that registry.

- ~~**FJS-D1599 — Does `fli deploy`'s first-release setup become a reconcile — every write idempotent and gated on what is missing — or keep its never-released gate and report how far a failed run got?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-28 (`FJS-D1599`): A (confidence 2).** Found reviewing `FJS-3529`. Seen on NELA / Preview against yuma.awe.dev: `applyPlan` (`packages/cli/commands/basecamp/deploy.md`) patched App `app`, wrote 10 Variables, created the static App `app web` and the Domain `nela.awe.dev`, then failed at the first `POST /domain-routes` (`FJS-3530`), so `/api` and `/ws` were never routed. A rerun skips the whole setup, because `neverReleased` (`packages/cli/core/release-preflight.js`) now sees a port and Variables, and no finding names a Domain whose web App has no route back to the API. `FJS-3525` already proposes that each preflight finding carry its fix as data. Prior art: Kubernetes controllers move current state toward desired state on every pass rather than running setup once ([docs](https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/architecture/controller/)); Terraform's plan/apply diffs real state against config each run.
  - **A** — a reconcile: setup reads the App, the web App, the Domain and its routes, computes the writes that are MISSING, and applies each one idempotently (an upsert, or a read before a create). The gate becomes "anything missing" rather than "never released", and a Domain on the web App with no route to the API is a preflight finding whose fix is a route write, so preflight and setup share one plan (`releasePlan` in `release-preflight.js`; `neverReleased` goes, and nothing is kept beside it). A field that DIFFERS from the plan is a finding, never a write, so a hand edit on Basecamp is never overwritten. Cost: every Basecamp write setup makes needs an idempotent form (`/domains` and `/domain-routes` first, `FJS-3530`'s path), and the plan has to read the routes too.
  - **B** — keep the gate; on a failed write, print what was written, what was not, and the command that finishes it. Cost: a rerun still skips in silence, and the person finishes by hand.
  - **C** — one server call: Basecamp grows an endpoint that applies the whole first-release plan in one transaction. Cost: a new endpoint and a cli–server version pair to keep agreeing, and a Domain/route write that touches the proxy may not roll back cleanly.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — `FJS-3525` already makes each finding a fix as data, so a reconcile is that same plan applied, and a rerun becomes the repair for any half-done setup rather than a silent skip. The deploy already rules against reconciling (`packages/cli/core/deploy-plan.js` "Drift refuses and names itself; nothing reconciles", `commands/deploy/_module.md` "refuses rather than reconciles"). That ruling is about two intents that conflict. A reconcile that only creates what is missing, and refuses on a difference, never picks between intents, so it does not cross that line. Basecamp's rows stay the one record; no journal beside them. B is what A prints anyway when a write fails. C moves the problem to the server without making the proxy half transactional.

- ~~**FJS-D1600 — Does the generated `.dockerignore` name what the API image takes (ignore everything, then un-ignore what the Dockerfile copies) instead of listing what it leaves out?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-28 (`FJS-D1600`): A (confidence 3).** Found reviewing `FJS-3524`. The template in `packages/cli/commands/make/deploy.md` lists exclusions, and each leak found so far was one more line: a logger database's `*.jsonl` (production's audit trail, after a data move), SQLite copies not named `*.db`, `desktop/shell/target` at 2 GB, `widgets/` and `cli/`, and `node_modules` below the root. The Dockerfile the same command writes copies only `deploy/generated/`, `tsconfig*.json`, `api`, `db` and, when needed, `web/src`. Detection is built: `build-check.js` `inspectIgnored` warns on a context file git ignores. Prior art: Docker's `.dockerignore` takes `!` exceptions and the last matching line wins, which is what makes ignore-all-then-include a supported shape ([docs](https://docs.docker.com/build/concepts/context/#dockerignore-files)).
  - **A** — an allowlist derived from the COPY lines: `*`, then `!api/`, `!db/`, `!deploy/generated/`, `!tsconfig*.json` and `!web/src` when the API reads it. Below those come the excludes for inside the included directories: `**/node_modules`, the SQLite and sidecar globs, `db/backups/`, `**/*.jsonl` (rather than the logger paths read from `schema.lite`: same coverage, nothing to parse), and the test globs. One JS list feeds both the Dockerfile's COPY lines and the `!` lines. Cost: a COPY added to the Dockerfile by hand needs its `!` line too. Without one the build fails, loudly, naming the missing path.
  - **B** — a wider denylist: add `**/node_modules`, every surface directory the API image does not build (`desktop/`, `widgets/`, `cli/`, `extension/`, `mobile/`, `site/`), and the logger paths. Cost: each new surface or new kind of file leaks until someone adds its line, which is the history so far.
  - **C** — as it is, with build-check's warning. Cost: the template keeps writing the gap, and the warning is the only guard.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 3)** — the template already does this for one directory: `web/*` then `!web/src` (`make/deploy.md`, the `.dockerignore` step) is ignore-all-then-include, and A applies it to the root. One command writes both files, so the allowlist is derived from the same list the Dockerfile copies and cannot drift from it. A file left out now fails the build instead of quietly shipping in the image. `db/` still comes in whole, so a new kind of file inside it still leaks quietly, and the inner excludes stay in every option. The template skips an existing `.dockerignore`, so an app scaffolded before this keeps its list, and `inspectIgnored` warns only on what git ignores, so its `widgets/` or `cli/` stays unflagged. The reopen path is build-check warning on a `.dockerignore` with no `*` head line.

- **FJS-D1650 — How does Basecamp move an enrolled machine's Outpost pin to `OUTPOST_VERSION`?** Found fixing FJS-3533: the unit's `ExecStart` carries `@frontierjs/outpost@<version at install>` (`enrollment.ts` unit heredoc) and nothing rewrites it, so yuma ran 0.1.5 after 0.1.8 shipped. Basecamp already holds both numbers (`Server.outpostVersion` off the heartbeat, `OUTPOST_VERSION`) and the signed `/exec` channel with the certificate pinned (`FJS-D557`). The red test: after the action, a machine enrolled at an older pin has `ExecStart` naming `OUTPOST_VERSION`, its `outpostCert` and env file untouched. The restart ends the Outpost serving the call, so whichever option is chosen detaches it (`systemd-run`, as `docker.js` does for recipes). `/servers/` marking a machine behind `OUTPOST_VERSION` is the same in every option.
  - **A** — a Server action `upgradeOutpost` sends one `/exec` script: `sed` the pin in `/etc/systemd/system/outpost.service`, `daemon-reload`, detached `systemctl restart outpost`; a `fli basecamp:server:upgrade` command calls it. Works on every machine already enrolled, yuma included, with no install change. Cost: the script restates the unit's `ExecStart` line, so a change to that line in `enrollment.ts` must keep the `sed` matching (a test holds the two together).
  - **B** — the install writes `/usr/local/sbin/outpost-upgrade <version>` beside `outpost-uninstall`, and the action runs it over `/exec`. The edit lives next to the unit it edits and a person at a shell can run it. Cost: a machine enrolled before this has no script, so yuma still takes one hand edit, and every machine carries a second Basecamp-written file to keep honest.
  - **C** — unpin the unit (`@frontierjs/outpost@latest` or a dist-tag) so a restart upgrades. No action, no gap. Cost: two machines provisioned a week apart stop running the same thing (the pin exists for that, `enrollment.ts` header), an unpublished or broken release reaches every machine on its next restart, and `FJS-3534` widens from new enrollments to the whole fleet.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 3)** — it is the only option that reaches machines already enrolled, which is the row's own failing case, and the pin stays so a release still reaches a machine only when Basecamp says. B is A plus a file, and the file's only extra reader is a person at a shell, who has `sed`. The reopen path is a second place that edits the unit, which would make the script worth lifting into the install (B). Two conditions on building A: `enrollment.ts` exports the `ExecStart` line (or its matcher) so the heredoc and the script share one copy, and the action checks `outpostUnpublished()` first, since a bad pin leaves `Restart=always` looping on the only channel that could repair it; the script is idempotent and `grep`s the pin after the `sed`, exiting non-zero when it did not change.

- **FJS-D1651 — How does an agent (Ace, Charles) walk into a Haven that takes only a Frontier member's machine?** Found fixing FJS-3471: `POST /haven/login` with no `links` is a 401 (`login.ts` § `body.links === undefined`, `FJS-D1477`), and `agents.js` signs in with a key of its own under `~/.fli/haven/agents/<nick>/` through `machineKeys` + `signIn`, so `bun run agents` is refused at its first login and the live halves of `agents.test.js` are `describe.skip`. The red assertion: an agent's `signIn` against a Haven with `HAVEN_POST_KEY` set returns a token and a member whose nick is the agent's.
  - **A** — A Haven-listed agent key: `HAVEN_AGENTS=ace:<pubkey>,charles:<pubkey>` read by `loginRoute` beside `mods`, as Matrix appservice registration. A bare `{ publicKey }` login whose key is listed gets the listed nick and no chain; every other key stays refused. The agents keep their own keys and `machineKeys` stays for them. Cost: a second door in `login.ts` (one more way to make a member), and the operator pastes two public keys into the Haven's env.
  - **B** — Staked membership: each agent's key is a machine in a Frontier chain (`fli frontier:stake` once per agent, signed by the post). Login stays one shape and `login.ts` gains nothing. Cost: an agent is a Frontier member, which costs a stake per agent and mints a post row for something that is not a person.
  - **C** — In-process: `agents.js` runs inside the Haven API and writes through `db.asSystem()` and the room's own calls, with no login and no key. Cost: agents leave the process boundary they have now (one `claude` per soul, `ask-claude.js`), `machineKeys` goes with no remaining caller, and the socket path the agents are tested through stops being exercised.
  - **D** — A link in the owner's chain: the owner links each agent key into their own chain (`fli frontier:link`), so an agent logs in as a machine of its owner. Cost: the Haven sees one member with several machines, and the nick is the owner's, so Ace and Charles cannot be told apart in a room.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 3)** — it is the only option that keeps both the one-login-shape refusal for strangers (the list is the Haven's, not the caller's) and a distinct nick per agent without minting Frontier members; B is the cleaner door but pays a stake for a non-person, and C undoes the agents' process boundary.

- **FJS-D1652 — Is `FJS-3525`'s part (3) — the declared inputs arriving in the App's create body, so a first `--dry` has nothing to list — wanted, now that `fli basecamp:app` makes the App bare and `basecamp:deploy` completes it on every run?** Found fixing `FJS-3525`: parts (1) and (2) are built and applied on every run (`FJS-D1599`, `releasePlan` in `packages/cli/core/release-preflight.js`). `basecamp:app` ([app.md](packages/cli/commands/basecamp/app.md)) POSTs `/projects`, `/environments` and `/apps` and is bare on purpose. So a fresh App's first `releaseFindings` is the reconcile's to-do list (3 errors, 7 warnings), not a defect.
  - **A** — close (3) as delivered: bare create plus reconcile derives each setting once from `releasePlan`. Cost: a first `--dry` on a fresh App still lists the whole setup as findings.
  - **B** — `basecamp:app` imports `releasePlan` and sends the plan's `app` patch and Variables in its create body. Cost: `app.md` needs the Domain question and the secret minting `deploy.md` has, and the plan is applied in two commands.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — one owner per translation (Invariant 4): `releasePlan` plus `applyPlan` own the settings and `basecamp:app` owns the rows; B puts the plan's output in a second command to save one `--dry` listing.

- **FJS-D1659 — Outside row tenancy, who hears a gate-mode frame (the `FJS-D597` regrade, a bulk count): every reader of the model's gate, only readers of the written row's images, or only readers of a named workspace claim?** `gradeRecipients`' gate branch (`transport/channels.ts`) narrows by tenant only under `strategy row` with a claims resolver (`FJS-D1038`); in an app that separates workspaces by `check(org)` it admits on `readGateLevel` alone, so quo's dan (another org) hears `calls changed`, `callLegs changed` and four `agentTurns changed` as `{ operation: 'regrade', count: null }`. The red test asserts that a second-workspace socket receives no frame after a write to the first workspace's call (quo `ring.test.ts`, `agent.test.ts` § who hears).
  - **A** — admit a gate-mode recipient outside row tenancy only when `$readAs` reads the WRITTEN row's before- or after-image for them, so `gate` mode carries the row (or both images) like `row` mode does. Cost: a revoke whose Share the grantee cannot read never heals their screen, the stale-screen case `FJS-D597` turned down; the tap threads two images to the gate branch. The ruling must also say which model grades the image (a `D597` regrade counts a DEPENDENT model, so the written Share is graded under the SOURCE model's accessor, not the dependent's) and what a bulk write threads (`FJS-D1038` B was turned down for materializing many-row purges: one image per row, or a cap).
  - **B** — leave the gate branch as is and have the boot warning (`warnRefusedAll`'s sibling) name a policy-separated app, documenting that call cadence crosses workspaces. Cost: the leak stays and every such app owns it.
  - **C** — let the app name the workspace claim its `check(org)` reads (the claims resolver's key), and grade a gate frame the way `FJS-D1038` does under row tenancy, by the tenants the write touched. Cost: a new option, and Litestone must answer which workspaces a write touched for a policy-scoped model, which it answers today only through `@@tenant`.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the cadence of another workspace's calls is a leak and a stale revoke is a delay, but `FJS-D597` chose fail-closed over exactly this, so the ruling should say the heal case (a Share the grantee cannot read) is paid by the app announcing it.

- **FJS-D1662 — How does the App picker label tell apart two projects' same-named environments?** Found fixing FJS-3639: `App` declares `@@label([name, environment.name])`, one hop (`schema.lite` § model App); `project` is two hops away, so `worker · Production` repeats across projects. `FJS-D674` refuses a two-hop `@@label` at parse by name, and a `@generated` column cannot read another table.
  - **A** — widen `FJS-D674` to allow two to-one hops (`@@label([name, environment.project.name, environment.name])`): parser, `x-label-field`, and sierra `options()` populate/search follow the longer path. Cost: reopens a ruling made for the one-hop cases measured, and every consumer of the list (fts title, ⌘K, relation filter) gains a depth.
  - **B** — give `App` a `projectId` own column with a `project` relation, so `project.name` is one hop (`App` already carries `workspaceId` the same way, denormalized). Cost: a schema change on a frozen migration lock (`FJS-D1660`), and the column must stay equal to `environment.project` on every write.
  - **C** — give `Environment` an own `label` column holding `project · name`, kept by a hook, and label `App` by `[name, environment.label]`. Cost: a derived column written by a hook, which Invariant 6 prefers declared in the schema; renaming a project must rewrite every environment's copy.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the repeat is a label problem and A fixes it where labels live, with no second copy of the project to keep equal; B and C each add a denormalized column a rename can leave stale.

- **FJS-D1663 — How does a route file (`FJS-D626`) reach the app: a second argument, `ctx.app`, or an ambient import?** `autoloadRoutes` (`packages/junction/src/core/loader.ts`) registers `mod[v]` as the handler, so `export const GET = (ctx) => …` sees only the `TransportContext`, which has no `app` (`ServiceContext` does, `context.ts:211`). Basecamp's `GET /backup-archive/{id}` needs `app.db.asSystem()`, `app.sqlite.filename` and `app.logger`, so it stays a plugin and `app-layout` holds a ceiling of 1 (`FJS-3596`). Red test: a route file `export const GET = (ctx) => ({ hasLogger: !!ctx.app?.logger })`, or `(ctx, app) => …`, answers `hasLogger: false` today.
  - **A** — `autoloadRoutes` binds the app as a second argument: `register(url, ctx => mod[v](ctx, app))`, so `GET(ctx, app)`. Cost: a handler signature the plain `app.get` form lacks; the one place that knows both is the loader, so no transport changes.
  - **B** — `ctx.app` on `TransportContext`, parallel to `ServiceContext.app`. Cost: the HTTP and WS transports build contexts apart from the app (`http.ts` `_buildContext`, `bridge.internal()`), so each must be handed the app, `context-contract` and the testing parity runner gain a field, and every middleware and plugin handler sees it too.
  - **C** — an ambient `app` export from `@frontierjs/junction` set at boot, like `$`. Cost: a process-global that a test mounting two apps cannot hold, and a second owner of `app` beside `createApp`'s result (Invariant 5).
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the loader already holds the app and is the only code that registers route files, so it is the one-owner fix with no transport or contract-test surface; B is the wider design and waits until a non-route handler needs the app too.

- **FJS-D1664 — A stand-in a person runs beside the dev pair binds a port, and `test-port-literal` can only prescribe `portFor()`, which has no category for it: exempt it, give it a category, or only reword the message?** Found fixing FJS-2525: maidtech's `api/test/standins/crm.ts` (8794 under `import.meta.main`) and ELA's four stand-ins (8790–8793, `SA_URL` documented as `localhost:8790`) are tools a person starts by hand and types the URL of from memory. `portFor(root, 'tooling', { env: 'dev' })` answers 8500 for every scaffolded app (`tooling` is not in `SLOTTED`, [ports.js](packages/cli/core/ports.js)), `{ env: 'test' }` answers 7500–7503 for every one, and `'be'` is pinned to the app's slot. The red assertion: a file `api/test/standins/crm.ts` holding `listen(8794)` yields a `test-port-literal` finding today (`checks/app.js` `driveFiles(root, /^tests?$/)` takes every script under `test/`). Both apps carry it in `check-baseline.json` naming FJS-2525.
  - **A** — the rule skips a `standins/` directory under `test/`: a stand-in is a tool, its port is documented, and a branch checkout of it is not meant to move. Cost: a directory-name convention the rule reads, and a stand-in written elsewhere stays graded; two apps' stand-ins can still collide if both pick the same literal, which `fli ports` would not see.
  - **B** — give stand-ins a port category (a `SLOTTED` entry or a tenth CAT digit) so `portFor(…, 'standin', { service })` hands each app its own and a branch moves it. Cost: the CAT digits 0–8 are used and `ports.js` header says the schema is closed at nine, so this adds a tenth noun (`the-nine-stay-nine` pressure) and the URL stops being typeable from memory in a branch.
  - **C** — keep the rule and reword its message to name the existing per-file `allow` with a reason, the way both apps already use it. Cost: the finding still fires on a thing that is not a defect, and every app with a stand-in carries an allow row.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the rule exists so a *test* follows a branch's offset; a stand-in a person runs is not a test and must not move, and the directory is already where both apps put theirs.

- **FJS-D1665 — A memo chain past the engine's stack depth throws a bare `RangeError` that names no node: guard the depth, pull known deps iteratively, or document the ceiling?** `read` → `_recompute` → `read` (`packages/mesa/src/runtime.js` `createMemo`) recurses once per link. Probed under vitest on node 22: a 2000-link chain of `createMemo(() => p() + 1)` reads 2000; 5000 and 20000 throw `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`, on first read and after a write alike. The ceiling is the engine's, so node and bun differ (`FJS-907`). The red assertion: a 20000-link chain read either returns its value or throws an error naming a memo.
  - **A** — a depth counter in `_recompute`: past a fixed limit (say 1000) throw a Mesa error naming the memo and the limit, the same on every runtime. Cost: one module-level counter on the hottest path, and it turns a chain bun holds today into an error; a limit chosen by this package is a number to defend.
  - **B** — pull iteratively: on a dirty read, walk the memo's previous `_deps` that are dirty memos with an explicit stack and recompute leaves first, so the recursion never exceeds one link. Cost: fixes only chains that have computed once, since deps are unknown before `fn()` first runs, so a first read of 20000 links still overflows; a second pull path beside `read` (Invariant 4 wants one owner).
  - **C** — leave it and write the ceiling into `docs/hazards.md` as engine-bound. Cost: the overflow still names no node, and a shared app keeps a different limit per runtime.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — nothing anyone has written reaches the limit, so the cheapest honest fix is the same limit on both runtimes with a message that names the node; B leaves the first-read case open and adds a second pull path.

- **FJS-D1666 — How does a server render stop waiting on an `{#await}` whose promise never settles: a timeout option, an abort signal, or nothing?** `_render`/`_renderString` (`packages/mesa/src/render.js`) `await settleAwaits()` / `settle(out)` with no bound, so `renderToHTML(Comp)` over `{#await new Promise(() => {})}` never returns, and its `_queue` tail never resolves either, so every later render in the process hangs behind it, naming no file. The label a failure already carries is `options.label` (`renderFailure`). The red assertion: `renderToHTML(Hang, {}, { label: 'Hang.mesa', <bound> })` rejects with a message naming `Hang.mesa`; today it never settles.
  - **A** — `options.timeout` (ms) on `renderToHTML`: past it the render rejects through `renderFailure` as `await did not settle in Nms`, disposes the root, and releases the queue. No default, so a slow legitimate fetch is never cut. Cost: a new option on the public renderer; the number is the caller's to pick; a `setTimeout` raced against the wait in both paths.
  - **B** — `options.signal` (an `AbortSignal`) instead, React's shape: the caller owns the clock and can share one abort across a whole prerender (`AbortSignal.timeout(n)` gives A for free). Cost: the same plumbing as A, plus callers must build the signal, so the build's own prerender loop (`render-component.js` `executeComponent`) has to choose a number anyway.
  - **C** — a finite default bound (say 30 s) on `executeComponent` only, with `renderToHTML` left unbounded, Svelte's shape. Cost: no new public option, but an unbounded direct caller still hangs and the default is a number this package defends.
  - **Recommend B (confidence 2)** — `AbortSignal.timeout` spells A in one call, and a signal lets a build abort every in-flight page at once; the refusal names `options.label` so the hang finally has an address.

- **FJS-D1667 — How does a Basecamp blueprint param mint a valid ntfy sender token, when `ParamGenerator` is a hex-only enum and the token must name a user in a sibling param?** `paramValues` (`packages/basecamp/api/src/services/apps/apps.service.ts`) calls `mint(generator)` for one param, and `mint` accepts only `random_hex_N`; `NTFY_AUTH_TOKENS` needs `name:tk_<exactly 29 letters or digits>:label` with `name` a row of `NTFY_AUTH_USERS`, whose `name:hash:role` carries a bcrypt hash nothing here can mint. Red probe: no `ParamGenerator` value yields a string `firstSenderToken` (`api/src/push.ts`) accepts. Adding a value widens the enum and its `CHECK` in `20260801000000_initial_schema.sql`, which `FJS-D1660` freezes, so the change is a new appended migration, not an edit.
  - **A** — a generator `ntfy_token` that mints the whole `NTFY_AUTH_TOKENS` value with a fixed sender name (`frontier:tk_<29>:Frontier`). Cost: enum change plus an appended migration; the operator still types a matching `frontier:<hash>:user` into `NTFY_AUTH_USERS` by hand, so half the restart-loop is still possible, and the generator names a user the blueprint does not own.
  - **B** — a generator that mints only the `tk_<29>` secret into a new generated param `NTFY_SENDER_TOKEN`, and the ntfy container's `NTFY_AUTH_TOKENS` is composed from it at install. Cost: a derived-value concept (a param built from other params) the blueprint model has no word for, and `firstSenderToken` reads a different variable.
  - **C** — no generator; keep the hand-typed value and the hint and notes FJS-3595 added (`ntfy token generate` makes a valid one). Close FJS-3657 as not worth a schema change. Cost: the first install still needs a docker run before the form submits.
  - **D** — refuse a malformed or unprovisioned `NTFY_AUTH_TOKENS` at install, naming the field, instead of letting the container restart-loop (no enum change, no migration); file as its own row if taken.
  - **Recommend C** — a generator can only make half of a pair whose other half is a bcrypt hash it cannot make, so it moves the typing rather than removing it, and it costs an enum change and a migration after the freeze. `decision-rules` agrees; D is the cheap follow-up if first-install friction hurts.

- ~~**FJS-D1677 — Does `fli top` ship native source that fli builds on first use, and in which language?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-30 (`FJS-D1677`): C — both: Rust when cargo is on the PATH, C when it is not, and a refusal naming both when neither is. Cost: one monitor in two languages, changed together.** The owner asked for a vtop look-alike as an fli command (CPU and memory graphs, processes grouped by name, `dd` to kill). Interpreted, it cannot match a compiled `/proc` reader: vtop costs ~23% of a core and ~100 MB, both native builds ~1.3% and ~2 MB, and Bun idles at ~27 MB before drawing anything. So fli builds the monitor on first use into `~/.cache/frontierjs/top` and `execv`s it through `bun:ffi`, which leaves no Bun process behind. Doctrine fails Q1 (a second toolchain for one command), Q3 (a native build step the package has never had) and Q6 (a source a JS-only reader cannot review).
  - **A** — Rust only (`native/top/src/main.rs`, no crates, so it builds offline in ~5 s). Cost: refuses on a machine without cargo.
  - **B** — C only (`native/top/top.c`, `cc -O2`, ~0.4 s). Measured equal to A: 1.30% CPU / 2.1 MB vs 1.33% / 2.4 MB.
  - **C** — both: Rust when cargo is on the PATH, C when it is not, and a refusal naming both when neither is. Cost: one monitor in two languages, changed together.
  - **Recommend B** — one file and the compiler nearly every Linux box already has; A buys no speed.

- **FJS-D1675 — How do junction's README examples that grade a hostname run offline: an injectable resolver, a prelude that stubs DNS and fetch, or stay fragment?** `app.webhooks.register('https://partner.com/…')` (`README.md` § Registering a subscriber) reaches `gradePublicUrl` (`src/core/public-url.ts:155`), whose `resolve` is a third parameter no plugin option reaches and whose `lookup` is a named import of `node:dns/promises`, so a doc-runner prelude cannot swap it. The conduit blocks (`api.hetzner.cloud`, `outpost:srv-abc`) dial a real address. Red assertion: with the network down, `DOC_EXAMPLES_ONLY=README.md:1962` fails at grading with a lookup error, so the fence cannot drop `fragment`. The `FRAGMENT_CEILING` in `test/doc-examples.test.ts` falls by one per block completed.
  - **A** — `PublicUrlOptions.resolve`: the existing third parameter MOVES into the option (no second spelling; the three `webhook-targets.test.ts` call sites become `{ resolve }`), and the prelude passes a stub resolver. Cost: a new public option on the SSRF guard, shared by webhooks, conduit `destinations`, basecamp and webpush, that exists only for tests, next to `allowPrivate`; it is never fed from a caller-supplied value. It completes the `register` block alone, so the ceiling falls by one, not three: the conduit blocks still need the stub transport README already documents.
  - **B** — prelude only: preload that replaces `globalThis.fetch` and `Bun.dns`/`node:dns` before the block, no source change. Cost: `lookup` binds at import, so this needs a bun plugin rewriting the import in the run's tmpdir; the block then proves a stub, not the guard.
  - **C** — leave the 3 network blocks `fragment` and record why next to the ceiling; complete only the surface ones that can run. Cost: three examples the suite never runs, which Invariant 16 names a bug.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the third parameter already exists for exactly this, `allowPrivate` sets the precedent of a policy key a test turns on and says so, and B proves a stub where A proves the guard's real code path.
